For the improved bound for covering one function, we exploited the correlated choice of the hyperedges chosen in two consecutive recursive calls to show quantifiable progress within two recursive calls.

1.4
Related Work

In network design problems, the goal is to construct a graph that has certain pre-specified properties. This is a broad family of problems encompassing polynomial-time solvable problems like minimum cost spanning tree and shortest
(s,t)
-path as well as NP-hard problems like Steiner Tree and Tree Augmentation.
The polynomial-time solvable network design problems have been studied from the perspective of fast algorithms while the NP-hard network design problems have been studied from the perspective of approximation algorithms leading to a rich toolkit of techniques on both fronts (e.g., see
[
30
,
48
,
54
,
62
,
55
,
56
]
). In this section, we mention related works on
hypergraph
network design problems.

Hypergraph cut/coverage/spectral sparsifiers can be viewed as special cases of hypergraph network design problems. Here, the input is a hypergraph and the goal is to construct a sub-hypergraph (i.e., the hyperedges in the constructed hypergraph form a subset of the hyperedges of the input hypergraph and their weights are allowed to be different from the input hypergraph) with few hyperedges in order to approximately preserve cut and/or coverage values of all subsets of vertices and/or spectral values of all vectors on
V
coordinates where
V
is the vertex set of the input hypergraph.
Recent works have shown the existence of cut/coverage/spectral sparsifiers where the number of hyperedges is near-linear in the number of vertices and that they can be constructed in time that is near-linear in the size of the input hypergraph
[
43
,
19
,
57
,
2
,
20
,
41
,
40
,
45
,
39
,
52
]
. These results do not have direct implications for the problems that we study in our work since we focus on degree-specified network design problems. Even if we relax the degree-specification requirement, these results only imply the
existence
of hypergraphs with near-linear number of hyperedges that satisfy certain kinds of cut/coverage requirements
approximately
. In contrast, our work focuses on efficient algorithmic construction of small-sized hypergraphs that satisfy cut/coverage requirements exactly.

The rank of a hypergraph is the size of its largest hyperedge.
Degree-specified hypergraph local connectivity augmentation using
rank-
t
hyperedges of minimum total weight problem is a variant of
DS-Hypergraph-CA-using-H
where the returned hypergraph is additionally required to have rank at most
t
(where
t
is an input integer).
Thus,
DS-Hypergraph-CA-using-E
is a special case of degree-specified hypergraph local connectivity augmentation using rank-
2
hyperedges. Although
DS-Graph-CA-using-E
is polynomial-time solvable
[
65
,
12
,
29
,
28
,
32
,
10
,
49
,
5
,
6
,
44
]
,
DS-Hypergraph-CA-using-E
is NP-hard (i.e., hypergraph local connectivity augmentation using edges of minimum total weight is NP-hard)
[
22
]
.
In a surprising twist, independent works by Cosh
[
21
]
, Nutov
[
50
]
, and Bernáth and Király
[
9
]
have shown that feasible instances of
DS-Hypergraph-CA-using-H
admit a solution with at most one hyperedge of size at least
3
while all other hyperedges are in fact size-
2
edges.
Their results are also applicable to
DS-Sym-Skew-SupMod-StrongCover-using-H
. We note that their proof technique does not generalize to obtain near-uniform hyperedges or to address simultaneous cover of two functions.

2
Preliminaries

Projection of Laminar Families.

We recall that two sets
X,Y\subseteq V
are said to cross if the sets
X-Y,Y-X,X\cap Y
are non-empty. A
family
{\mathcal{L}}\subseteq 2^{V}
is
laminar
if no two sets in
{\mathcal{L}}
cross. We will be interested in the
projection
of a laminar family, i.e., the restriction of a laminar family
{\mathcal{L}}\subseteq 2^{V}
to a ground set
V-{\mathcal{Z}}
for some
{\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq V
. The next lemma from
[
7
]
says that the size of the projection is comparable to that of the original family
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Lemma 2.1

(Lemma 2.1 of
[
7
]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}\subseteq 2^{V}
be a laminar family and
{\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq V
be a subset of elements. Let
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{X-{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\in{\mathcal{L}}\}-\{\emptyset\}
. Then, the family
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
is a laminar family and
|{\mathcal{L}}|\leq|{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}|+3|{\mathcal{Z}}|
.

Maximization Oracles.

We recall that in our algorithmic problems, we have access to the input set function
p
via
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. We describe two additional oracles that will simplify our proofs. We will show that both oracles defined below can be implemented in strongly polynomial time using
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.

Definition 2.1

(
p
-maximization oracles)
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a set function.
We define two oracles for the set function
p
. Both oracles take as input disjoint sets
S_{0},T_{0}\subseteq V
, and a vector
{y_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
.

1.

{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}\left(\left({G_{0}},{w_{0}}\right),S_{0},T_{0},{y_{0}}\right)
additionally takes a hypergraph
(G_{0}=(V,E_{0}),c_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
as input and returns a tuple
(Z,p(Z))
, where
Z
is an optimum solution to the following problem:

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\left\{p(Z)-b_{(G_{0},c_{0})}(Z)+y_{0}(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S_{0}\subseteq Z\subseteq V-T_{0}\right\}.

(
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
)

2.

{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S_{0},T_{0},y_{0})
returns a tuple
(Z,p(Z))
, where
Z
is an optimum solution to the following problem:

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\left\{p(Z)+y_{0}(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S_{0}\subseteq Z\subseteq V-T_{0}\right\}.

(
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
)

The evaluation oracle for a function
p
can be implemented using one query to

{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
;
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
can be implemented using one query to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
where the input hypergraph is the empty hypergraph. The next lemma from
[
7
]
shows that
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
can be implemented using at most
|V|+1
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
where the hypergraphs used as input to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
queries have size of the order of the size of the hypergraph input to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. We note that the
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
as defined in
[
7
]
does not include the vector
y_{0}
as an input parameter. The proof of the next lemma follows by modifying the proof of the lemma from
[
7
]
to incorporate the parameter
y_{0}
. We omit details here for brevity.

Lemma 2.2

(Lemma 2.2 of
[
7
]
)
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a set function,
(G=(V,E),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
be a hypergraph, and
S,T\subseteq V
be disjoint sets. Then,
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}((G,w),S,T)
can be implemented to run in
O(|V|(|V|+|E|))
time using at most
|V|+1
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.
The run-time includes the time to construct the hypergraphs used as input to the queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Moreover, each query to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
is on an input hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
that has at most
|V|
vertices and
|E|
hyperedges.

3
Minimal Maximizers of Skew-Supermodular Function Sequences

In this section, we consider set families of maximizing sets of
skew-supermodular
functions. We state certain structural properties of these families which we leverage in subsequent sections to show rapid convergence of our algorithms.

For a function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, a set
X\subseteq V
is a
p
-maximizer
if
p(X)=K_{p}
. We let
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
denote the family of
minimal

p
-maximizers. The following lemma says that the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is disjoint if the function
p
is skew-supermodular. An important consequence of this lemma is that a transversal for the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
has polynomial (in particular, at most linear) size, a fact that we will use in our analyzing the runtime of one of our algorithms in subsequent sections.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a skew-supermodular function. Then, the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is a disjoint set-family.

The structural property of
Lemma

3.1
is in fact a special case of a more general structural property of certain sequences of skew-supermodular functions. Let
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{\ell}
be a sequence of skew-supermodular functions defined over a ground set
V
. We will be interested in the structure of the family of minimal maximizers across the entire sequence of functions. In particular, we will focus on the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}
, where the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq i}}
for
i\in[\ell]
is defined as follows:

{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq i}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{j\in[i]}{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{i}}

We note that for a general sequence of skew-supermodular functions, the sets of the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}
may not be well-structured. Consequently,
Lemma

3.1
applied to each function in the sequence implies that
|{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}|\leq O(\ell|V|)
.
However, we will focus on a specific structured sequence of skew-supermodular functions that will arise in the analyses of all our algorithms in later sections. The next lemma shows that for such a sequence, the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}
is
laminar
(which in turn, will be helpful to conclude later that
\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}\right|=O(|V|)
).

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be a positive integer. Let the sequence
V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{\ell}
be such that
V_{i+1}\subseteq V_{i}

and
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\coloneqq V_{i}-V_{i+1}
for
i\in[\ell-1]
. Let
g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{\ell}
be a sequence of non-negative monotone submodular functions such that
g_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
for each
i\in[\ell]
. Let
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{\ell}
be a sequence of functions such that
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
for
i\in[\ell]
, the function
p_{1}
is skew-supermodular, and
p_{i+1}\coloneqq{(p_{i}-g_{i})}/_{\mathcal{Z}_{i}}
for each
i\in[\ell-1]
. Then, the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}
is a laminar family over the ground set
V_{1}
.

We prove
Lemma

3.2
in
Section

B.1
. For completeness, we also include a simplified proof of the special case
Lemma

3.1
in the same section.

4
Weak Cover with Linear Number of Hyperedges

In this section we prove Theorem
1.7
. We restate it below.

See
1.7

We first describe our proof of
Theorem

1.7
under the assumption that the input function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a
positive
function.
In
Section

4.1
, we present our algorithm (see
Algorithm

1
).
In
Section

4.2
, we show that our algorithm terminates within a finite (pseudo-polynomial) number of recursive calls and returns a hypergraph satisfying properties (1), (2) and (3) of
Theorem

1.7
(see
Lemma

4.4
). In
Section

4.3
, we give a tighter bound on the number of recursive calls witnessed by our algorithm and show that the hypergraph returned by algorithm also satisfies property (4) of
Theorem

1.7
(see
Lemma

4.9
). Finally, in
Section

4.4
, we show that our algorithm runs in strongly polynomial time, given the appropriate function evaluation oracle (see
Lemma

4.11
).
Lemmas

4.4
,
4.9
, and
4.11
together complete the proof of
Theorem

1.7
under the assumption that the input function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a
positive
function.

We now briefly remark on how to circumvent the positivity assumption on the input function
m
in the above proof. We note that if
\mathcal{Z}\coloneqq\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m(u)=0\}\not=\emptyset
, then
{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V-\mathcal{Z}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
{m}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V-\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a
positive
function satisfying the two hypothesis conditions of
Theorem

1.7
, i.e.
{m}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}(X)\geq{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
and
{m}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}(u)\leq K_{{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}}
for every
u\in V-\mathcal{Z}
. Furthermore, we observe that a hypergraph satisfying properties (1)-(4) for the functions
{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{m}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}
also satisfies the four properties for the functions
p
and
m
. Finally,
{{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
can be implemented using
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
in strongly polynomial time.

4.1
The Algorithm

Our algorithm takes as input (1) a skew-supermodular function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, and (2) a
positive
function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and returns a hypergraph
\left(H=\left(V,E\right),w\right)
. We note that in contrast to the non-negative function
m
appearing in the statement of
Theorem

1.7
, the function
m
that is input to our algorithm should be positive.

We now give an informal description of the algorithm (refer to
Algorithm

1
for a formal description). Our algorithm is recursive.
If
V=\emptyset
, then the algorithm is in its base case and returns the empty hypergraph. Otherwise, the algorithm is in its recursive case. First, the algorithm computes an arbitrary minimal transversal
T\subseteq V
for the family of
p
-maximizers – i.e., an inclusionwise minimal set
T\subseteq V
such that
T\cap X\neq\emptyset
for every
X\subseteq V
with
p(X)=K_{p}
. Next, the algorithm computes the set
{\mathcal{D}}\coloneqq\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m(u)=K_{p}\}
and defines the set
A\coloneqq T\cup{\mathcal{D}}
.
Next, the algorithm uses the set
A
to compute the following three intermediate quantities
\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)}
:

•

\alpha^{(1)}\coloneqq\min\{m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in A\}
,

•

\alpha^{(2)}\coloneqq\min\left\{K_{p}-p(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\subseteq V-A\right\}
, and

•

\alpha^{(3)}\coloneqq\min\left\{K_{p}-m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in V-A\right\}
,

and defines
\alpha\coloneqq\min\left\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)}\right\}
.
Next, the algorithm computes the set
\mathcal{Z}\coloneqq\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m(u)-\alpha=0\}
and defines
(H_{0},w_{0})
to be the hypergraph on vertex set
V
consisting of a single hyperedge
A
with weight
w_{0}(A)=\alpha
. Next, the algorithm defines the two functions
m^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
m^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V-\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
as
m^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=m-\alpha\chi_{A}
, and
m^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}={m^{\prime}}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}
. Next, the algorithm defines the two functions
p^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
p^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V-\mathcal{Z}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
as

p^{\prime}\coloneqq p-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}
and
p^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq{p^{\prime}}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
. The algorithm then recursively calls itself with the input tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
to obtain a hypergraph
\left(H^{\prime\prime}=\left(V^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq V-\mathcal{Z},E^{\prime\prime}\right),w^{\prime\prime}\right)
. Finally, the algorithm extends the hypergraph
H^{\prime\prime}
with the set
\mathcal{Z}
of vertices, adds the set
A
with weight
\alpha
as a new hyperedge to the hyperedge set
E^{\prime\prime}
, and returns the resulting hypergraph. If the hyperedge
A
already has non-zero weight in
(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})
, then the algorithm increases the weight of the hyperedge
A
by
\alpha
, and returns the resulting hypergraph.

Algorithm 1

Weak covering with hyperedges

Input:
Skew-supermodular function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and positive function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}

Output:
Hypergraph
(H=(V,E),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})

\mathbf{\textsc{Algorithm}}\left(p,m\right)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}

1:

if

V=\emptyset

then

return
Empty Hypergraph
\left(\left(\emptyset,\emptyset\right),\emptyset\right)
.

2:

else
:

3:

{\mathcal{D}}\coloneqq\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m(u)=K_{p}\}

4:

T\coloneqq
an arbitrary minimal transversal for the family of
p
-maximizers

5:

A\coloneqq T\cup{\mathcal{D}}

6:

\alpha\coloneqq\min\begin{cases}\alpha^{(1)}\coloneqq\min\left\{m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in A\right\}\\
\alpha^{(2)}\coloneqq\min\left\{K_{p}-p(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\subseteq V-A\right\}\\
\alpha^{(3)}\coloneqq\min\left\{K_{p}-m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in V-A\right\}\end{cases}

7:

\mathcal{Z}\coloneqq\left\{u\in A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m(u)-\alpha=0\right\}

8:

Construct
\left(H_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left(V,E_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left\{A\right\}\right),w_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{0}\rightarrow\left\{\alpha\right\}\right)

9:

m^{\prime}\coloneqq m-\alpha\chi_{A}
and
m^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq{m^{\prime}}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}

10:

p^{\prime}\coloneqq p-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}
and
p^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq{p^{\prime}}/_{\mathcal{Z}}

11:

(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})\coloneqq\textsc{Algorithm}\left(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}\right)

12:

Obtain hypergraph
(G,c)
from
(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})
by adding vertices
\mathcal{Z}
.

13:

return

(G+H_{0},c+w_{0})

4.2
Termination and Partial Correctness

In this section, we show that
Algorithm

1
terminates within a finite (pseudo-polynomial) number of recursive calls, and moreover, returns a hypergraph satisfying properties (1)-(3) of
Theorem

1.7
. The ideas in this section are modifications of the results due to Szigeti
[
58
]
; we suggest first time readers to skip this section and return to it as needed.

Lemma

4.4
is the main result of this section and is included at the end of the section. The next lemma states useful properties of
Algorithm

1
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose that
V\not=\emptyset
and the input to
Algorithm

1
is a tuple
(p,m)
, where
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
. Let
\alpha,\mathcal{Z},m^{\prime\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

1
. Then, we have that

(a)

\alpha\geq 1
,

(b)

m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})<m(V)
,

(c)

m(X)\geq p(X)+\alpha|A\cap X|-\alpha
for every
X\subseteq V
.

Proof.

We note that
K_{p}\geq m(u)\geq 1
for every
u\in V
, where the final inequality is because
m
is a positive function, and consequently
K_{p}>0
since
V\not=\emptyset
. We now prove each property separately below.

(a)

We note that

\alpha^{(1)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
since the function
m
is a positive integer valued function. Thus, it suffices to show that
\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Our strategy to show this will be to assume otherwise and arrive at a contradiction to the algorithm’s choice of the set
A
.

First, we recall that
\alpha^{(2)}=\min\{K_{p}-p(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\subseteq V-A\}
. We note that
\alpha^{(2)}\in\mathbb{Z}
since
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, it suffices to show that
\alpha^{(2)}\geq 1
. By way of contradiction, say
\alpha^{(2)}\leq 0
. Since
K_{p}\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
by definition, we have that
\alpha^{(2)}\geq 0
. Thus,
\alpha^{(2)}=0
. Let
X\subseteq V
be a set that witnesses
\alpha^{(2)}=0
, i.e.
\alpha^{(2)}=K_{p}-p(X)
and
X\subseteq V-A
. We note that
X\not=\emptyset
, since otherwise we have that
0=\alpha^{(2)}=K_{p}-p(\emptyset)\geq K_{p}-m(\emptyset)=K_{p}>0
, a contradiction. Since
\alpha^{(2)}=0
, we have that
p(X)=K_{p}
and thus, the set
X
is a
p
-maximizer that is disjoint from the set
A
, contradicting that
A
contains a transversal for the family of
p
-maximizers by Steps
4
and
5
.

Next, we recall that
\alpha^{(3)}=\min\{K_{p}-m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in V-A\}
. We note that
\alpha^{(3)}\in\mathbb{Z}
since
K_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, it suffices to show that
\alpha^{(3)}\geq 1
. By way of contradiction, say
\alpha^{(3)}\leq 0
. Since
K_{p}\geq m(u)
for all
u\in V
, we have that
\alpha^{(3)}\geq 0
. Thus,
\alpha^{(3)}=0
. Consequently, there exists a vertex
v\in V
that witnesses
\alpha^{(3)}=0
, i.e.
\alpha^{(3)}=0=K_{p}-m(v)
and
v\in V-A
, contradicting Steps
3
and
5
.

(b)

Let
m^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{Z}
be as defined by
Algorithm

1
. We note that by Steps
4
and
5
, the set
A
contains a transversal for the family of
p
-maximizers. If
\emptyset
is the only
p
-maximizer, then we have that
0<K_{p}=p(\emptyset)\leq m(\emptyset)=0
, a contradiction. Thus, there exists a non-empty
p
-maximizer and consequently
A\not=\emptyset
. Moreover, by part (a) of the current lemma, we have that
\alpha\geq 1
. Then, the following shows the claim.

m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})={m^{\prime}}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}(V-\mathcal{Z})=m^{\prime}(V)=m(V)-\alpha\chi_{A}<m(V).

(c)

Let
X\subseteq V
be arbitrary. We will show the claim by induction on
|X|
. We first consider the base case
|X|=0
. Then, we have that
m(X)\geq p(X)>p(X)-\alpha=p(X)+\alpha|A\cap X|-\alpha
, and so the claim holds.

Next, we consider the inductive case of
|X|\geq 1
. Let
T
and
{\mathcal{D}}
be as defined by
Algorithm

1
for input tuple
(p,m)
. Let
D\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}={\mathcal{D}}-T
so that
A=T\uplus D
. We now prove the claim by considering two cases. First, suppose that
D\cap X\not=\emptyset
. Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle m(X)

\displaystyle\geq m(A\cap X)

\displaystyle=m(T\cap X)+m(D\cap X)

\displaystyle\geq\alpha|T\cap X|+K_{p}\cdot|D\cap X|

\displaystyle\geq K_{p}+\alpha|T\cap X|+\alpha(|D\cap X|-1)

\displaystyle\geq p(X)+\alpha|A\cap X|-\alpha.

Here, the second inequality is because
m(u)\geq\alpha^{(1)}\geq\alpha
for each
u\in A
by Step
6
and
m(u)=K_{p}
for each
u\in D
. The third inequality is because
K_{p}\geq m(u)\geq\alpha^{(1)}\geq\alpha
for each
u\in A
by Step
6
. The final inequality is because
K_{p}\geq p(X)
and
A=T\uplus D
.

Next, suppose that
D\cap X=\emptyset
. Suppose that
T\cap X=\emptyset
. Then, we have that
A\cap X=\emptyset
since
A=T\uplus D
. Consequently, we have that

m(X)\geq p(X)>p(X)-\alpha=p(X)+\alpha|A\cap X|-\alpha,
and hence, the claim holds. Here, the first inequality is because
m(Z)\geq p(Z)
for every
Z\subseteq V
.
We henceforth assume that
T\cap X\not=\emptyset
. Let
y\in T\cap X
be an arbitrary element. Since the set
T
is a minimal transversal of
p
-maximizers, there exists a
p
-maximizer
Y\subseteq V
such that
T\cap Y=\{y\}
. In particular, we have that
|A\cap(X-Y)|=|A\cap X|-1
.
We now consider two cases based on the skew-supermodularity behavior of the function
p
at the sets
X
and
Y
.

First, suppose that
p(X)+p(Y)\leq p(X\cap Y)+p(X\cup Y)
. Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle p(X)+p(Y)

\displaystyle\leq p(X\cap Y)+p(X\cup Y)

\displaystyle\leq p(X\cap Y)+p(Y)

\displaystyle\leq m(X\cap Y)+p(Y)

\displaystyle=m(X)-m(X-Y)+p(Y)

\displaystyle\leq m(X)-m(A\cap(X-Y))+p(Y)

\displaystyle\leq m(X)-\alpha|A\cap(X-Y)|+p(Y)

\displaystyle=m(X)-\alpha(|A\cap X|-1)+p(Y).

Here, the second inequality is because the set
Y
is a
p
-maximizer. The third inequality is because
m(Z)\geq p(Z)
for every
Z\subseteq V
. The fourth inequality is because
m
is a positive function.
The fifth inequality is because
m(u)\geq\alpha^{(1)}\geq\alpha
for each
u\in A
by Step
6
. The final equality is because
|A\cap(X-Y)|=|A\cap X|-1
. We note that rearranging the terms gives us the claim.

Next, suppose that
p(X)+p(Y)\leq p(X-Y)+p(Y-X)
. Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle p(X)+p(Y)

\displaystyle\leq p(X-Y)+p(Y-X)

\displaystyle\leq p(X-Y)+p(Y)

\displaystyle\leq m(X-Y)-\alpha|A\cap(X-Y)|+\alpha+p(Y)

\displaystyle=m(X)-m(X\cap Y)-\alpha|A\cap(X-Y)|+\alpha+p(Y)

\displaystyle=m(X)-m(X\cap Y)-\alpha|A\cap X|+\alpha|A\cap X\cap Y|+\alpha+p(Y)

\displaystyle\leq m(X)-\alpha|A\cap X|+\alpha+p(Y).

Here, the second inequality is because the set
Y
is a
p
-maximizer. The third inequality is by the inductive hypothesis for the set
X-Y
: we note that
|X-Y|<|X|
since
y\in X\cap Y\not=\emptyset
. The final equality is because
|A\cap(X-Y)|=|A\cap X|-|A\cap X\cap Y|
. The final inequality is because
\alpha|A\cap X\cap Y|\leq m(A\cap X\cap Y)\leq m(X\cap Y)
since
m(u)\geq\alpha^{(1)}\geq\alpha
for each
u\in A
by Step
6
.

∎

The next two lemmas will allow us to conclude that if the input functions
p
and
m
to a recursive call of
Algorithm

1
satisfy conditions
Theorem

1.7
(a) and (b), then the intermediate functions
p^{\prime},m^{\prime}
and the input functions to the subsequent recursive call
p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}
as constructed by
Algorithm

1
also satisfy conditions
Theorem

1.7
(a) and (b). Such recursive properties will be useful in inductively proving the existential version of
Theorem

1.7
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose that
V\not=\emptyset
and the input to
Algorithm

1
is a tuple
(p,m)
, where
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
. Let
\alpha,p^{\prime},m^{\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

1
. Then, we have that

(a)

The function
m^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is a non-negative function,

(b)

K_{p^{\prime}}\coloneqq K_{p}-\alpha
,

(c)

m^{\prime}(u)\leq K_{p^{\prime}}
for all
u\in V^{\prime}
, and

(d)

m^{\prime}(X)\geq p^{\prime}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove each property separately below.

(a)

Let
u\in V
be a vertex. If
u\not\in A
, then
m^{\prime}(u)=m(u)\geq 0
. Otherwise
u\in A
and we have that
m^{\prime}(u)=m(u)-\alpha\geq m(u)-\alpha^{(1)}\geq 0
, where the last inequality is by definition of
\alpha^{(1)}
.

(b)

Let
X\subseteq V
be a
p
-maximizer and
Y\subseteq V
be a
p^{\prime}
-maximizer.
By Steps
4
and
5
, the set
A
is a transversal for the family of
p
-maximizers, and consequently,
|A\cap X|\geq 1
. Thus,
K_{p^{\prime}}=p^{\prime}(Y)\geq p^{\prime}(X)=p(X)-\alpha=K_{p}-\alpha
. Here, the inequality is because
Y
is a
p^{\prime}
-maximizer, and the equality
p^{\prime}(X)=p(X)-\alpha
is by the definition of the function
p^{\prime}
and because
|A\cap X|\geq 1
. We now show that
K_{p^{\prime}}\leq K_{p}-\alpha
. If the set
Y
is also a
p
-maximizer, then the claim holds since we have that
K_{p^{\prime}}=p^{\prime}(Y)=p(Y)-\alpha=K_{p}-\alpha
, where the second equality is because the set
A
is a transversal for the family of
p
-maximizers. Thus, we may assume that
Y
is not a
p
-maximizer. If
|A\cap Y|\geq 1
, then we have that

K_{p}-\alpha=p(X)-\alpha=p^{\prime}(X)\leq K_{p}^{\prime}=p^{\prime}(Y)=p(Y)-\alpha\leq p(X)-\alpha,

where the third equality is because
Y
is a
p^{\prime}
-maximizer, the fourth equality is because of
|A\cap Y|\geq 1
and the definition of
p^{\prime}
, and the final inequality is because the set
X
is a
p
-maximizer. Thus, all inequalities are equations and the claim holds. Otherwise, suppose that
|A\cap Y|=0
. Here, we have that

K_{p}-\alpha=p(X)-\alpha\geq p(X)-\alpha^{(2)}\geq p(X)-\left(p(X)-p(Y)\right)=p(Y)=p^{\prime}(Y)=K_{p^{\prime}},

where the first inequality is by definition of
\alpha
, the second inequality is by definition of
\alpha^{(2)}
and the fact that the set
X
is a
p
-maximizer, and the third equality is because of
|A\cap Y|=0
and definition of the function
p^{\prime}
.

(c)

First, consider a vertex
u\in A
. Then, we have that
m^{\prime}(u)=m(u)-\alpha\leq K_{p}-\alpha=K_{p^{\prime}}
, where the inequality is because
m(v)\leq K_{p}
for every
v\in V
, and the final equality is by part (b) of the current lemma. Next, consider a vertex
u\in V-A
. Thus,
m^{\prime}(u)=m(u)
. In particular, we have that
K_{p}-m^{\prime}(u)=K_{p}-m(u)\geq\alpha^{(3)}\geq\alpha
. Rearranging the terms, we get that
K_{p^{\prime}}=K_{p}-\alpha\geq m^{\prime}(u)
, where the first equality is again by part (b) of the current lemma.

(d)

Let
X\subseteq V
be arbitrary and let the hypergraph
(H_{0},w_{0})
be as defined in Step
8
. First, suppose that
|X\cap A|=0
. Then, we have that
b_{(H_{0},w_{0}))}(X)=0
, and consequently
m^{\prime}(X)=m(X)\geq p(X)=p^{\prime}(X)
. Thus, the claim holds. Next, suppose that
|X\cap A|\geq 1
. Then, we have the following:

m^{\prime}(X)=m(X)-\alpha|X\cap A|\geq p(X)-\alpha=p(Z)-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(X)=p^{\prime}(X).

Here, the inequality holds by
Lemma

4.1
(c). The final equality is because
|X\cap A|\geq 1
.

∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose that
V\not=\emptyset
and the input to
Algorithm

1
is a tuple
(p,m)
, where
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
. Let
\alpha,\mathcal{Z},p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

1
. Then, we have that

(a)

p^{\prime\prime}={(p-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})})}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
; moreover, the function
p^{\prime\prime}
is skew-supermodular,

(b)

the function
m^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V-\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function,

(c)

K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=K_{p}-\alpha
,

(d)

m^{\prime\prime}(u)\leq K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
for all
u\in V^{\prime\prime}
, and

(e)

m^{\prime\prime}(X)\geq p^{\prime\prime}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove each property separately below.

(a)

We note that
p^{\prime\prime}={(p-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})})}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
by Step
10
. Moreover, the function
p^{\prime\prime}
is skew-supermodular because
b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}
is submodular and function contraction is known to preserve skew-supermodularity (see Proposition 4 of
[
58
]
).

(b)

We have that
m^{\prime\prime}(u)=m^{\prime}(u)>0
for each
u\in V-\mathcal{Z}
, where the inequality is by
Lemma

4.2
(a) and the definition of the set
\mathcal{Z}
in Step
7
.

(c)

By
Lemma

4.2
(b), it suffices to show that
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=K_{p^{\prime}}
. Let
X\uplus R_{X}
be a
p^{\prime}
-maximizer, where
R_{X}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
and
X\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
. Furthermore, let
Y\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
be a
p^{\prime\prime}
-maximizer such that
p^{\prime\prime}(Y)=p^{\prime}(Y\uplus R_{Y})
where
R_{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
. Then, we have the following:

K_{p^{\prime}}=p^{\prime}(X\uplus R_{X})\leq\max\left\{p^{\prime}(X\uplus R^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}\right\}=p^{\prime\prime}(X)\leq K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=p^{\prime\prime}(Y)=p^{\prime}(Y\uplus R_{Y})\leq K_{p^{\prime}}.

Thus, all inequalities are equations.

(d)

Let
u\in V-\mathcal{Z}
. Then we have that
m^{\prime\prime}(u)=m^{\prime}(u)\leq K_{p^{\prime}}=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
, where the inequality is by
Lemma

4.2
(a) and the final equality is by
Lemma

4.2
(b) and part (c) of the current lemma.

(e)

Let
X\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
be arbitrary. Let
R\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
such that
p^{\prime\prime}(X)=p^{\prime}(X\uplus R)
. Then, we have that
m^{\prime\prime}(X)=m^{\prime}(X\uplus R)\geq p^{\prime}(X\uplus R)=p^{\prime\prime}(X)
. Here, the first equality is because
R\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
and the inequality is by
Lemma

4.2
(a).

∎

We conclude the section with the main result of the section.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

1
is a tuple
(p,m)
, where
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
.
Then,
Algorithm

1
terminates within a finite (pseudo-polynomial) number of recursive calls. Furthermore, the hypergraph
\left(H=\left(V,E\right),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)
returned by
Algorithm

1

satisfies the following three properties:

1.

b_{(H,w)}(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
,

2.

b_{(H,w)}(u)=m(u)
for all
u\in V
, and

3.

\sum_{e\in E}w(e)=K_{p}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction on the potential function
\phi(m)\coloneqq m(V)
. We note that
\phi(m)\geq 0
since
m
is a positive function. For the base case of induction, suppose that
\phi(m)=0
. Then, we have that
V=\emptyset
since
m
is a positive function. Consequently,
Algorithm

1
is in its base case (Step
1
) and terminates. Moreover, Step
1
returns an empty hypergraph which satisfies properties (1)-(3) and so the lemma holds.

For the inductive case, suppose that
\phi(m)>0
. Since
m
is a positive function, we have that
V\not=\emptyset
and so
Algorithm

2
is in its recursive case. Thus, by
Lemma

4.3
(a), (b), (d) and (e), the tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
constructed by
Algorithm

1
satisfy the hypothesis of the current lemma. We note that the tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
is the input to the subsequent recursive call of
Algorithm

1
by Step
11
. Moreover,
\phi(m^{\prime\prime})<\phi(m)
by
Lemma

4.1
(b). Thus, by induction, the subsequent recursive call to
Algorithm

1
terminates and returns a hypergraph
(H^{\prime\prime}=(V^{\prime\prime},E^{\prime\prime}),w^{\prime\prime})
satisfying properties (1)-(3) for the tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. Consequently, the entire execution of
Algorithm

1
terminates within a finite number of recursive calls. Let
(H=(V,E),w)
be the hypergraph returned by Step
11
and let
(H_{0},w_{0}),\mathcal{Z},A,\alpha
be as defined by
Algorithm

1
for the input tuple
(p,m)
. We first show that the hypergraph
(H,w)
satisfies property (1). Let
X\subseteq V
be arbitrary. Then, we have that

\displaystyle b_{(H,w)}(X)

\displaystyle=b_{(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})}(X-\mathcal{Z})+b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(X)

\displaystyle\geq p^{\prime\prime}(X-\mathcal{Z})+b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(X)

\displaystyle\geq p^{\prime}(X)+b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(X)

\displaystyle=p(X).

Here, the first inequality holds by the inductive hypothesis property (1) and the second inequality is because
p^{\prime\prime}={p^{\prime}}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
by Step
10
.
Next, we show that hypergraph
(H,w)
satisfies property (2). Let
u\in V
be arbitrary. Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle b_{(H,w)}(u)

\displaystyle=b_{(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})}(u)+b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(u)

\displaystyle=b_{(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})}(u)+\alpha\chi_{A}(u)

\displaystyle=m^{\prime\prime}(u)+\alpha\chi_{A}(u)

\displaystyle=m(u).

Here, the third equality is by the inductive hypothesis property (2). Next, we show that the hypergraph
(H,w)
satisfies property (3). We have the following:

\sum_{e\in E}w(e)=\sum_{e\in E^{\prime\prime}}w(e)+w(A)=\sum_{e\in E^{\prime\prime}}w^{\prime\prime}(e)+\alpha=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}+\alpha=K_{p}.

Here, the third equality is by induction hypothesis property (3), and the final equality is by
Lemma

4.3
(c).
∎

4.3
Recursion Depth and Hypergraph Support Size

In this section, we give an upper bound on the number of recursive calls witnessed by an execution of
Algorithm

1
. The main result of this section is
Lemma

4.9
which we show at the end of the section.
We begin with some convenient notation that will be used in this section as well as the next section.

Notation.

By
Lemma

4.4
, the number of recursive calls made by
Algorithm

1
is finite.
We will use
\ell
to denote the depth of recursion. We will refer to the recursive call at depth
i\in[\ell]
as
recursive call
i
or the
i^{th}
recursive call
. We let
V_{i}
denote the ground set at the start of recursive call
i
, and
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
m_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
denote the input functions to recursive call
i
. Furthermore, we let
{\mathcal{D}}_{i},T_{i},A_{i},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\mathcal{Z}_{i},(H^{i}_{0},w^{i}_{0}),p^{\prime}_{i},m^{\prime}_{i},p^{\prime\prime}_{i},m^{\prime\prime}_{i}
denote the quantities
{\mathcal{D}},T,A,\alpha,\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\mathcal{Z},(H_{0},w_{0}),

p^{\prime},m^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}
as defined by
Algorithm

1
during the
i^{th}
recursive call for
i\in[\ell-1]
. For convenience, we also define the relevant sets, values and functions during the base case (
\ell^{th}
recursive call) as follows:
A_{\ell},{\mathcal{D}}_{\ell},\mathcal{Z}_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\emptyset
,
\alpha_{\ell},\alpha_{\ell}^{(1)},\alpha_{\ell}^{(2)},\alpha_{\ell}^{(3)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=0
,
m_{\ell}^{\prime},m_{\ell}^{\prime\prime},m_{\ell+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=m_{\ell}
, and
p_{\ell}^{\prime},p_{\ell}^{\prime\prime},p_{\ell+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=p_{\ell}
. Finally, let

\left(H_{i}=\left(V_{i},E_{i}\right),w_{i}\right)
denote the hypergraph returned by the
i^{th}
recursive call.

We note that
Lemma

4.3
and induction on the recursion depth
i
immediately imply the following lemma which says that for every
i\in[\ell]
, the input tuple
(p_{i},m_{i})
satisfies the hypothesis of
Theorem

1.7
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

1
is a tuple
(p_{1},m_{1})
, where
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V_{1}
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for all
u\in V_{1}
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be the number of recursive calls witnessed by the execution of
Algorithm

1
and, for all
i\in[\ell]
, let
(p_{i},m_{i})
be the input tuple to the
i^{th}
recursive call of the execution. Then, for all
i\in[\ell]
we have that
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m_{i}(X)\geq p_{i}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V_{i}
and
m_{i}(u)\leq K_{p_{i}}
for all
u\in V_{i}
.

Set Families.

For our analysis, we will focus on certain set families associated with an execution of
Algorithm

1
. Let
i\in[\ell]
be a recursive call of
Algorithm

1
. We define
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\leq i}\coloneqq\cup_{j\in[i]}\mathcal{Z}_{i}
. We use
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}
to denote the families of
minimal

p_{i}
-maximizers and
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizers respectively, i.e.,
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
is the collection of inclusionwise minimal sets in the family
\{X\subseteq V_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}p_{i}(X)=K_{p_{i}}\}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}
is the collection of inclusionwise minimal sets in the family
\{X\subseteq V_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}p_{i}^{\prime}(X)=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}\}
.
Lemma

4.6
below shows the progression of these families across recursive calls of an execution of
Algorithm

1
. We will also be interested in families of
all
minimal maximizers of the input functions witnessed by the algorithm up to a given recursive call. Formally, we define the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\coloneqq\cup_{j\in[i]}{\mathcal{F}}_{j}
.

Lemma

4.7
below summarizes useful properties of the stated families.

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

1
is a tuple
(p_{1},m_{1})
, where
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V_{1}
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for all
u\in V_{1}
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be the number of recursive calls witnessed by the execution of
Algorithm

1
and, for all
i\in[\ell]
, let
(p_{i},m_{i})
be the input tuple to the
i^{th}
recursive call of the execution. Then, for all
i\in[\ell]
, we have the following:

(a)

if
Y\subseteq V_{i}
is a
p_{i}
-maximizer, then
Y
is also a
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizer, and

(b)

if
Y\subseteq V_{i}
is a
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizer such that
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i}\not=\emptyset
, then
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i}
is a
p_{i+1}
-maximizer.

Proof.

We note that both properties trivially hold if
i=\ell
, and so we consider the case where when
i\leq\ell-1
(i.e.
Algorithm

1
is in its recursive case). We recall that by
Lemma

4.5
,
p_{i}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m_{i}
is a positive function such that
m_{i}(X)\geq p_{i}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V_{i}
and
m_{i}(u)\leq K_{p_{i}}
for all
u\in V_{i}
. We prove both parts separately below.

(a)

Let
Y\subseteq V_{i}
be a
p_{i}
-maximizer. We recall that by Steps
4
and
5
during the
i^{th}
recursive call, the set
A_{i}
is a transversal for the family of
p_{i}
-maximizers. Thus, we have that
p_{i}^{\prime}(Y)=p_{i}(Y)-\alpha_{i}=K_{p_{i}}-\alpha_{i}=K_{p^{\prime}_{i}}
, where the first equality is because
A_{i}\cap Y\not=\emptyset
and the third equality is by
Lemma

4.5
and
Lemma

4.2
(b).

(b)

Let
Y\subseteq V_{i}
be a
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizer such that
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i}\neq\emptyset
. We have the following:

K_{p_{i+1}}\geq p_{i+1}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i})=\max\{p_{i}^{\prime}(Y\cup R)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{i}\}\geq p_{i}^{\prime}(Y)=K_{p^{\prime}_{i}}=K_{p_{i+1}},

where the final equality is by
Lemma

4.2
(b) and
Lemma

4.3
(c). Thus, all inequalities are equations and we have that
p_{i+1}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i})=K_{p_{i+1}}
.

∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

1
is a tuple
(p_{1},m_{1})
, where
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V_{1}
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for all
u\in V_{1}
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be the number of recursive calls witnessed by the execution of
Algorithm

1
and, for all
i\in[\ell]
, let
(p_{i},m_{i})
be the input tuple to the
i^{th}
recursive call of the execution. Then, for all
i\in[\ell-1]
, we have the following:

(a)

\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i+1}
; furthermore,
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
if and only if
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\not=\emptyset
(i.e.,
\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i}\subsetneq\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i+1}
),

(b)

{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}
; furthermore, if
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(2)}<\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
, then
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\subsetneq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}
,

(c)

{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}
; furthermore, if
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(3)}
, then
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subsetneq{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We recall that by
Lemma

4.5
,
p_{i}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m_{i}
is a positive function such that
m_{i}(X)\geq p_{i}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V_{i}
and
m_{i}(u)\leq K_{p_{i}}
for all
u\in V_{i}
. We prove each property separately below.

(a)

We have that
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\leq i}\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}_{\leq i+1}
by definition. We now show the second part of the claim. The reverse direction follows because the function
m
is a positive function. For the forward direction, suppose that
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
. Let
u\in A_{i}
be a vertex that witnesses
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
, i.e.
m_{i}(u)=\alpha_{i}
. Then, we have that
m_{i}^{\prime}(u)=m_{i}(u)-\alpha_{i}\chi_{A_{i}}(u)=0
. Thus,
u\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}
.

(b)

We note that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}
follows by definition. We now show the second part of the claim. Suppose that
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(2)}<\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
. Then by part (a) of the current lemma, we have that
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\emptyset
. Consequently
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i+1}
by the definition of the two functions. We consider the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}
of minimal
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizers.
By
Lemma

4.6
(a), we have that
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}={\mathcal{F}}_{i+1}
, and thus,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\cup{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}
, where the equalities in both the previous expressions are because
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i+1}
. We now show that the first inclusion is strict. For convenience, we let
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}^{\prime}\coloneqq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\cup{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}
.
By way of contradiction, suppose that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}^{\prime}
.
Let
X\subseteq V_{i}-A_{i}
be a set such that
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(2)}=K_{p_{i}}-p_{i}(X)
(such a set exists since
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(2)}
). Then, we have that
p_{i}^{\prime}(X)=p_{i}(X)=K_{p_{i}}-\alpha_{i}^{(2)}=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}.

Here, the first equality is because
X\subseteq V_{i}-A_{i}
, the second equality is because our choice of
X
satisfies
\alpha_{i}^{(2)}=K_{p_{i}}-p_{i}(X)
, and the final inequality is by
Lemma

4.2
(b). Thus, the set
X
is a
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizer. Furthermore,
X
is not a
p_{i}
-maximizer since
A_{i}
is a transversal for the family of
p_{i}
-maximizers by Step
5
, but
A_{i}\cap X=\emptyset
.
Consequently, there exists a set
Y\subseteq X
such that
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}-{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}^{\prime}
, we have that
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i-1}
. In particular, there exists a recursive call
j\in[i-1]
such that
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{j}
. Since
Y\subseteq V_{i}
, by
Lemma

4.6
(a), (b) and induction on
j
, we have that
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
. Thus, the set
Y
is a
p_{i}
-maximizer and so
A_{i}\cap Y\not=\emptyset
by Step
5
, a contradiction to
A_{i}\cap X=\emptyset
.

(c)

First, we show that
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
. Let
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
. Then we have the following:

m_{i}(u)^{\prime}=m_{i}(u)-\alpha_{i}\chi_{A_{i}}(u)=K_{p_{i}}-\alpha_{i}\chi_{A_{i}}(u)=K_{p_{i}}-\alpha_{i}=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}.

Here, the second equality is because
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
, the third equality is because
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
, and the final equality is by
Lemma

4.2
(b). Thus,
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
and we have that
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
. Next, we show that
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}
. Let
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
. We note that if
u\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}
, then we have that
0=m_{i}^{\prime}(u)=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}
, and thus
i=\ell
, contradicting
i\in[1,\ell-1]
. Thus,
u\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}
, i.e.
u\in V_{i+1}
. Then, we have that
m_{i+1}(u)=m_{i}^{\prime}(u)=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}=K_{p_{i+1}}
, where the final equality is by
Lemma

4.2
(b) and
Lemma

4.3
(c). Thus,
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}
and we have that
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}
.

We now show the second part of the claim. Suppose that
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(3)}
and let
v\in V_{i}-A_{i}
be the vertex which witnesses
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(3)}
, i.e.
\alpha_{i}=K_{p_{i}}-m_{i}(v)
. Then, we have that:

m_{i}^{\prime}(v)=m_{i}(v)-\alpha_{i}\chi_{A_{i}}(v)=m_{i}(v)=K_{p_{i}}-\alpha_{i}=K_{p_{i+1}}.

Here, the second equality is because
v\in V_{i}-A_{i}
. The third equality is because the vertex
v
witnesses
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(3)}
. The final equality is by
Lemma

4.3
(c). Thus,
v\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}\backslash{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
.

∎

The next lemma shows how the function input to an arbitrary recursive call is related to the function input to the first recursive call. This expression will be useful in implementing
Algorithm

1
since it will allow us to construct function maximization oracle for
p_{i}
using only polynomially many queries to the function maximization oracle of
p_{1}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

1
is a tuple
(p_{1},m_{1})
, where
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V_{1}
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for all
u\in V_{1}
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be the number of recursive calls witnessed by the execution of
Algorithm

1
. For
i\in[\ell]
, let
(p_{i},m_{i})
be the input tuple to the
i^{th}
recursive call; moreover, for
i\in[\ell-1]
, let
\mathcal{Z}_{i},(H^{i}_{0},w^{i}_{0})
be as defined by
Algorithm

1
for the input
(p_{i},m_{i})
.
Then, for each
i\in[\ell]
we have that

p_{i}={\left(p_{1}-\sum_{j\in[i-1]}b_{(\tilde{H}_{j},\tilde{w}_{j})}\right)}/_{\bigcup_{j\in[i-1]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}},

where
(\tilde{H}_{0}^{i},\tilde{w}_{0}^{i})
denotes the hypergraph obtained by adding the vertices
\cup_{j\in[i-1]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}
to the hypergraph
(H^{i}_{0},w^{i}_{0})
.

Proof.

We show the lemma by induction on
i
. The base case
i=1
trivially holds. For induction, suppose that
i\geq 2
. Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle p_{i}={\left(p_{i-1}-b_{({H}^{i-1}_{0},{w}^{i-1}_{0})}\right)}/_{\mathcal{Z}_{i-1}}

\displaystyle={\left({\left(p_{1}-\sum_{j\in[i-2]}b_{(\tilde{H}_{0}^{j},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j})}\right)}/_{\bigcup_{j\in[i-2]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}}-b_{({H}^{i-1}_{0},{w}^{i-1}_{0})}\right)}/_{\mathcal{Z}_{i-1}}

\displaystyle={\left({\left(p_{1}-\sum_{j\in[i-2]}b_{(\tilde{H}_{0}^{j},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j})}\right)}/_{\bigcup_{j\in[i-2]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}}-b_{(\tilde{H}^{i-1}_{0},\tilde{w}^{i-1}_{0})}\right)}/_{\mathcal{Z}_{i-1}}

\displaystyle={\left({\left(p_{1}-\sum_{j\in[i-1]}b_{(\tilde{H}_{0}^{j},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j})}\right)}/_{\bigcup_{j\in[i-2]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}}\right)}/_{\mathcal{Z}_{i-1}}

Here, the first equality is by Step
10
, the second equality is by the inductive hypothesis, and the third and fourth equalities are because
b_{(\tilde{H}_{0}^{i-1},\tilde{w}_{0}^{i-1})}(X)=b_{({H}_{0}^{i-1},{w}_{0}^{i-1})}(X\cap V_{i-1})
for all
X\subseteq V
by definition of the coverage function, where
V_{i}
is the ground set at the
i^{th}
recursive call. Simplifying the final RHS gives the claimed expression.
∎

We now show the main result of the section which says that an execution of
Algorithm

1
witnesses
O(|V|)
recursive calls. Since every recursive call adds at most one new hyperedge to the hypergraph returned by the execution, this also implies that the number of hyperedges in a solution returned by
Algorithm

1
is
O(|V|)
. This shows property (4) of
Theorem

1.7
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose that
V_{1}\not=\emptyset
and the input to
Algorithm

1
is a tuple
(p_{1},m_{1})
, where
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V_{1}
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for all
u\in V_{1}
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be the number of recursive calls witnessed by the execution of
Algorithm

1
. Then,

1.

the recursion depth
\ell
of
Algorithm

1
is at most
4|V_{1}|-1
, and

2.

the number of hyperedges in the hypergraph
(H=(V_{1},E),w)
returned by
Algorithm

1
is at most
4|V_{1}|-1
.

Proof.

We note that part (2) of the current lemma follows from part (1) since every recursive call adds at most one new hyperedge to the hypergraph returned by the execution in Step
11
. Thus, it suffices to show part (1). We define a potential function
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[\ell]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
as follows: for each
i\in[\ell]
,

\phi(i)\coloneqq|\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i}|+|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}|+|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}|.

By
Lemma

4.7
, we have that
\phi
is a monotone increasing function, since each of the three terms is non-decreasing and at least one of the three terms strictly increases with increasing
i\in[\ell]
. Consequently, the number of recursive calls witnessed by the execution of
Algorithm

1
is at most
\phi(\ell)-\phi(0)\leq|\mathcal{Z}_{\leq\ell}|+|{\mathcal{F}}_{{\leq\ell}}|+|{\mathcal{D}}_{\ell}|\leq 2|V|+|{\mathcal{F}}_{{\leq\ell}}|\leq 4|V|-1
. Here, the final inequality is because the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}
is laminar by the following: let
(p_{i},m_{i})
denote the input to the
i^{th}
recursive call of
Algorithm

1
. By
Lemma

4.3
(a) and induction on
i
, we have that
p_{i+1}={(p_{i}-b_{(H_{0}^{i},w_{0}^{i})})}/_{\mathcal{Z}_{i}}
for every recursive call
i\in[\ell-1]
. Then, laminarity of the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}
is obtained by applying
Lemma

3.2
to the sequence of functions
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
.
∎

4.4
Runtime

In this section, we show that
Algorithm

1
can be implemented to run in strongly polynomial time provided that the input function
p
is skew-supermodular.

Lemma

4.11
is the main lemma of this section. Let
i\in[\ell-1]
be a recursive call of an execution of
Algorithm

1
.
In order to show that the
i^{th}
recursive call can be implemented in polynomial time, we will require the ability to compute a minimal transversal for the family of
p_{i}
-maximizers in strongly polynomial time (Step
4
). We recall that the function
p_{i}
is skew-supermodular by
Lemma

4.5
. Consequently,
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{i}}
is a disjoint family by
Lemma

3.1
. Thus, in order to compute a transversal for the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{i}}
in strongly polynomial time, it suffices to explicitly compute the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{i}}
itself in strongly polynomial time, and pick an arbitrary vertex from each set of the family. We note that disjointness of the family guarantees that it contains at most a linear number of sets. The next lemma shows that
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{i}}
can be computed in strongly polynomial time using the function maximization oracle for
p_{i}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a skew-supermodular function. Then, the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
can be computed in
O(|V|^{3})
time and
O(|V|^{2})
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. The run-time includes the time to construct the input to the queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.

Proof.

We describe a recursive procedure that takes a
p
-maximizer
X\subseteq V
as input and returns a minimal
p
-maximizer contained in
X
. For every
u\in X
, let
Z^{u}
be the set returned by querying
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\emptyset,T_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(V-X)\cup\{u\},y_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{0\}^{V})
. If there exists a vertex
u\in X
such that the set
Z^{u}
is a
p
-maximizer, then recursively compute a minimal
p
-maximizer contained in
Z^{u}
. Otherwise, return the set
X
.

The correctness of the above procedure is because of the following. The above procedure terminates (in at most
|V|
recursive calls) because the cardinality of the input set
X
strictly decreases during each recursive call (i.e.,
|X^{\prime}|>|X|
). Let
Y\subseteq X
be a minimal
p
-maximizer contained in the set
X
. If
Y=X
, then there are no
p
-maximizers that are strictly contained in the set
X
. Here, the procedure correctly returns the set
X
. Alternatively, suppose that
Y\subsetneq X
and let
u\in X-Y
. Then, there exists a
p
-maximizer
Z
such that
Y\subseteq Z\subseteq X-\{u\}
. Consequently, the set
Z^{u}
computed by the procedure will be a
p
-maximizer that is strictly contained in the set
X
. Using this fact, the procedure can be shown to return a minimal
p
-maximizer contained in the set
X
by induction on
|X|
.

We now describe a slight modification of the above procedure that leads to an improved runtime and a smaller number of queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Let
u\in X
be a vertex. If the set
Z^{u}
is a
p
-maximizer, then we include
u
in the input set
T_{0}
to all
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
queries in subsequent recursive calls of the procedure. Alternatively, if the set
Z^{u}
is not a
p
-maximizer, then we include
u
in the input set
S_{0}
to all
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
queries in subsequent recursive calls procedure. Consequently, the number of queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
(and number of recursive calls of the procedure) is at most
|V|
. We note that constructing the input to each query and verifying whether the set returned by the query is a
p
-maximizer can be done in
O(|V|)
time. Thus, the procedure terminates in
O(|V|^{2})
time. Here, the runtime includes the time to construct the inputs to the
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
queries.

We note that by
Lemma

3.1
, the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is disjoint. Moreover, one can compute a set from the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
using the procedure from above. Thus, the entire family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
can be computed by iteratively applying the procedure from above – each time a set from the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is found, it will be included in the input set
T_{0}
to all
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
queries in subsequent iterations of the procedure. The lemma follows because each iteration of this new procedure runs in
O(|V|^{2})
time and uses at most
|V|
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
; moreover, there are at most
O(|V|)
iterations of this new procedure since
|{\mathcal{F}}_{p}|=O(|V|)
by the disjointness of
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
.
∎

We now show the main lemma of the section which says that
Algorithm

1
can be implemented in strongly polynomial time and polynomial number of calls to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. We remark that the maximization oracle used in Lemma
4.11
is stronger than the one used in Lemma
4.10
. This stronger version is needed for the input function
p
in order to be able to construct the required
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
for all recursive calls
i\in[\ell]
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

1
is a tuple
(p,m)
, where
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
. Then,
Algorithm

1
can be implemented to run in
O(|V|^{5})
time using
O(|V|^{4})
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. The run-time includes the time to construct the hypergraphs used as input to the queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.
Moreover, for each query to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
|V|
vertices and
O(|V|)
hyperedges.

Proof.

We recall that
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
denotes the number of recursive calls witnessed by the execution of
Algorithm

1
and that
(p_{i},m_{i})
denotes the input tuple to the
i^{th}
recursive call of the execution. Let
i\in[\ell]
be a recursive call.
The next claim shows that all steps of
Algorithm

1
except for Steps
10
and
11
in the
i^{th}
recursive call can be implemented to run in strongly polynomial time and strongly polynomial number of queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.

Claim 4.1
.

All steps except for Steps
10
and
11
in the
i^{th}
recursive call can be implemented to run in
O(|V_{i}|^{3})
time using
O(|V_{i}|^{2})
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. The run-time includes the time to construct the inputs for the queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.

Proof.

We note that the value
K_{p_{i}}
can be computed by querying
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\emptyset,T_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\emptyset,y_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{0\}^{V_{i}})
. Step
3
can be computed in
O(|V_{i}|)
time by explicitly checking the condition
m_{i}(u)=K_{p_{i}}
for every
u\in V_{i}
. By
Lemma

4.5
we have that the function
p_{i}
is skew-supermodular. Consequently, the family of minimal
p_{i}
-maximizers is a disjoint family by
Lemma

3.1
. Moreover, by
Lemma

4.10
, this family can be computed in
O(|V_{i}|^{3})
time using
O(|V_{i}|^{2})
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where the runtime includes the time to construct the inputs for queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Consequently, by picking an arbitrary vertex from each set of the family, Steps
4
and
5
can be implemented in
O(|V_{i}|^{3})
time using
O(|V_{i}|^{2})
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where the runtime includes the time to construct the inputs for queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. The values
\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
and
\alpha_{i}^{(3)}
can be computed in
O(|V_{i}|)
time by iterating over all vertices in
V_{i}
. The value
\alpha_{i}^{(2)}
can be computed by the single query
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\emptyset,T_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=A,y_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{0\}^{V_{i}})
. Thus, Step
6
can be implemented in
O(|V_{i}|)
time using a single query to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where the runtime includes the time to construct the inputs for the query to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Steps
7
and
9
can be computed in
O(|V_{i}|)
time by iterating over all vertices in
V_{i}
. We note that the number of distinct hyperedges in the hypergraph obtained from recursion (Step
11
) is at most
\ell-i
since each recursive call adds at most one distinct hyperedge to the returned. Thus, Steps
12
and
13
can be implemented in
O(|V_{i}|+\ell)=O(|V_{1}|)
time, where the equality is because
\ell=O(|V_{1}|)
by
Lemma

4.9
.
∎

Next, we focus on the time to implement a query to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
using
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where we recall that
p_{1}=p
is the input function to the initial call to
Algorithm

1
.

Claim 4.2
.

For disjoint subsets
S,T\subseteq V_{i}
and a vector
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{V_{i}}
, the answer to the query
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S,T_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=T,y_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=y)
can be computed in
O(|V_{1}|^{2})
time using at most
|V_{1}|+1
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. The run-time includes the time to construct the inputs for the queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Moreover, for each query to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
|V_{1}|
vertices and
O(|V_{1}|)
hyperedges.

Proof.

We prove the claim in two steps. In the first step, we will use the expression for the function
p_{i}
given by
Lemma

4.8
to construct an answer for the query
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S,T,y)
using a single query to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. In the second step, we will obtain the desired runtime using
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
by invoking
Lemma

2.2
.

For every
j\in[i]
, let
(\tilde{H}_{0}^{j},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j})
denote the hypergraph obtained by adding the vertices
\cup_{k\in[j-1]}\mathcal{Z}_{k}
to the hypergraph
(H^{k}_{0},w^{k}_{0})
. We now show the first step of the proof via the following procedure. First, construct the hypergraph
(G,c)
and the vector
y_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{V_{1}}
, where
G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{j\in[i-1]}\tilde{H}_{0}^{j}
,
c\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{j\in[i-1]},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j}
and
y_{1}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=y_{0}(u)
for every
u\in V_{i}
,
y_{1}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=0
otherwise Next, obtain
(Z,p_{1}(Z))
by querying
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}((G,c),S,T,y_{1})
. Finally, return
(Z-\cup_{j=1}^{i-1}\mathcal{Z}_{j},p(Z))
as the answer to the query
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S,T,y)
. We note that correctness of the procedure is because
p_{i}={\left(p_{1}-\sum_{j\in[i-1]}b_{(\tilde{H}_{j},\tilde{w}_{j})}\right)}/_{\bigcup_{j\in[i-1]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}}
by
Lemma

4.8
.

We now show the second step of the proof. We observe that the hypergraph
(G,c)
constructed above has vertex set
V_{1}
since for every
j\in[i-1]
, the vertex set of
(\tilde{H}_{0}^{j},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j})
is contained in
V_{1}
. Moreover,
(G,c)

has
i-1\leq\ell=O(|V_{1}|)
hyperedges because for every
j\in[i-1]
, the hypergraph
(\tilde{H}_{0}^{j},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j})
has exactly
1
hyperedge by Step
8
, and
\ell=O(|V_{1}|)
by
Lemma

4.9
. Consequently, by
Lemma

2.2
, the answer to the query
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}((G,c),S,T,y_{1})
in the above procedure can be computed in
O(|V_{1}|^{2})
time using
|V_{1}|+1
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where the runtime includes the time to construct the inputs to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
; moreover, the hypergraph used as input to each
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
query has at most
|V_{1}|
vertices and
O(|V_{1}|)
hyperedges. Finally, we note that the construction of hypergraph
(G,c)
and returning the (set, value) pair returned by the above procedure can be implemented in
O(|V_{1}|^{2})
time and so the claim holds.
∎

We now complete the proof using the two claims above. By
4.1
, all steps except for Steps
10
and
11
in the
i^{th}
recursive call can be implemented to run in
O(|V_{i}|^{3})
time using
O(|V_{i}|^{2})
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. By
4.2
, an answer to a single
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
query can be computed in
O(|V_{1}|^{2})
time using at most
|V_{1}|+1
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Thus, all steps except for Steps
10
and
11
in the
i^{th}
recursive call can be implemented to run in
O(|V_{1}|^{4})
time and
O(|V_{1}|^{3})
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. By Claims
4.1
and
4.2
, the run-time also includes the time to construct the inputs for the queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Moreover, by
4.2
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to each
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
query has
|V_{1}|
vertices and
O(|V_{1}|)
hyperedges.

We note that Steps
10
and
11
need not be implemented explicitly for the purposes of the algorithm. Instead,
{p_{i+1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
can be used to execute all steps except for Steps
10
and
11
in the
(i+1)^{th}
recursive call. We note that
4.2
also enables us to answer a
{p_{i+1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
query in
O(|V_{1}|^{2})
time using at most
|V_{1}|+1
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Thus, we have shown that each recursive call can be implemented to run in
O(|V_{1}|^{4})
time and
O(|V_{1}|^{3})
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Moreover, each query to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
is on an input hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
that has
|V_{1}|
vertices and
O(|V_{1}|)
hyperedges. By
Lemma

4.9
, the number of recursive calls witnessed by the execution of
Algorithm

1
is
O(|V_{1}|)
. Thus,
Algorithm

1
can be implemented to run in
O(|V_{1}|^{5})
time and
O(|V_{1}|^{4})
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where each query to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
is on an input hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
that has
|V_{1}|
vertices and
O(|V_{1}|)
hyperedges.
∎

5
Weak Cover using Near-Uniform Hyperedges

In this section, we present an algorithm to obtain a degree-specified near-uniform weak cover of a function. We emphasize that our algorithm does not require any assumption on the structure of the input set-function
p
other than it being integer-valued and satisfying certain conditions with the input degree bounds. In subsequent sections where we prove

Theorem

1.8
and
Theorem

1.9
, we will show that this algorithm, when executed on inputs satisfying the theorem-specific hypotheses (regarding skew-supermodularity of
p
), returns hypergraphs with the claimed properties of the respective theorems.

In
Section

5.1
, we present our algorithm (See
Algorithm

2
). Our algorithm is recursive in nature. We define the notion of a
feasible execution
of our algorithm in the same section. In
Section

5.2
, we prove that a feasible execution of the algorithm terminates within finitely many recursive calls.
Here, we also show that it returns a hypergraph satisfying properties (1)–(4) of
Theorem

1.8
and
Theorem

1.9
. In
Section

5.3
, we bound the number of recursive calls of a specific type during feasible executions of our algorithm—we use this bound in subsequent sections to show properties of our algorithm when the input function is skew-supermodular or the maximum of two skew-supermodular functions.
In
Section

5.4
, we turn our attention to the case where the input is a skew-supermodular function or the maximum of two skew-supermodular functions. Here, we show that the execution of our algorithm is a feasible execution on such inputs.
In
Section

5.5
, we bound the run-time of the algorithm as a function of the recursion depth for skew-supermodular functions and maximum of two skew-supermodular functions as inputs.

Finally, in Sections
5.6
and
5.7
, we use the results from the preceding sections to prove our main results. In
Section

5.6
, we bound the number of recursive calls of type not considered in
Section

5.3
for when the input function
p
is a skew-supermodular function and complete the proof of
Theorem

1.8
. In
Section

5.7
, we bound the number of recursive calls of type not considered in
Section

5.3
for when the input function
p
is the maximum of two skew-supermodular functions and complete the proof of
Theorem

1.9
.

5.1
The Algorithm

Our algorithm takes as input (1) a function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, (2) a positive function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and (3) a set
J\subseteq V

and returns a hypergraph
\left(H=\left(V,E\right),w\right)
. We note that in contrast to the non-negative function
m
appearing in the statements of
Theorem

1.8
and
Theorem

1.9
, the function
m
that is input to our algorithm should be positive. However, the algorithm in this section will not require the function
p
to be skew-supermodular. The algorithm is recursive. The input set
J\subseteq V
is used to share information between subsequent recursive calls and is thus a consequence of the recursive nature of the algorithm. This input parameter can be suppressed if the algorithm is implemented in an iterative manner.

Our algorithm is LP-based. We define the polyhedron associated with the LP now.
For a set function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and a function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we define the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
as:

Q(p,m)\coloneqq\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{V}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{l}{(\text{i})}{\ \ \ 0\leq x(u)\leq\min\{1,m(u)\}}\hfill{\qquad\forall\ u\in V}\\
{(\text{ii})}{\ \ x(Z)\geq 1}\hfill{\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall\ Z\subseteq V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\ p(Z)=K_{p}}\\
{(\text{iii})}{\ x(u)=1}\hfill{\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall\ u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\ m(u)=K_{p}}\\
{(\text{iv})}{\ x(Z)\leq m(Z)-p(Z)+1}\hfill{\ \ \ \forall\ Z\subseteq V}\\
{(\text{v})}{\ \ \left\lfloor\frac{m(V)}{K_{p}}\right\rfloor\leq x(V)\leq\left\lceil\frac{m(V)}{K_{p}}\right\rceil}\hfill\\
\end{array}\right\}.

(
Q
-polyhedron)

We now give an informal description of the algorithm (refer to
Algorithm

2
for a formal description).
If
V=\emptyset
, then the algorithm returns the empty hypergraph. Otherwise, the algorithm computes an extreme point optimum solution
y
to the LP
\max\{\sum_{u\in J}y_{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y\in Q(p,m)\}
and defines
A
to be the support of
y
.
The algorithm uses the set
A
to compute the following five intermediate quantities
\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(4)},\alpha^{(5)}
:

•

\alpha^{(1)}\coloneqq\min\{m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in A\}
,

•

\alpha^{(2)}\coloneqq\min\left\{K_{p}-p(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\subseteq V-A\right\}
,

•

\alpha^{(3)}\coloneqq\min\left\{K_{p}-m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in V-A\right\}
,

•

\alpha^{(4)}\coloneqq\min\left\{\left\lfloor{\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}}\right\rfloor\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|A\cap X|\geq 2\right\}
, and

•

\alpha^{(5)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m(V)\mod K_{p}&\text{if $|A|=\left\lceil{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rceil>m(V)/K_{p}$,}\\
K_{p}-(m(V)\mod K_{p})&\text{if $|A|=\left\lfloor{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rfloor<m(V)/K_{p}$,}\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

and defines
\alpha\coloneqq\min\left\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(4)},\alpha^{(5)}\right\}
.
Next, the algorithm computes the set
\mathcal{Z}\coloneqq\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m(u)-\alpha=0\}
and defines
(H_{0},w_{0})
to be the hypergraph on vertex set
V
consisting of a single hyperedge
A
with weight
w_{0}(A)=\alpha
. Next, the algorithm defines the two functions
m^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
m^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V-\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
as
m^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=m-\alpha\chi_{A}
, and
m^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}={m^{\prime}}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}
. Next, the algorithm defines the two functions
p^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
p^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V-\mathcal{Z}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
as

p^{\prime}\coloneqq p-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}
and
p^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq{p^{\prime}}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
. The algorithm then defines the set
J^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq A-\mathcal{Z}
and recursively calls itself with the input tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime})
to obtain a hypergraph
\left(H^{\prime\prime}=\left(V^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq V-\mathcal{Z},E^{\prime\prime}\right),w^{\prime\prime}\right)
. Finally, the algorithm extends the hypergraph
H^{\prime\prime}
with the set
\mathcal{Z}
of vertices, adds the set
A
with weight
\alpha
as a new hyperedge to the hyperedge set
E^{\prime\prime}
, and returns the resulting hypergraph. If the hyperedge
A
already has non-zero weight in
(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})
, then the algorithm increases the weight of the hyperedge
A
by
\alpha
, and returns the resulting hypergraph.

The definition of the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
immediately implies the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a set function with
K_{p}>0
and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be a function. Let
y
be an integral vector in
Q(p,m)

and
A\coloneqq\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y_{u}>0\}
.
Then, the following properties hold:

(1)

The set
A
is a transversal for the family of
p
-maximizers,

(2)

\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m(u)=K_{p}\}\subseteq A
,

(3)

|A\cap Z|\leq m(Z)-p(Z)+1
for all
Z\subseteq V
, and

(4)

|A|\in[\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor,\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil]
.

We recall that a polyhedron is integral if all its extreme points are integral. Non-emptiness and integrality of the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
will be an important requirement to show properties about
Algorithm

2
. We will denote recursive calls of
Algorithm

2
during which
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron as
feasible recursive calls
and say that an execution of
Algorithm

2
is a
feasible execution
if every recursive call during the execution is feasible or
V=\emptyset
in that recursive call. We note that if
V=\emptyset
in a recursive call, then the algorithm terminates with that recursive call.

Algorithm 2

Weak covering with hyperedges

Input:
Function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and positive function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}

Output:
Hypergraph
(H=(V,E),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})

\mathbf{\textsc{Algorithm}}\left(p,m,J\right)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}

1:

if

V=\emptyset

then

return
Empty Hypergraph
\left(\left(\emptyset,\emptyset\right),\emptyset\right)
.

2:

else
:

3:

Compute an extreme point optimum solution
y
to
\max\left\{\sum_{u\in J}y_{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y\in Q(p,m)\right\}

4:

A\coloneqq\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y_{u}>0\}

5:

\alpha\coloneqq\min\begin{cases}\alpha^{(1)}\coloneqq\min\left\{m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in A\right\}\\
\alpha^{(2)}\coloneqq\min\left\{K_{p}-p(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\subseteq V-A\right\}\\
\alpha^{(3)}\coloneqq\min\left\{K_{p}-m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in V-A\right\}\\
\alpha^{(4)}\coloneqq\min\left\{\left\lfloor{\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}}\right\rfloor\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|A\cap X|\geq 2\right\}\\
\alpha^{(5)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m(V)\mod K_{p}&\text{if $|A|=\left\lceil{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rceil>m(V)/K_{p}$,}\\
K_{p}-(m(V)\mod K_{p})&\text{if $|A|=\left\lfloor{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rfloor<m(V)/K_{p}$,}\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}\end{cases}

6:

\mathcal{Z}\coloneqq\left\{u\in A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m(u)=\alpha\right\}

7:

Let
(H_{0}\coloneqq(V,E_{0}\coloneqq\{A\},w_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{0}\rightarrow\{\alpha\})

8:
