m^{\prime}\coloneqq m-\alpha\chi_{A}
and
m^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq{m^{\prime}}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}

9:

p^{\prime}\coloneqq p-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}
and
p^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq{p^{\prime}}/_{\mathcal{Z}}

10:

(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})\coloneqq\textsc{Algorithm}\left(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq A-\mathcal{Z}\right)

11:

Obtain
(G,c)
from
(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})
by adding vertices
\mathcal{Z}

12:

return

(G+H_{0},c+w_{0})

5.2
Termination and Partial Correctness of
Algorithm

2

This section is devoted to proving that
a feasible execution of
Algorithm

2
terminates within finite number of recursive calls and returns a hypergraph satisfying properties (1)–(4) of
Theorem

1.8
and
Theorem

1.9
–see
Lemma

5.5
.
We also prove certain additional properties in this section about the execution of
Algorithm

2
that will help in subsequent analysis.
We emphasize that all lemmas that appear in this section do not require the function
p
to be skew-supermodular.
The ideas to prove
Lemma

5.5
are modifications of the results due to Bernáth and Király
[
8
]
; we suggest first time readers to skip reading this section until this lemma and return to them as needed.

We begin with the following lemma which shows that the value
\alpha
computed in Step
5
is positive and the function value of the entire ground set for function
m
strictly decreases in each feasible recursive call.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a set function, and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
,
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
, and
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron. Let
\alpha,\mathcal{Z}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
. Then, we have that

(a)

\alpha\geq 1
, and

(b)

m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})<m(V)
.

Proof.

We note that since
Q(p,m)
is non-empty, we have that
V\not=\emptyset
. Consequently,
Algorithm

2
is in its recursive case. We prove both properties separately below.

(a)

Let
y,A,\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(4)},\alpha^{(5)}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
.
Since
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron, the vector
y
is the indicator vector of the set
A
. We note that
\alpha^{(1)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
since the function
m
is a positive integer valued function. Thus, it suffices to show that
\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(4)},\alpha^{(5)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.

First, we recall that
\alpha^{(2)}=\min\{K_{p}-p(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\subseteq V-A\}
.
We note that
\alpha^{(2)}\in\mathbb{Z}
since
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, it suffices to show that
\alpha^{(2)}\geq 1
. By way of contradiction, say
\alpha^{(2)}\leq 0
. Since
K_{p}\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
by definition, we have that
\alpha^{(2)}\geq 0
. Thus,
\alpha^{(2)}=0
. Let
X\subseteq V
be a set that witnesses
\alpha^{(2)}=0
, i.e.
\alpha^{(2)}=K_{p}-p(X)
and
X\subseteq V-A
. Since
\alpha^{(2)}=0
, we have that
p(X)=K_{p}
and thus, the set
X
is a
p
-maximizer that is disjoint from the set
A
. This contradicts
Lemma

5.1
(1).

Next, we recall that
\alpha^{(3)}=\min\{K_{p}-m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in V-A\}
.
We note that
\alpha^{(3)}\in\mathbb{Z}
since
K_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, it suffices to show that
\alpha^{(3)}\geq 1
.
By way of contradiction, say
\alpha^{(3)}\leq 0
. Since
K_{p}\geq m(u)
for all
u\in V
, we have that
\alpha^{(3)}\geq 0
. Thus,
\alpha^{(3)}=0
. Let
v\in V
be a vertex that witnesses
\alpha^{(3)}=0
, i.e.
\alpha^{(3)}=K_{p}-m(v)
and
v\in V-A
. Since
\alpha^{(3)}=0
, we have that
m(v)=K_{p}
. However, this contradicts
Lemma

5.1
(2).

Next, we recall that
\alpha^{(4)}=\min\left\{\left\lfloor{\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}}\right\rfloor\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|A\cap X|\geq 2\right\}
.
We note that
\alpha^{(4)}\in\mathbb{Z}
by definition and so it suffices to show that
\alpha^{(4)}\geq 1
.
By way of contradiction, say
\alpha^{(4)}\leq 0
. Since
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
, we have that
\alpha^{(4)}\geq 0
. Thus,
\alpha^{(4)}=0
. Let
Z\subseteq V
be a set that witnesses
\alpha^{(4)}=0
, i.e.
Z\coloneqq\arg\min\left\{\left\lfloor{\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}}\right\rfloor\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|A\cap X|\geq 2\right\}
. Consequently, we have that
\frac{m(Z)-p(Z)}{|A\cap Z|-1}<1
. However, this gives us that
m(Z)-|A\cap Z|<p(Z)-1
, contradicting
Lemma

5.1
(3).

Finally, we recall that

\alpha^{(5)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m(V)\mod K_{p}&\text{if $|A|=\left\lceil{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rceil>m(V)/K_{p}$,}\\
K_{p}-(m(V)\mod K_{p})&\text{if $|A|=\left\lfloor{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rfloor<m(V)/K_{p}$,}\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
\alpha^{(5)}=\infty
, then we are done. Suppose that
\alpha^{(5)}\neq\infty
. Then,
m(V)/K_{p}
is not an integer. By definition, this immediately implies that
\alpha^{(5)}\geq 1
.

(b)

Let
A,m^{\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
. Since
Q(p,m)
is non-empty, we have that
A\neq\emptyset
.
By part (1) of the current lemma, we have that
\alpha\geq 1
. Hence,
m^{\prime}(V)=m(V)-\alpha\chi_{A}<m(V)
by Step
8
. Since the function
m^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{V-\mathcal{Z}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is defined as
m^{\prime\prime}={(m-\alpha\chi_{A})}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}
by Step
9
, we have that
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})=m^{\prime}(V)<m(V)
and the claim holds.

∎

The next two lemmas will allow us to conclude that if the input functions
p
and
m
to a feasible recursive call of
Algorithm

2
satisfy
certain conditions, then the intermediate functions
p^{\prime},m^{\prime}
and the input functions to the subsequent recursive call
p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}
also satisfy the same conditions.
Such recursive properties will be useful in inductively proving properties about
Algorithm

2
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a set function, and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
,
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
, and
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron.
Let
p^{\prime}
,
m^{\prime}
and
\alpha
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
. Then, the following hold:

(a)

The function
m^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is a non-negative function,

(b)

K_{p^{\prime}}\coloneqq K_{p}-\alpha
,

(c)

m^{\prime}(u)\leq K_{p^{\prime}}
for all
u\in V^{\prime}
, and

(d)

m^{\prime}(X)\geq p^{\prime}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We note that since
Q(p,m)
is non-empty, we have that
V\not=\emptyset
. Consequently,
Algorithm

2
is in its recursive case. Moreover, since since
Q(p,m)
is an integral polyhedron, the vector
y
is the indicator vector of the set
A
, where
y
and
A
are as defined by the algorithm.
Let
\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(4)},\alpha^{(5)},\alpha
, and
(H_{0}=(V,E_{0}\coloneqq\{A\},w_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{0}\rightarrow\{\alpha\})
be as defined by the algorithm.
We show that the functions
m^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime}
satisfy each of the properties (a)-(d) below.

(a)

The function
m^{\prime}
is non-negative due to the following: if the vertex
u\not\in A
, then
m^{\prime}(u)=m(u)\geq 0
. Otherwise
u\in A
and we have that
m^{\prime}(u)=m(u)-\alpha\geq m(u)-\alpha^{(1)}\geq 0
, where the last inequality is by definition of
\alpha^{(1)}
in Step
5
.

(b)

Let
X\subseteq V
be a
p
-maximizer and
Y\subseteq V
be a
p^{\prime}
-maximizer.
We recall that the set
A
is a transversal for the family of
p
-maximizers by
Lemma

5.1
(1), and consequently,
|A\cap X|\geq 1
. Thus,
K_{p^{\prime}}=p^{\prime}(Y)\geq p^{\prime}(X)=p(X)-\alpha=K_{p}-\alpha
, where the inequality is because the set
Y
is a
p^{\prime}
-maximizer, and the second equality is because
|A\cap X|\geq 1
and the definition of the function
p^{\prime}
in Step
9
. We now show that
K_{p^{\prime}}\leq K_{p}-\alpha
. If the set
Y
is also a
p
-maximizer, then the claim holds since we have that
K_{p^{\prime}}=p^{\prime}(Y)=p(Y)-\alpha=K_{p}-\alpha
, where the second equality is because the set
A
is a transversal for the family of
p
-maximizers. Thus, we may assume that
Y
is not a
p
-maximizer. If
|A\cap Y|\geq 1
, then we have that

p(X)-\alpha\leq p^{\prime}(Y)=p(Y)-\alpha\leq p(X)-\alpha,

where the first inequality is because we have already shown that
K_{p^{\prime}}\geq K_{p}-\alpha
, the equality is because of
|A\cap Y|\geq 1
and the definition of
p^{\prime}
in Step
9
, and the final inequality is because the set
X
is a
p
-maximizer. Thus, all inequalities are equations and the claim holds. Otherwise, suppose that
|A\cap Y|=0
. Here, we have that

p(X)-\alpha\geq p(X)-\alpha^{(2)}\geq p(X)-\left(p(X)-p(Y)\right)=p(Y)=p^{\prime}(Y),

where the first inequality is because
\alpha\leq\alpha^{(2)}
in Step
5
, the second inequality is by definition of
\alpha^{(2)}
in Step
5
and the fact that the set
X
is a
p
-maximizer, and the final equality is because of
|A\cap Y|=0
and definition of the function
p^{\prime}
in Step
9
.

(c)

Suppose
u\in A
. Then, we have that
m^{\prime}(u)=m(u)-\alpha\leq K_{p}-\alpha=K_{p^{\prime}}
, where the first equality is by definition of
m^{\prime}
in Step
8
and the final equality is by part (b) of the current lemma. Next, suppose
u\in V-A
, implying
m^{\prime}(u)=m(u)
. In particular, we have that
K_{p}-m^{\prime}(u)=K_{p}-m(u)\geq\alpha^{(3)}\geq\alpha
, where the first inequality is by definition of
\alpha^{(3)}
and the second inequality is by definition of
\alpha
in Step
5
. Rearranging the terms, we get that
K_{p^{\prime}}=K_{p}-\alpha\geq m^{\prime}(u)
, where the first equality is again by part (b) of the current lemma.

(d)

Let
X\subseteq V
be arbitrary.
Suppose that
|X\cap A|=0
. Then,
b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(X)=0
and hence
m^{\prime}(X)=m(X)\geq p(X)=p^{\prime}(X)
, so the claim holds. Suppose that
|X\cap A|\geq 1
. Then, we have the following:

m^{\prime}(X)=m(X)-\alpha|X\cap A|\geq p(X)-\alpha=p(X)-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(X)=p^{\prime}(X).

Here, the inequality holds trivially if
|X\cap A|=1
, while for
|X\cap A|\geq 2
it holds because
\alpha\geq\alpha^{(4)}
in Step
5
. The last equality is because
|X\cap A|\geq 1
.

∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a set function, and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
,
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
, and
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron.
Let
\alpha,\alpha^{(5)},\mathcal{Z},p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
. Then, the following hold:

(a)

The function
m^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V-\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function,

(b)

K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=K_{p}-\alpha
,

(c)

m^{\prime\prime}(u)\leq K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
for all
u\in V^{\prime\prime}
,

(d)

m^{\prime\prime}(X)\geq p^{\prime\prime}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V^{\prime\prime}
, and

(e)

either
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=0
or
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\in[\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor,\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil]
; furthermore, if
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}>0
and
\alpha<\alpha^{(5)}
, then we have that

\left\lfloor{(m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor{(m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rfloor\text{ and }\left\lceil{(m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rceil=\left\lceil{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rceil.

Proof.

We note that since
Q(p,m)
is non-empty, we have that
V\not=\emptyset
. Consequently,
Algorithm

2
is in its recursive case. Let
A
,
p^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, and
m^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be as defined by the
Algorithm

2
.
We show that the functions
m^{\prime\prime}
and
p^{\prime\prime}
satisfy each of the properties (a)-(d) below.

(a)

We have that
m^{\prime\prime}(u)=m^{\prime}(u)>0
for every
u\in V-\mathcal{Z}
, where the inequality is by
Lemma

5.3
(a) and the definition of the set
\mathcal{Z}
in Step
6
.

(b)

By
Lemma

5.3
(b), it suffices to show that
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=K_{p^{\prime}}
. Let
X\uplus R_{X}
be a
p^{\prime}
-maximizer, where
R_{X}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
and
X\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
. Furthermore, let
Y\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
be a
p^{\prime\prime}
-maximizer such that
p^{\prime\prime}(Y)=p^{\prime}(Y\uplus R_{Y})
where
R_{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
. Then, we have the following:

K_{p^{\prime}}=p^{\prime}(X\uplus R_{X})\leq\max\left\{p^{\prime}(X\uplus R^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}\right\}=p^{\prime\prime}(X)\leq K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=p^{\prime\prime}(Y)=p^{\prime}(Y\uplus R_{Y})\leq K_{p^{\prime}}.

Thus, all inequalities are equations.

(c)

Let
u\in V-\mathcal{Z}
. Then we have that
m^{\prime\prime}(u)=m^{\prime}(u)\leq K_{p^{\prime}}=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
, where the inequality is by
Lemma

5.3
(c) and the final equality is by
Lemma

5.3
(b) and part (b) of the current lemma.

(d)

Let
X\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
be arbitrary. Let
R\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
such that
p^{\prime\prime}(X)=p^{\prime}(X\uplus R)
. Then, we have that
m^{\prime\prime}(X)=m^{\prime}(X\uplus R)\geq p^{\prime}(X\uplus R)=p^{\prime\prime}(X)
. Here, the first equality is because
R\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
and the inequality is by
Lemma

5.3
(d).

(e)

We note that
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\geq 0
because
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=K_{p}-\alpha\geq K_{p}-\alpha^{(1)}\geq K_{p}-m(u)\geq 0
for every
u\in A
, where the final inequality is because
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
. Suppose that
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}>0
. By
Lemma

5.1
(4), we know that
|A|\in[\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor,\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil]
. First, we consider the case where
m(V)/K_{p}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, we have that
|A|=m(V)/K_{p}
and hence,

\frac{m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}=\frac{m(V)-\alpha|A|}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}=\frac{m(V)-\alpha\frac{m(V)}{K_{p}}}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}=\frac{m(V)-\alpha\frac{m(V)}{K_{p}}}{K_{p}-\alpha}=\frac{m(V)}{K_{p}},

where the third equality is by
Lemma

5.3
(b). Here, we also observe that the second part of the claim holds (regardless of the value of
\alpha
).
Next, suppose that
m(V)/K_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
d,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be positive integers such that
m(V)=d\cdot K_{p}+r
with
r<K_{p}
(such a
d
and
r
exist since
m
is a positive function,
m(Z)\geq p(Z)
for every
Z\subseteq V
, and
m(V)/K_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
). We note that
d=\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor
and
d+1=\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil
and hence, it suffices to show that
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p}\in[d,d+1]
.
We consider two cases. For he first case, suppose that
|A|=\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor
. This implies that
|A|=d
and
\alpha\leq\alpha^{(5)}=K_{p}-r
. Then, we have the following:

\frac{m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}=\frac{m(V)-\alpha|A|}{K_{p}-\alpha}=\frac{dK_{p}+r-\alpha d}{K_{p}-\alpha}\in[d,d+1].

For the second case, suppose that
|A|=\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil
. This implies that
|A|=d+1
and
\alpha\leq\alpha^{(5)}=r
. Then, we have the following:

\frac{m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}=\frac{m(V)-\alpha|A|}{K_{p}-\alpha}=\frac{dK_{p}+r-\alpha(d+1)}{K_{p}-\alpha}\in[d,d+1].

Then, the first part of the claim holds by the final inclusions in both cases above. Furthermore, we observe that in both cases, the final inclusions are in the range
(d,d+1)
if
\alpha<\alpha^{(5)}
. Consequently,
\left\lfloor{m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rfloor=d
and
\left\lceil{m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rceil=d+1
and so the second part of the claim also holds.

∎

We recall that an execution of
Algorithm

2
is feasible if the
Q(p,m)
polyhedron is integral for every recursive call of the algorithm. We now show the main lemma of the section which says that a feasible execution of
Algorithm

2
terminates within a finite number of recursive calls and returns a hypergraph satisfying properties (1)–(4) of
Theorem

1.8
and
Theorem

1.9
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a set function, and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
,
and the execution of
Algorithm

2
for the input tuple
(p,m,J)
is feasible.
Then,
Algorithm

2
terminates in a finite (weakly-polynomial) number of recursive calls. Furthermore, the hypergraph
\left(H=\left(V,E\right),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)
returned by
Algorithm

2

satisfies the following properties:

(1)

b_{(H,w)}(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
,

(2)

b_{(H,w)}(u)=m(u)
for all
u\in V
,

(3)

\sum_{e\in E}w(e)=K_{p}
, and

(4)

if
K_{p}>0
, then
|e|\in[\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor,\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil]
for all
e\in E
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction on the potential function
\phi(m)\coloneqq m(V)
. We note that
\phi(m)\geq 0
since
m
is a positive function. For the base case of induction, suppose that
\phi(m)=0
. Then, we have that
V=\emptyset
since
m
is a positive function. Consequently,
Algorithm

2
is in its base case (Step
1
) and terminates.
Moreover, Step
1
returns an empty hypergraph which satisfies properties (1)-(4) and so the lemma holds. Here, we note that
K_{p}\leq 0
since otherwise, we have that
0=m(\emptyset)\geq p(\emptyset)>0
, a contradiction.

For the inductive case, suppose that
\phi(m)>0
. We note that since
m
is a positive function, we have that
V\not=\emptyset
and
K_{p}\geq m(u)>0
for every
u\in V
. Consequently,
Algorithm

2
is in its recursive case. Since the execution of
Algorithm

2
is feasible for the input tuple
(p,m,J)
, we have that
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron. By
Lemma

5.4
(a), (c) and (d), the tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime})
constructed by
Algorithm

2
satisfy the hypothesis of the current lemma. We note that the tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime})
is the input to the subsequent recursive call of
Algorithm

2
by Step
10
. Moreover,
\phi(m^{\prime\prime})<\phi(m)
by
Lemma

5.2
(b). Thus, by induction, the subsequent recursive call to
Algorithm

2
terminates and returns a hypergraph
(H^{\prime\prime}=(V^{\prime\prime},E^{\prime\prime}),w^{\prime\prime})
satisfying properties (1)-(4) for the tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime})
. Consequently, the entire execution of
Algorithm

2
terminates within a finite number of recursive calls. Let
(H=(V,E),w)
be the hypergraph returned by Step
10
and let
(H_{0},w_{0}),\mathcal{Z},A,\alpha
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
for the input tuple
(p,m,J)
. We first show that the hypergraph
(H,w)
satisfies property (1). Let
X\subseteq V
be arbitrary. Then, we have that

\displaystyle b_{(H,w)}(X)

\displaystyle=b_{(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})}(X-\mathcal{Z})+b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(X)

\displaystyle\geq p^{\prime\prime}(X-\mathcal{Z})+b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(X)

\displaystyle\geq p^{\prime}(X)+b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(X)

\displaystyle=p(X).

Here, the first inequality holds by the inductive hypothesis property (1) and the second inequality is because
p^{\prime\prime}={p^{\prime}}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
by Step
9
.
Next, we show that hypergraph
(H,w)
satisfies property (2). Let
u\in V
be arbitrary. Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle b_{(H,w)}(u)

\displaystyle=b_{(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})}(u)+b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(u)

\displaystyle=b_{(H^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime\prime})}(u)+\alpha\chi_{A}(u)

\displaystyle=m^{\prime\prime}(u)+\alpha\chi_{A}(u)

\displaystyle=m(u).

Here, the third equality is by the inductive hypothesis property (2). Next, we show that the hypergraph
(H,w)
satisfies property (3). We have the following:

\sum_{e\in E}w(e)=\sum_{e\in E^{\prime\prime}}w(e)+w(A)=\sum_{e\in E^{\prime\prime}}w^{\prime\prime}(e)+\alpha=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}+\alpha=K_{p}.

Here, the third equality is by induction hypothesis property (3), and the final equality is by
Lemma

5.4
(b).
Next, we show that property (4) holds. Let
e\in E
be an arbitrary hyperedge. We consider two cases.
First, suppose that
e\in E-E^{\prime\prime}
. Then, we have that
e=A
by Steps
7
,
11
and
12
. Consequently, we have that
|e|=|A|\in[\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor,\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil]
by
Lemma

5.1
(4). Alternatively, suppose that
e\in E^{\prime\prime}
. We note that
|E^{\prime\prime}|>0
implies that
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}>0
by property (3) of the inductive hypothesis. Moreover,
|e|\in[\lfloor m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\rfloor,\lceil m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\rceil]
by the induction hypothesis property (4). By
Lemma

5.4
(e), we have that
m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\in[\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor,\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil]
. Thus, the interval
[\lfloor m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\rfloor,\lceil m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\rceil]
is contained in the interval
[\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor,\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil]
and so property (4) holds.
∎

We conclude the section by showing two properties of
Algorithm

2
which we will leverage in subsequent sections while analyzing the recursion depth and runtime of the algorithm. The first lemma below shows that any feasible execution of
Algorithm

2
can witness
\alpha=\alpha^{(5)}
in at most one recursive call. The second lemma below shows that the set
A
chosen during a feasible recursive call of
Algorithm

2
is not feasible for the subsequent recursive call.
As a consequence, the value
\alpha
computed in Step
5
during a feasible recursive call of
Algorithm

2
is the maximum value such that the input tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime})
to the subsequent recursive call in Step
10
satisfy the hypothesis of
Lemma

5.5
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a set function, and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
,
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
, and
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron.
Let
A,\alpha^{(5)},\alpha,\mathcal{Z},p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
. If
\alpha=\alpha^{(5)}
and
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}>0
, then
m(V)/K_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
,
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\neq|A|
.

Proof.

We note that since
Q(p,m)
is non-empty, we have that
V\not=\emptyset
and consequently
Algorithm

2
is in its recursive case. Since
\alpha=\alpha^{(5)}
, it follows that
\alpha^{(5)}<\infty
(because
\alpha^{(5)}=\alpha\leq\alpha^{(1)}\leq\max_{u\in V}m(u)<\infty
, where final inequality is because the range of
m
is
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
). and hence,
m(V)/K_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
by definition of
\alpha^{(5)}
in Step
5
. Next, we prove the conclusions about
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
. Let
d,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be positive integers such that
m(V)=d\cdot K_{p}+r
with
r<K_{p}
(such a
d
and
r
exist since
m
is a positive function,
m(Z)\geq p(Z)
for every
Z\subseteq V
, and
m(V)/K_{p}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
). We note that
d=\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor
and
d+1=\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil
.
We consider two cases: Firstly, suppose that
|A|=\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor
. It implies that
|A|=d
and
\alpha=\alpha^{(5)}=K_{p}-r
. Then, we have the following:

\frac{m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}=\frac{m(V)-\alpha|A|}{K_{p}-\alpha}=\frac{dK_{p}+r-(K_{p}-r)d}{K_{p}-K_{p}-r}=d+1,

and hence,
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\neq|A|
. In the above, the first equality is by definition of
m^{\prime\prime}
in Step
8
and by
Lemma

5.4
(b).
Next, suppose that
|A|=\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil
. It implies that
|A|=d+1
and
\alpha=\alpha^{(5)}=r
. Then, we have the following:

\frac{m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}=\frac{m(V)-\alpha|A|}{K_{p}-\alpha}=\frac{dK_{p}+r-r(d+1)}{K_{p}-r}=d,

and hence,
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\neq|A|
. In the above, the first equality is by definition of
m^{\prime\prime}
in Step
8
and by
Lemma

5.4
(b).

∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a set function, and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for all
X\subseteq V
,
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for all
u\in V
, and
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron.
Let
A,\mathcal{Z},p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
. If
A\cap\mathcal{Z}=\emptyset
and
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}>0
, then the indicator vector
\chi_{A}\in\{0,1\}^{V-\mathcal{Z}}
is not in
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
.

Proof.

We note that since
Q(p,m)
is non-empty, we have that
V\not=\emptyset
and consequently
Algorithm

2
is in its recursive case. By way of contradiction, suppose that
A\cap\mathcal{Z}=\emptyset
and the indicator vector
\chi_{A}\in\{0,1\}^{V-\mathcal{Z}}
is in
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. Consequently, the set
A
satisfies the properties
Lemma

5.1
(1)-(4) with respect to the functions
p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
.
We recall the definition of
\alpha
below:

\alpha\coloneqq\min\begin{cases}\alpha^{(1)}\coloneqq\min\left\{m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in A\right\}\\
\alpha^{(2)}\coloneqq\min\left\{K_{p}-p(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\subseteq V-A\right\}\\
\alpha^{(3)}\coloneqq\min\left\{K_{p}-m(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in V-A\right\}\\
\alpha^{(4)}\coloneqq\min\left\{\left\lfloor{\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}}\right\rfloor\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|A\cap X|\geq 2\right\}\\
\alpha^{(5)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m(V)\mod K_{p}&\text{if $|A|=\left\lceil{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rceil>m(V)/K_{p}$,}\\
K_{p}-(m(V)\mod K_{p})&\text{if $|A|=\left\lfloor{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rfloor<m(V)/K_{p}$,}\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}\end{cases}

First, suppose that
\alpha=\alpha^{(1)}
. Let
u\in A
be a vertex such that
\alpha=m(u)
. Then, we have that
u\in\mathcal{Z}\not=\emptyset
. Thus, we have that
u\in A\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}\subseteq V-\{u\}
, a contradiction.

Next, suppose that
\alpha=\alpha^{(2)}
. Let
X\subseteq V-A
be a set such that
\alpha=K_{p}-p(X)
. Then, we have that

p^{\prime\prime}(X)=p(X)=K_{p}-\alpha=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}.

Here, the first equality is because
X\subseteq V-A
and the final equality is by

Lemma

5.4
(b). Thus, the set
X
is a
p^{\prime\prime}
-maximizer and hence
X\cap A\not=\emptyset
since the set
A
satisfies
Lemma

5.1
(1) with respect to the functions
p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
,
a contradiction to
X\subseteq V-A
.

Next, suppose
\alpha=\alpha^{(3)}
. Let
u\in V-A
be a vertex such that
\alpha=K_{p}-m(u)
. Then, we have that
m^{\prime\prime}(u)=m(u)=K_{p}-\alpha=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
. Here, the first equality is because
u\in V-A
, and the final equality is by
Lemma

5.4
(b). Thus,
m^{\prime\prime}(u)=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
and consequently
u\in A
since the set
A
satisfies
Lemma

5.1
(2) with respect to the functions
p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
, a contradiction to
u\in V-A
.

Next, suppose that
\alpha=\alpha^{(4)}
. Let
X\subseteq V
be such that
|A\cap X|\geq 2
and
\alpha=\left\lfloor{\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}}\right\rfloor
. First, we consider the case where
\alpha=\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}
.
Then, we have that

m^{\prime\prime}(X)=m(X)-\alpha|A\cap X|=p(X)-\alpha=p^{\prime\prime}(X)
, where the first and final equalities are because
|A\cap X|\geq 1
. This gives us the required contradiction as follows:
p^{\prime\prime}(X)-1\leq m^{\prime\prime}(X)-|A\cap X|=p^{\prime\prime}(X)-|A\cap X|<p^{\prime\prime}(X)-1
, a contradiction. Here, the first inequality is because the set
A
satisfies
Lemma

5.1
(3) with respect to the functions
p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
and the final inequality is because
|A\cap X|\geq 2
.
Next, we consider the case where
\alpha<\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}
. Then, it follows that
\alpha+1>\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}
. Rewriting this shows that
p(X)-\alpha+|A\cap X|-1>m(X)-\alpha|A\cap X|
. Consequently,
m^{\prime\prime}(X)=m(X)-\alpha|A\cap X|<p(X)-\alpha+|A\cap X|-1=p^{\prime\prime}(X)+|A\cap X|-1
. This gives us the required contradiction as follows:
p^{\prime\prime}(X)-1\leq m^{\prime\prime}(X)-|A\cap X|<p^{\prime\prime}(X)-1
, a contradiction. Here, the first inequality is because the set
A
satisfies
Lemma

5.1
(3) with respect to the functions
p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
.

Next, suppose that
\alpha=\alpha^{(5)}
. By
Lemma

5.6
, we have that
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
is an integer and
m^{\prime\prime}(V-\mathcal{Z})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\neq|A|
. This contradicts
Lemma

5.1
(4) with respect to the functions
p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

5.3
Number of Recursive Calls of
Algorithm

2
with
\alpha\in\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(5)}\}

In this section, we bound the number of recursive calls of
Algorithm

2
that witness
\alpha\in\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(5)}\}
. The main result of this section is
Corollary

5.1
which we show at the end of the section.

Notation.

We first set up some convenient notation that will be used in the remainder of this section as well as in subsequent sections.
By
Lemma

5.5
, the number of recursive calls made by the algorithm is finite.
We will use
\ell
to denote the depth of recursion of the algorithm. We will refer to the recursive call at depth
i
as
recursive call
i
or the
i^{th}
recursive call
. Let
V_{i}
denote the ground set at the start of recursive call
i
, and
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
,
m_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and
J_{i}\subseteq V_{i}
denote the inputs to recursive call
i
.
Furthermore, let
A_{i},\alpha_{i},\alpha^{(1)}_{i},\alpha^{(2)}_{i},\alpha^{(3)}_{i},\alpha^{(4)}_{i},\alpha^{(5)}_{i},\mathcal{Z}_{i},p^{\prime}_{i},m^{\prime}_{i}
denote the quantities
A,\alpha,\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(4)},\alpha^{(5)},\mathcal{Z},p^{\prime},m^{\prime}
defined by
Algorithm

2
at recursive call
i
. For notational convenience, we define
\mathcal{Z}_{\ell},{\mathcal{D}}_{\ell}=\emptyset
.
Finally, let

\left(H_{i}=\left(V_{i},E_{i}\right),w_{i}\right)
denote the hypergraph returned by recursive call
i
.

We note that
Lemma

5.4
and induction on the recursion depth
i
immediately imply the following lemma which says that for every
i\in[\ell]
, the input tuple
(p_{i},m_{i},J_{i})
satisfies the hypothesis of
Lemma

5.5
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Consider a feasible execution of
Algorithm

2
with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
recursive calls. For every
i\in[\ell]
, suppose that the input to the
i^{th}
recursive call
of
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p_{i},m_{i},J_{i})
, where
J_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
is an arbitrary set, and the functions
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and

m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}

are such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for every
u\in V_{1}
. Then, for every
i\in[\ell]
, we have that the function
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and positive function
m_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
satisfy
m_{i}(X)\geq p_{i}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V_{i}
and
m_{i}(u)\leq K_{p_{i}}
for all
u\in V_{i}
.

Set Families.

We now define certain set families that will be useful in the analysis. We recall that
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\{u\in V_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m_{i}^{\prime}(u)=0\}
. We let the family
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\leq i}\coloneqq\cup_{j\in[i]}\mathcal{Z}_{i}
.
We also let
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\coloneqq\{u\in V_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m_{i}(u)=K_{p_{i}}\}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{u\in V_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m_{i}^{\prime}(u)=K_{p^{\prime}_{i}}\}
.
We use
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
to denote the families of
minimal

p_{i}
-maximizers.
Lemma

5.9
below shows the progression of minimal
p_{i}
-maximizer families across recursive calls of an execution of
Algorithm

2
. We will also be interested in families of all minimal
maximizers of the input functions witnessed by the algorithm up to a given recursive call. Formally, we
define the family

{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\coloneqq\cup_{j\in[i]}{\mathcal{F}}_{j}
.

Lemma

5.10
below summarizes the properties of the stated families that will help in subsequent analysis.

Lemma 5.9
.

Consider a feasible execution of
Algorithm

2
with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
recursive calls. For every
i\in[\ell]
, suppose that the input to the
i^{th}
recursive call
of
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p_{i},m_{i},J_{i})
, where
J_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
is an arbitrary set, and the functions
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and

m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}

are such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for every
u\in V_{1}
. Then, for every
i\in[\ell-1]
we have the following:

(a)

if
Y\subseteq V_{i}
is a
p_{i}
-maximizer, then
Y
is also a
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizer, and

(b)

if
Y\subseteq V_{i}
is a
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizer such that
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i}\not=\emptyset
, then
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i}
is a
p_{i+1}
-maximizer.

Proof.

By
Lemma

5.4
(a), (c), and (d), we have that for every
i\in[\ell]
, the function
m_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a positive function, and the functions
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and

m_{i}

are such that
m_{i}(X)\geq p_{i}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V_{i}
and
m_{i}(u)\leq K_{p_{i}}
for every
u\in V_{i}
. We prove both claims below for a fixed
i\in[\ell]
.

(a)

Let
Y\subseteq V_{i}
be a
p_{i}
-maximizer. By
Lemma

5.1
(1), the set
A_{i}
is a transversal for the family of
p_{i}
-maximizers. Thus, we have that
p_{i}^{\prime}(Y)=p_{i}(Y)-\alpha_{i}=K_{p_{i}}-\alpha_{i}=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}
, where the first equality is because
A_{i}\cap Y\not=\emptyset
and the third equality is by
Lemma

5.3
(b).

(b)

Let
Y\subseteq V_{i}
be a
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizer such that
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
We have the following:

K_{p_{i+1}}\geq p_{i+1}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i})=\max\{p_{i}^{\prime}(Y\cup R)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{i}\}\geq p_{i}^{\prime}(Y)=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}=K_{p_{i+1}},

where the final equality is by
Lemma

5.4
(b). Thus, all inequalities are equations and we have that
p_{i+1}(Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i})=K_{p_{i+1}}
.

∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Consider a feasible execution of
Algorithm

2
with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
recursive calls. For every
i\in[\ell]
, suppose that the input to the
i^{th}
recursive call
of
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p_{i},m_{i},J_{i})
, where
J_{1}\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set, and the functions
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and

m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}

are such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for every
u\in V_{1}
. Then, for every
i\in[\ell-1]
we have the following:

(a)

\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i+1}
; furthermore,
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
if and only if
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\not=\emptyset
(i.e.,
\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i}\subsetneq\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i+1}
),

(b)

{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}
; furthermore, if
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(2)}<\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
, then
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\subsetneq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}
,

(c)

{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}
; furthermore, if
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(3)}
, then
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subsetneq{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
, and

(d)

if
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\emptyset
, then
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}={\mathcal{F}}_{i+1}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

5.4
(a), (c), and (d), we have that for every
i\in[\ell]
, the function
m_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a positive function, and the functions
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
m_{i}
are such that
m_{i}(X)\geq p_{i}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V_{i}
and
m_{i}(u)\leq K_{p_{i}}
for every
u\in V_{i}
. We prove each claim separately below.

(a)

We have that
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\leq i}\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}_{\leq i+1}
by definition. We now show the second part of the claim. For the forward direction, suppose that
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
. Let
u\in A_{i}
be a vertex such that
m_{i}(u)=\alpha_{i}
(such a vertex exists since
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
). Then, we have that
m_{i}^{\prime}(u)=m_{i}(u)-\alpha_{i}\chi_{A_{i}}(u)=0
. Thus,
u\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}
. The reverse direction of the claim follows because the function
m
is a positive function.

(b)

We note that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}
follows by definition. We now show the second part of the claim. Suppose that
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(2)}<\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
. Then by part (a) of the current lemma, we have that
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\emptyset
, and consequently
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i+1}
by the definition of the two functions.
We consider the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}
of minimal
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizers. By
Lemma

5.9
(a), we have that
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}={\mathcal{F}}_{i+1}
, and thus,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\cup{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}
, where the equalities in both the previous expressions are because
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i+1}
. We now show that the first inclusion is strict. For convenience, we let
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}^{\prime}\coloneqq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\cup{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}
.
By way of contradiction, suppose that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}^{\prime}
.
Let
X\subseteq V_{i}-A_{i}
be a set such that
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(2)}=K_{p_{i}}-p_{i}(X)
(such a set exists since
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(2)}
). Then, we have that
p_{i}^{\prime}(X)=p_{i}(X)=K_{p_{i}}-\alpha_{i}^{(2)}=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}.

Here, the first equality is because
X\subseteq V_{i}-A_{i}
, the second equality is because our choice of
X
satisfies
\alpha_{i}^{(2)}=K_{p_{i}}-p_{i}(X)
, and the final inequality is by
Lemma

5.3
(b). Thus, the set
X
is a
p_{i}^{\prime}
-maximizer. Furthermore, the set
X
is not a
p_{i}
-maximizer since the set
A_{i}
is a transversal for the family of
p_{i}
-maximizers by
Lemma

5.1
(1), but
A_{i}\cap X=\emptyset
.
Consequently, there exists a set
Y\subseteq X
such that
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}-{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}^{\prime}
, we have that
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i-1}
. In particular, there exists a recursive call
j\in[i-1]
such that
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{j}
. Since
Y\subseteq V_{i}
, by
Lemma

5.9
(a), (b) and induction on
j
, we have that
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
. Thus, the set
Y
is a
p_{i}
-maximizer and consequently
A_{i}\cap Y\not=\emptyset
by
Lemma

5.1
(1), a contradiction to
A_{i}\cap X=\emptyset
.

(c)

First, we show that
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
for every
i\in[\ell]
. Let
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
. Then we have the following:

m_{i}^{\prime}(u)=m_{i}(u)-\alpha_{i}\chi_{A_{i}}(u)=K_{p_{i}}-\alpha_{i}\chi_{A_{i}}(u)=K_{p_{i}}-\alpha_{i}=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}.

Here, the second equality is because
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
, the third equality is because
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
by
Lemma

5.1
(2), and the final equality is by
Lemma

5.3
(b). Thus,
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
and we have that
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
. Next, we show that
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}
. Let
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
. We note that if
u\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}
, then we have that
0=m_{i}^{\prime}(u)=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}
, and thus
i=\ell
, contradicting
i\in[1,\ell-1]
. Thus,
u\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{i}
, i.e.
u\in V_{i+1}
. Then, we have that
m_{i+1}(u)=m_{i}^{\prime}(u)=K_{p_{i}^{\prime}}=K_{p_{i+1}}
, where the final equality is by
Lemma

5.4
(b). Thus,
u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}
and we have that
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}
.

We now show the second part of the claim. Suppose that
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(3)}
and let
v\in V_{i}-A_{i}
be a vertex such that
\alpha_{i}=K_{p_{i}}-m_{i}(v)
(such a vertex exists since
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(3)}
). Then, we have that:

m_{i}^{\prime}(v)=m_{i}(v)-\alpha_{i}\chi_{A_{i}}(v)=m_{i}(v)=K_{p_{i}}-\alpha_{i}=K_{p_{i+1}}.

Here, the second and third equalities are by the choice of
v
and the final equality is by
Lemma

5.3
(b). Thus,
v\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i+1}\backslash{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
.

(d)

We note that since
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\emptyset
, the function
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i+1}
. Consequently,
{\mathcal{F}}_{i+1}={\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}^{\prime}
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}
, we have that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}^{\prime}
. By
Lemma

5.9
(a), we have that
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}
. Thus, it suffices to show the reverse inclusion
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{i}

By way of contradiction, let
X\in{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}-{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\not=\emptyset
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}^{\prime}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}
, we have that
X\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}
. By definition of the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}
and our choice of the set
X
, there exists
j\in[i-1]
and
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{j}\cup{\mathcal{F}}_{j}^{\prime}
such that
Y\cap V_{i}=X
. By
Lemma

5.9
(a), (b) and induction on
j
, we have that
X\in{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
, a contradiction to the choice of set
X
.

∎

We now show the main result of the section which says that the number of recursive calls of a feasible execution of
Algorithm

2
for which
\alpha\in\left\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(5)}\right\}
is at most
|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq\ell}|+2|V|+1
. We derive this as a consequence of
Lemma

5.10
.

Corollary 5.1
.

Consider a feasible execution of
Algorithm

2
with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
recursive calls. Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p_{1},m_{1},J_{1})
, where
J_{1}\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set, and the functions
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
are such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for every
u\in V_{1}
.
Then, we have that

\left|\left\{i\in[\ell-1]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\alpha_{i}\in\left\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\alpha_{i}^{(5)}\right\}\right\}\right|\leq\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq\ell}\right|+2|V|+1.

Proof.

We first show that the number of iterations
i
for which
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(5)}
is at most one. Let
i\in[\ell-1]
be the least index such that
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(5)}
. Then, by
Lemma

5.6
, we have that
m_{i}(V_{i})/K_{p_{i}}\not\in Z
and one of the following holds: either
K_{p_{i+1}}=0
or
m_{i+1}(V_{i+1})/K_{p_{i+1}}\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
K_{p_{i+1}}=0
, then
i=\ell-1
and this is the only iteration where
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(5)}
. If
m_{i+1}(V_{i+1})/K_{p_{i+1}}\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
\alpha_{j}^{(5)}=\infty
for every
j\geq i+1
by definition and consequently,
\alpha\neq\alpha_{j}^{(5)}
for every
j\geq i+1
.

By the above arguments, it suffices to show that the number of iterations
i
for which
\alpha_{i}\in\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)}\}
is at most
\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq\ell}\right|+2|V|
. For this, we define a potential function
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[\ell]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
as follows: for every
i\in[\ell]
,

\phi(i)\coloneqq|\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i}|+|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}|+|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}|.

By
Lemma

5.10
, we have that the function
\phi
is non-decreasing as each of the three terms of
\phi
are non-decreasing. By
Lemma

5.10
, we also have that if
\alpha_{i}\in\left\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)}\right\}
, then
\phi(i)<\phi(i+1)
. In particular, we have that the number of recursive calls of the algorithm with
\alpha\in\left\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)}\right\}
is at most
\phi(\ell)-\phi(0)\leq|\mathcal{Z}_{\leq\ell}|+|{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}|+|{\mathcal{D}}_{\ell}|\leq 2|V|+|{\mathcal{F}}_{{\leq\ell}}|
.
∎

5.4
Feasibility of Execution for Skew-supermodular Functions

In this section, we show that the execution of
Algorithm

2
is feasible under a skew-supermodularity assumption on the input function. We recall that a call to
Algorithm

2
is feasible if the input functions
p
and
m
are such that the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
is non-empty and integral. Moreover, the execution of
Algorithm

2
is feasible if for every recursive call to the algorithm during its execution, either
Algorithm

2
is in its base case or recursive call is feasible Bernáth and Király showed the following result.

Lemma 5.11

(
[
8
]
)
.

Let
q,r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be skew-supermodular functions and

p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the function defined as
p(X)\coloneqq\max\{q(X),r(X)\}
for every
X\subseteq V
. Furthermore, let
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a non-negative function satisfying
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
. Then, the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron.

Using
Lemma

5.11
in conjunction with
Lemma

5.8
leads to the following corollary showing feasibility of the execution of
Algorithm

2
.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
q,r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be skew-supermodular functions.
Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a function defined by
p(X)\coloneqq\max\{q(X),r(X)\}
for every
X\subseteq V
with
K_{p}>0
and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive integer-valued function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
. Let
\mathcal{Z},(H_{0},w_{0}),p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
. Then,
the input tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime})
for the subsequent recursive call in the execution of the algorithm either has
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=0
or satisfies the following properties:

(1)

the functions
q^{\prime\prime}
and
r^{\prime\prime}
defined by

q^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq{(q-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})})}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
and
r^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq{(r-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})})}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
, where
(H_{0},w_{0}),\mathcal{Z}
are as defined by
Algorithm

2
,
are skew-supermodular; furthermore
p^{\prime\prime}(X)=\max\{q^{\prime\prime}(X),r^{\prime\prime}(X)\}
for every
X\subseteq V
,

(2)

m^{\prime\prime}
is a positive integer-valued function,

(3)

m^{\prime\prime}(X)\geq p^{\prime\prime}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,

(4)

m^{\prime\prime}(u)\leq K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
for every
u\in V^{\prime\prime}
, and

(5)

Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
is a non-empty integral polyhedron.

In particular, the execution of
Algorithm

2
on the input tuple
(p,m,J)
is a feasible execution with finite recursion depth.

Proof.

We note that the function
b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}
is submodular, and consequently, the functions
q^{\prime\prime}
and
r^{\prime\prime}
are skew-supermodular by definition. Let
V^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=V-\mathcal{Z}
. Consider a set
X\subseteq V^{\prime\prime}
. Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle p^{\prime\prime}(X)

\displaystyle={(p-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})})}/_{\mathcal{Z}}(X)

\displaystyle={\left(\max\{q,r\}-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}\right)}/_{\mathcal{Z}}(X)

\displaystyle={\left(\max\{q-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})},r-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}\}\right)}/_{\mathcal{Z}}(X)

\displaystyle=\max\{q^{\prime\prime}(X),r^{\prime\prime}(X)\},

where the first equality is by Step
9
.
Thus, property (1) of the claim holds. Furthermore, properties (2), (3) and (4) of the claim hold by
Lemma

5.4
. Finally, property (5) of the claim holds by
Lemma

5.11
and the previously shown properties (2), (3) and (4) of the claim.

We now show that the execution of
Algorithm

2
on the input tuple
(p,m,J)
is a feasible execution with finite recursion depth using (reverse) induction on the value
K_{p}
. For the base case, suppose that
K_{p}=0
. Then, we have that
V=\emptyset
since
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function with
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
. Thus, Step
1
returns the empty hypergraph and the claim holds. For the induction step, suppose that
K_{p}>0
.
We recall that
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=K_{p}-\alpha\leq K_{p}-1
, where the equality is by
Lemma

5.4
and the inequality is by
Lemma

5.2
. By the inductive hypothesis, we have that the execution of
Algorithm

2
on the input tuple
(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime})
, where
J^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime\prime}
is the set defined by
Algorithm

2
, is a feasible execution with finite recursion depth. Furthermore, by
Lemma

5.11
,
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron. Consequently the execution of
Algorithm

2
on the input tuple
(p,m,J)
is also feasible execution with finite recursion depth.
∎

Lemma

5.11
implies the following for skew-supermodular functions.

Lemma 5.12

(
[
8
]
)
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a skew-supermodular function and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a non-negative function satisfying
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
. Then, the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron.

Using
Lemma

5.12
in conjunction with
Lemma

5.8
leads to the following corollary (similar to the proof of
Corollary

5.2
).

Corollary 5.3
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function with
K_{p}>0
, and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive integer-valued function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
. Then,
the input
(p^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V^{\prime\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},m^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},J^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime\prime})
for every recursive call in the execution of the algorithm either has
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=0
or satisfies the following properties:

(1)

p^{\prime\prime}
is skew-supermodular,

(2)

m^{\prime\prime}
is a positive integer-valued function,

(3)

m^{\prime\prime}(X)\geq p^{\prime\prime}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,

(4)

m^{\prime\prime}(u)\leq K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
for every
u\in V^{\prime\prime}
, and

(5)

Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
is a non-empty integral polyhedron.

In particular, the execution of the algorithm is a feasible execution with finite recursion depth.

5.5
Run-time for Skew-supermodular Functions

In this section, we bound the run-time of
Algorithm

2
for skew-supermodular functions assuming a bound on the recursion depth of the algorithm.
Lemma

5.16
and
Lemma

5.17
are the main lemmas of this section.

In order to show that each recursive call of
Algorithm

2
can be implemented in polynomial time, we will require the ability to solve certain optimization problems in polynomial time.
In the next two lemmas, we summarize these optimization problems and show that they can be solved in polynomial time. The proofs of these lemmas use combinations of several known tools in submodular minimization.
We note that Lemma
5.14
below does not require the input function
p
to be skew-supermodular while
Lemma

5.13
below requires the input functions
q
and
r
to be skew-supermodular.

Lemma

5.13
concerns optimizing over the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
.
We note that
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
is the separation oracle for constraint (iv) of the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
. Consequently, given access to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, we can optimize over the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
in
weakly
polynomial time using the ellipsoid algorithm.
The following lemma shows that given access to
{q}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
and
{r}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where functions
q,r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
are
skew-supermodular
, we can optimize over the Q-polyhedron associated with the function
p
defined as
p(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max\{q(X),r(X)\}
for every
X\subseteq V
in
strongly
polynomial time. We defer the proof of the lemma to
Section

B.2
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
q,r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be skew-supermodular functions
and let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the function defined as
p(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max\{q(X),r(X)\}
for every
X\subseteq V
.
Then, the following optimization problem can be solved in
\poly(|V|)
time using
\poly(|V|)
queries to
{q}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
and
{r}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
: for a given non-negative function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
Q(p,m)\neq\emptyset
and a given cost vector
c\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
, find an extreme point optimum solution to the following linear program:

\max\left\{\sum_{u\in V}c_{u}x_{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x\in Q(p,m)\right\}.

Lemma

5.14
defines a ratio-maximization problem and shows that it can be solved in strongly polynomial time using polynomial number of maximization-oracle queries. This will be helpful in computing the value of
\alpha^{(4)}
in
Algorithm

2
Step
5
. We defer the proof of the lemma to
Section

B.3
.

Lemma 5.14
.

For a function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, the following optimization problem can be solved in
\poly(|V|)
time using
\poly(|V|)
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
: for a given vector
y\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
and a given set
A\subseteq V
, compute a set
Z
satisfying
|A\cap Z|\geq 2
that maximizes
(p(Z)-y(Z))/(|A\cap Z|-1)
i.e.,

\arg\max\left\{\frac{p(Z)-y(Z)}{|A\cap Z|-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|A\cap Z|\geq 2\right\}.

The next lemma shows how the function input to an arbitrary recursive call of
Algorithm

2
is related to the function input to the first recursive call. We will use this expression in proving the main lemma of the section. The proof of the lemma follows by induction on the recursion depth and is exactly the same as that of
Lemma

4.8
. We omit the proof for brevity.

Lemma 5.15
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p_{1},m_{1})
, where
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
are functions such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for all
X\subseteq V_{1}
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for all
u\in V_{1}
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be the number of recursive calls witnessed by the execution of
Algorithm

2
. For
i\in[\ell]
, let
(p_{i},m_{i})
be the input tuple to the
i^{th}
recursive call; moreover, for
i\in[\ell-1]
, let
\mathcal{Z}_{i},(H^{i}_{0},w^{i}_{0})
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
for the input
(p_{i},m_{i})
.
Then, for each
i\in[\ell]
we have that

p_{i}={\left(p_{1}-\sum_{j\in[i-1]}b_{(\tilde{H}_{j},\tilde{w}_{j})}\right)}/_{\bigcup_{j\in[i-1]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}},

where
(\tilde{H}_{0}^{i},\tilde{w}_{0}^{i})
denotes the hypergraph obtained by adding the vertices
\cup_{j\in[i-1]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}
to the hypergraph
(H^{i}_{0},w^{i}_{0})
.

We now bound the run-time of
Algorithm

2
in terms of its recursion depth. In subsequent sections, we will get strongly polynomial bounds on the recursion depth of the
Algorithm

2
when the input function
p
is skew-supermodular (or is defined to be the maximum of two skew-supermodular functions). Then,
Lemma

5.16
(or respectively,
Lemma

5.17
) below will imply an overall strongly polynomial runtime for
Algorithm

2
, given access to the appropriate function maximization oracle for
p
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2

is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is a set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function, and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive integer-valued function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
denote the recursion depth of
Algorithm

2
on the input tuple
(p,m,J)
is
\ell
. Then,
Algorithm

2
can be implemented to run in time poly
(|V|,\ell)
using poly
(|V|,\ell)
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. The run-time includes the time to construct the hypergraphs used as input to the queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Moreover, for each query to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
|V|
vertices and
O(\ell)
hyperedges.

Proof.

By
Corollary

5.3
, the execution of
Algorithm

2
is a feasible execution and
\ell
is finite.
For
i\in[\ell]
, let the tuple
(p_{i},m_{i},J_{i})
denote the input to the
i^{th}
recursive call – we note that
(p_{1},m_{1},J_{1})=(p,m,J)
.
The next claim shows that all steps of
Algorithm

2
except for Steps
9
and
10
in the
i^{th}
recursive call can be implemented to run in
\poly(|V|,\ell)
time and
\poly(|V|)
number of queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.

Claim 5.1
.

All steps except for Steps
9
and
10
in the
i^{th}
recursive call can be implemented to run in
\poly(|V_{i}|)+O(\ell)
time using
\poly(|V_{i}|)
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. The run-time includes the time to construct the inputs for the queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.

Proof.

Step
3
can be implemented in
\poly(|V_{i}|)
time using
\poly(|V_{i}|)
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
since we can find an extreme point optimum solution
y
to the LP
\max\{\sum_{u\in J_{i}}y_{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y\in Q(p_{i},m_{i})\}
in
\poly(|V_{i}|)
time using
\poly(|V_{i}|)
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
by
Lemma

5.13
.
Step
4
can be implemented in
O(|V_{i}|)
time by explicitly checking
y_{u}>0
for every
u\in V_{i}
.
Next, we show that Step
5
can be implemented in poly
(|V_{i}|)
time using poly
(|V_{i}|)
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.
We note that the value
K_{p_{i}}
can be computed by querying
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\emptyset,T_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\emptyset,y_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{0\}^{V_{i}})
. Then, the values
\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
,
\alpha^{(3)}
,
\alpha^{(3)}
and
\alpha^{(5)}
can be computed in
O(|V_{i}|)
time as follows:
\alpha_{i}^{(1)}
and
\alpha_{i}^{(3)}
can be computed by iterating over all vertices in
V_{i}
,
\alpha_{i}^{(2)}
can be computed by the single query
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\emptyset,T_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=A,y_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{0\}^{V_{i}})
, and
\alpha^{(5)}
can be computed by checking the relevant conditions. Moreover,
\alpha^{(4)}
can be computed in
\poly(|V_{i}|)
time using
\poly(|V_{i}|)
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
by
Lemma

5.14
. Consequently, Step
5
can be implemented in in
\poly(|V_{i}|)
time using
\poly(|V_{i}|)
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Steps
6
,
7
and
8
can be implemented in
O(|V_{i}|)
time by iterating over
V_{i}
. We note that the number of distinct hyperedges in the hypergraph obtained from recursion (Step
10
) is at most
\ell-i
since each recursive call adds at most one distinct hyperedge to the returned. Thus, Steps
11
and
12
can be implemented in
O(|V|+\ell)
time.
∎

Next, we focus on the time to implement a query to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.

Claim 5.2
.

For disjoint subsets
S,T\subseteq V_{i}
and a vector
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{V_{i}}
, the answer to the query
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S,T_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=T,y_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=y)
can be computed in
O(|V_{1}|(|V_{1}|+\ell))
time using at most
|V_{1}|+1
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. The run-time includes the time to construct the inputs for the queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Moreover, for each query to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
|V_{1}|
vertices and
O(i)
hyperedges.

Proof.

We prove the claim in two steps. In the first step, we will use the expression for the function
p_{i}
given by
Lemma

5.15
(b) to construct an answer for the query
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S,T,y)
using a single query to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. In the second step, we will obtain the desired runtime using
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
by invoking
Lemma

2.2
.

For every
j\in[i]
, let
(\tilde{H}_{0}^{j},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j})
denote the hypergraph obtained by adding the vertices
\cup_{k\in[j-1]}\mathcal{Z}_{k}
to the hypergraph
(H^{k}_{0},w^{k}_{0})
. We now show the first step of the proof via the following procedure. First, construct the hypergraph
(G,c)
and the vector
y_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{V_{1}}
, where
G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{j\in[i-1]}\tilde{H}_{0}^{j}
,
c\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{j\in[i-1]},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j})
and
y_{1}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=y_{0}(u)
for every
u\in V_{i}
,
y_{1}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=0
otherwise. Next, obtain
(Z,p_{1}(Z))
by querying
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}((G,c),S,T,y_{1})
. Finally, return
(Z-\cup_{j=1}^{i-1}\mathcal{Z}_{j},p(Z))
as the answer to the query
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}(S,T,y)
. We note that correctness of the procedure is because
p_{i}={\left(p_{1}-\sum_{j\in[i-1]}b_{(\tilde{H}_{j},\tilde{w}_{j})}\right)}/_{\bigcup_{j\in[i-1]}\mathcal{Z}_{j}}
by
Lemma

5.15
(b).

We now show the second step of the proof. We observe that the hypergraph
(G,c)
constructed above has vertex set
V_{1}
since for every
j\in[i-1]
, the vertex set of
(\tilde{H}_{0}^{j},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j})
is contained in
V_{1}
. Moreover,
(G,c)
has at most
i-1
distinct hyperedges because for every
j\in[i-1]
, the hypergraph
(\tilde{H}_{0}^{j},\tilde{w}_{0}^{j})
has exactly
1
hyperedge by Step
7
. Consequently, by
Lemma

2.2
, the answer to the query
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}((G,c),S,T,y_{1})
in the above procedure can be computed in
O(|V_{1}|(|V_{1}|+i))
time using
|V_{1}|+1
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where the runtime includes the time to construct the inputs to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
; moreover, the hypergraph used as input to each
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
query has at most
|V_{1}|
vertices and
O(i)
hyperedges. Finally, we note that the construction of hypergraph
(G,c)
and returning the (set, value) pair returned by the above procedure can be implemented in
O(|V_{1}|(|V_{1}|+i))
time and so the claim holds.
∎

We now complete the proof using the two claims above. By
5.1
, all steps except for Steps
9
and
10
in the
i^{th}
recursive call can be implemented to run in
\poly(|V_{i}|)+O(\ell)
time using
\poly(|V_{i}|)
queries to
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. By
5.2
, an answer to a single
{p_{i}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
query can be computed in
O(|V_{1}|(|V_{1}|+\ell))
time using at most
O(|V_{1}|)
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Thus, all steps except for Steps
9
and
10
in the
i^{th}
recursive call can be implemented to run in
\poly(|V_{1}|,\ell)
time and
\poly(|V_{1}|)
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. By Claims
5.1
and
5.2
, the run-time also includes the time to construct the inputs for the queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Moreover, by
5.2
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to each
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
query has
|V_{1}|
vertices and
O(\ell)
hyperedges.

We note that Steps
9
and
10
need not be implemented explicitly for the purposes of the algorithm. Instead,
{p_{i+1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
can be used to execute all steps except for Steps
9
and
10
in the
(i+1)^{th}
recursive call. We note that
5.2
also enables us to answer a
{p_{i+1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
query in
O(|V_{1}|(|V_{1}|+\ell))
time using at most
|V_{1}|+1
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Thus, we have shown that each recursive call can be implemented to run in
\poly(|V_{1}|,\ell)
time and
\poly(|V_{1}|)
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Moreover, each query to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
is on an input hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
that has
|V_{1}|
vertices and
O(\ell)
hyperedges.
Since there are
\ell
recursive calls,
Algorithm

2
can be implemented to run in
\poly(|V_{1}|,\ell)
time and
\ell\cdot\poly(|V_{1}|)=\poly(|V_{1}|,\ell)
queries to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}

where each query to
{p_{1}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
is on an input hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
that has
|V_{1}|
vertices and
O(\ell)
hyperedges.
∎

For simultaneous covering of two skew-supermodular functions, we have the following lemma whose proof is similar to the proof of
Lemma

5.16
. We omit the proof in the interests of brevity.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
q,r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be skew-supermodular functions.
Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2

is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is a set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is the function defined as
p(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\max\{q(X),r(X)\}
for every
X\subseteq V
,
and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive integer-valued function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
.
Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
denote the recursion depth of
Algorithm

2
on the input tuple
(p,m,J)
.
Then,
Algorithm

2
can be implemented to run in time poly
(|V|,\ell)
using poly
(|V|,\ell)
queries to
{q}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
and
{r}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{wc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.
The run-time includes the time to construct the hypergraphs used as input to the queries to
{q}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
and
{r}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. Moreover, for each query to
{q}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
and
{r}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
|V|
vertices and
O(\ell)
hyperedges.

5.6
Weak Cover of One Function with Linear Number of Near-Uniform Hyperedges

In this section, we prove the following result.

See
1.8

We first describe how to prove
Theorem

1.8
under the assumption that the input function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a
positive
function. Under this extra assumption, we prove
Theorem

1.8
by running
Algorithm

2
on the input instance
(p,m,\emptyset)
. By
Corollary

5.3
, the execution of
Algorithm

2
is a feasible execution with a finite recursion depth. Furthermore, by
Lemma

5.5
, the hypergraph returned by
Algorithm

2
satisfies properties (1), (2), (3) and (4) of
Theorem

1.8
. In this section, we show that the hypergraph returned by
Algorithm

2
also satisfies property (5) of
Theorem

1.8
and that
the recursion depth of the algorithm is polynomial.
By
Lemma

5.16
and the observation that each recursive call of the algorithm adds at most one hyperedge, it suffices to bound the recursion depth of the
Algorithm

2
by
O(|V|)
.

The rest of this section is devoted to bounding the recursion depth. In
Section

5.6.1
, we define a family of
cumulative projected maximal
(p,m)
-tight sets
(w.r.t. an execution of
Algorithm

2
) and prove certain properties about this family that will be useful in bounding the recursion depth. In
Section

5.6.2
, we define a notion of
good vectors
and prove certain properties which we subsequently use to bound the number of recursive calls not considered by the bound in
Corollary

5.1
. In
Section

5.6.3
, we use the tools which we developed in
Section

5.6.1
and
Section

5.6.2
to show that the recursion depth of the algorithm is linear in the size of the ground set (see
Lemma

5.22
). Thus, the returned hypergraph also satisfies property (5) of
Theorem

1.8
.
Lemma

5.22
and
Lemma

5.16
together show that
Algorithm

2
can be implemented to run in
strongly polynomial
time
given the appropriate function evaluation oracle (see
Lemma

5.16
).
Corollary

5.3
,
Lemma

5.5
,
Lemma

5.22
, and
Lemma

5.16

complete the proof of
Theorem

1.8
under the assumption that the input function
m
is positive.

We now briefly remark on how to circumvent the positivity assumption on the input function
m
in the above proof. We note that if
\mathcal{Z}\coloneqq\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m(u)=0\}\not=\emptyset
, then
{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V-\mathcal{Z}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function with
K_{{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}}\coloneqq\max\{{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}\}
and
{m}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V-\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a
positive
function such that
{m}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}(X)\geq{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
and
{m}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}(u)\leq K_{{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}}
for every
u\in V-\mathcal{Z}
(i.e., the functions
{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{m}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}
satisfy conditions (a) and (b) of
Theorem

1.8
). Furthermore, we observe that a hypergraph satisfying properties (1)–(5) of
Theorem

1.8
for the functions
{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}
and
{m}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}
also satisfies the five properties for the functions
p
and
m
. Thus, our final algorithm is to return the hypergraph returned by

Algorithm

2
on the instance
({p}/_{\mathcal{Z}},{m}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}},\emptyset)
. Finally,
{{p}/_{\mathcal{Z}}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
can be implemented using
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
in strongly polynomial time. This completes the proof of
Theorem

1.8
.

5.6.1
Laminarity of Projected Maximal Tight Sets Across Recursive Calls

In this section, we define
(p,m)
-tight set families and investigate how they interact with the family of minimal
p
-maximizers. We will also investigate the progression of these families during an execution of
Algorithm

2
. The properties we prove in this section will be useful in the next section where we bound the runtime of
Algorithm

2
as well as the number of hyperedges in the hypergraph returned by the algorithm.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be set functions. A set
X\subseteq V
is said to be
(p,m)
-tight
if
p(X)=m(X)
. We let
T_{p,m}
denote the family of
(p,m)
-tight sets and
{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
denote the family of
inclusion-wise maximal

(p,m)
-tight sets.

The next lemma says that if
p
is a skew-supermodular function and
m
is positive function satisfying
m(Z)\geq p(Z)
for each
Z\subseteq V
, then the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
is disjoint. Furthermore, if the set
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
is a maximal tight set w.r.t. functions
p
and
m
, and the set
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is a minimal
p
-maximizer, then either the sets
X
and
Y
are disjoint, or the maximal tight set
X
is contained in the minimal
p
-maximizer
Y
.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a skew-supermodular function and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be a positive function such that
m(Z)\geq p(Z)
for all
Z\subseteq V
. Then, we have the following:

(a)

if
X,Y\in T_{p,m}
such that
X\cap Y\not=0
, then
X\cap Y,X\cup Y\in T_{p,m}
,

(b)

the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
is a disjoint set-family, and

(c)

if
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
and
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
, then either
X\cap Y=\emptyset
or
X\subseteq Y
.

Proof.

We prove the claims below.

(a)

If
X\subseteq Y
or
Y\subseteq X
, the claim holds. Suppose that
X-Y,Y-X\not=\emptyset
. We consider two cases based on the behavior of the function
p
at the sets
X
and
Y
. First, suppose that
p(X)+p(Y)\leq p(X-Y)+p(Y-X)
. We have the following:

p(X)+p(Y)\leq p(X-Y)+p(Y-X)\leq m(X-Y)+m(Y-X)<m(X)+m(Y)=p(X)+p(Y),

which gives a contradiction. Here, the second inequality is because
m(Z)\geq p(Z)
for every
Z\subseteq V
. The third inequality is because the function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow Z_{+}
is a positive function and
X\cap Y\not=\emptyset
. The final equality is because
X,Y\in T_{p,m}
. Next, suppose that
p(X)+p(Y)\leq p(X\cup Y)+p(X\cap Y)
. We have the following:

p(X)+p(Y)\leq p(X\cup Y)+p(X\cap Y)\leq m(X\cup Y)+m(X\cap Y)=m(X)+m(Y)=p(X)+p(Y).

Here, the second inequality is because
m(Z)\geq p(Z)
for every
Z\subseteq V
. The final equality is because the sets
X,Y\in T_{p,m}
. Thus, we have that all inequalities are equations. In particular, the second inequality is an equation and consequently the set
p(X\cup Y)=m(X\cup Y)
and
p(X\cap Y)=m(X\cap Y)
.

(b)

By way of contradiction, let
X,Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
be distinct maximal tight sets such that
X\cap Y\not=\emptyset
. We note that since the sets
X,Y
are distinct and maximal,
X-Y,Y-X\not=\emptyset
. Since
X,Y\in T_{p,m}
, by part (a) of the current lemma (shown above), we have that
X\cup Y\in T_{p,m}
, contradicting maximality of the sets
X,Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
.

(c)

Let
X\cap Y\not=\emptyset
and suppose by way of contradiction that
X-Y\not=\emptyset
. We consider two cases based on the behavior of the function
p
at the sets
X,Y
and arrive at a contradiction in both cases. First, suppose that
p(X)+p(Y)\leq p(X\cup Y)+p(X\cap Y)
. Then, we have that

p(X)+p(Y)\leq p(X\cup Y)+p(X\cap Y)\leq p(Y)+m(X\cap Y)<p(Y)+m(X)=p(Y)+p(X),

which is a contradiction. Here, the second inequality is because the set
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is a
p
-maximizer and
m(Z)\geq p(Z)
for every
Z\subseteq V
. The third inequality is because the function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function and
X-Y\not=\emptyset
. The final equality is because
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
. Next, suppose that
p(X)+p(Y)\leq p(X-Y)+p(Y-X)
. Then, we have that:

p(X)+p(Y)\leq p(X-Y)+p(Y-X)<m(X-Y)+p(Y)<m(X)+p(Y)=p(X)+p(Y),

which is a contradiction. Here, the second inequality is because the set
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is a
p
-maximizer and
m(Z)\geq p(Z)
for every
Z\subseteq V
. The third inequality is the function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function and
X-Y\not=\emptyset
. The final equality is because
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
.

∎

In the remainder of the section, we will examine the progression of the maximal
(p,m)
-tight set family across an execution of
Algorithm

2
.
Consider an execution of
Algorithm

2
with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
recursive calls. Suppose that the input to the
i^{th}
recursive call is the tuple
(p_{i},m_{i},J_{i})
.
For every
i\in[\ell-1]
, we let
T_{i},T_{i}^{\prime},{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
, and
{\mathcal{T}}_{i}^{\prime}
denote the families
T_{p_{i},m_{i}},T_{p_{i}^{\prime},m_{i}^{\prime}},{\mathcal{T}}_{p_{i},m_{i}}
, and
{\mathcal{T}}_{p_{i}^{\prime},m_{i}^{\prime}}
respectively. For convenience, we also define the families
T_{\ell},T_{\ell}^{\prime}\coloneqq T_{p_{\ell},m_{\ell}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}^{\prime},\coloneqq{\mathcal{T}}_{p_{\ell},m_{\ell}}
. Furthermore, we define the family of
cumulative projected maximal
(p_{i},m_{i})
-tight sets
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}&\text{if $i=1$,}\\
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i-1}|_{V_{i}}\cup{\mathcal{T}}_{i}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

\forall i\in[\ell]
.

In the next lemma, we prove several useful properties of these set families during feasible executions of
Algorithm

2
.

Lemma 5.19
.

Consider a feasible execution of
Algorithm

2
on an input instance that terminates in
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
recursive calls. For every
i\in[\ell]
, suppose that the input to the
i^{th}
recursive call
of
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p_{i},m_{i},J_{i})
, where
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function with
K_{p_{1}}>0
,
m_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function, and
J_{1}\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set such that
m_{1}(X)\geq p_{1}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m_{1}(u)\leq K_{p_{1}}
for every
u\in V
. Then, for every
i\in[\ell-1]
we have that

(a)

T_{i}\subseteq T_{i}^{\prime}
,

(b)

T_{i}^{\prime}|_{V_{i+1}}\subseteq T_{i+1}
,

(c)

{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}\subseteq T_{i}
,

(d)

the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}
is laminar, and

(e)

if
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\emptyset
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i+1}
, then
