{\mathcal{T}}_{i}={\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}
.

Proof.

Let
i\in[\ell-1]
be a recursive call. By
Corollary

5.3
and induction on
i
,
we have that
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is skew-supermodular function with
K_{p_{i}}>0
,
m_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function such that
m_{i}(X)\geq p_{i}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V_{i}
,
m_{i}(u)\leq K_{p_{i}}
for every
u\in V_{i}
and
Q(p_{i},m_{i})
is a non-empty integral polyhedron. We now prove each claim separately below.

(a)

Let
X\in T_{i}
. We first show that
|A_{i}\cap X|\leq 1
. By way of contradiction, let
|A_{i}\cap X|\geq 2
. Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle m_{i}^{\prime}(X)

\displaystyle=m_{i}(X)-\alpha_{i}\chi_{A_{i}}(X)

\displaystyle=p_{i}(X)-\alpha_{i}\chi_{A_{i}}(X)

\displaystyle\leq p_{i}(X)-2\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle<p_{i}(X)-\alpha_{i}b_{(V_{i},\{A_{i}\})}(X)

\displaystyle=p_{i}^{\prime}(X),

which is a contradiction to the functions
m_{i}^{\prime}(X)\geq p_{i}^{\prime}(X)

by
Lemma

5.3
(d). Here, the second equality is because
X\in T_{i}
, the first inequality is by our assumption that
|A_{i}\cap X|\geq 2
and the second inequality is because
b_{(V_{i},\{A_{i}\})}(X)=1
. We now show that
X\in T_{i}^{\prime}
. We have the following:

m_{i}^{\prime}(X)=m_{i}(X)-\alpha_{i}|A_{i}\cap X|=p_{i}(X)-\alpha_{i}b_{(V_{i},\{A_{i}\})}(X)=p_{i}^{\prime}(X).

Here, the second equality holds because
X\in T_{i}
, and
b_{(V_{i},\{A_{i}\})}(X)=|A_{i}\cap X|
since
|A_{i}\cap X|\leq 1
.

(b)

We note that each set in the family
T_{i}^{\prime}|_{V_{i+1}}
is of the form
Y-\mathcal{Z}_{i}
for some set
Y\in T_{i}^{\prime}
. Thus, it suffices to consider an arbitrary set
X\in T_{i}^{\prime}
and show that
X-\mathcal{Z}_{i}\in T_{i+1}
. We have the following:

m_{i+1}(X-\mathcal{Z}_{i})=m_{i}^{\prime}(X)=p_{i}^{\prime}(X)\leq p_{i+1}(X-\mathcal{Z}_{i})\leq m_{i+1}(X-\mathcal{Z}_{i}).

Thus, all inequalities are equations and we have that
X-\mathcal{Z}\in T_{i+1}
. Here, the first equality is because
m^{\prime}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})=0
. The second equality is because
X\in T_{i}^{\prime}
. The first inequality is because
p_{i+1}={p_{i}^{\prime}}/_{\mathcal{Z}_{i}}
. The final inequality is because the functions
m_{i+1}
and
p_{i+1}
satisfy condition
m_{i+1}(Z)\geq p_{i+1}(Z)
for every
Z\subseteq V_{i+1}
.
We remark that the statement (and proof) of this part can be strengthened to say that
T_{i}^{\prime}|_{V_{i+1}}=T_{i+1}
, but the weaker statement suffices for our purposes and so we omit the details.

(c)

By way of contradiction, let
i\in[\ell-1]
be the smallest recursive call such that the claim is false. We note that
i\geq 2
because
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq 1}={\mathcal{T}}_{1}\subseteq T_{1}
. Thus, we have that
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i-1}|_{V_{i}}\cup{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
. We observe that
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i-1}\subseteq T_{i-1}\subseteq T_{i-1}^{\prime}
, where the first inclusion is by our choice of
i\in[\ell-1]
, and the second inclusion is by part (1) of the current lemma (shown above). Furthermore, we have that
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i-1}|_{V_{i}}\subseteq T_{i-1}^{\prime}|_{V_{i}}\subseteq T_{i}
, where the second inclusion is by part (2) of the current lemma (shown above). Thus,
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}\subseteq T_{i}
.

(d)

By way of contradiction, let
i\in[\ell-1]
be the smallest recursive call such that the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}

is not laminar.
We note that
i\geq 2
since the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq 1}={\mathcal{T}}_{1}
is disjoint by
Lemma

5.18
(b). Thus, we have that
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i-1}|_{V_{i}}\cup{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
.
We note that the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
is also disjoint by
Lemma

5.18
(b). Furthermore, the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i-1}
is laminar by our choice of
i\in[\ell-1]
. Since the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}
is not laminar, there exist distinct sets
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i-1}|_{V_{i}}
and
Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
such that
X-Y,Y-X,X\cap Y\not=\emptyset
. By part (c) of the current lemma (shown above), we have that
X\in T_{i}
. Consequently, there exists
Z\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
such that
X\subseteq Z
. We note that
Y\not=Z
because
X-Z=\emptyset
and
X-Y\not=\emptyset
. Thus, by
Lemma

5.18
(b) we have that
Z\cap Y=\emptyset
. Therefore,
X\cap Y=\emptyset
, contradicting our choice of sets
X
and
Y
.

(e)

We first show that
{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
. By way of contradiction, let
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}-{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\not=\emptyset
. We note that since
{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i+1}={\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}
, the set
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}
. Furthermore, since
X\not\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
, we have that
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i-1}|_{V_{i}}
by definition of the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}
. By part (c) of the current lemma (shown above) and
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\emptyset
, we have that
X\in T_{i}
. Thus, there exists a
Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
such that
X\subsetneq Y
, where the inclusion is strict because
X\not\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
. By parts (a) and (b) of the current lemma (shown above), we have that
Y\in T_{i+1}
, contradicting maximality of
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}
.

Next, we show that
{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}
. By way of contradiction, let
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}-{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}\not=\emptyset
. By parts (a) and (b) of the current lemma (shown above) and
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\emptyset
, we have that
X\in T_{i+1}
. Thus, there exists
Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}
such that
X\subsetneq Y
, where the strict inclusion is because
X\not\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}
. By the previous paragraph, we have that
{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
. Consequently
Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
, contradicting maximality of the set
X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
.

∎

5.6.2
Adding Good Hyperedges

In this section, we show an important property of the set
A
chosen during a recursive call of
Algorithm

2
.
This property will be useful to bound the number of recursive calls of the algorithm and the number of hyperedges returned by the algorithm. We now formally define the property.

Definition 5.2

(
\alpha\text{-good}
Sets)
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be functions defined over a finite set
V
and
\alpha
be a non-negative real value.
A set
A\subseteq V
is
\alpha
-good for a set
Z\subseteq V
w.r.t. the functions
p
and
m
if the following three properties hold:

\displaystyle(1)\

\chi_{A}\in Q(p,m)
,

\displaystyle(2)\

\alpha|A\cap Z|=m(Z)-p(Z)+\alpha
, and

(
\alpha
-Good-Set)

\displaystyle(3)\

there is no
Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
such that
Z\subseteq Y
.

Furthermore, the set
A\subseteq V
is
\alpha
-good w.r.t the functions
p
and
m
if there exists a set
Z\subseteq V
such that
A
is
\alpha
-good for the set
Z
w.r.t functions
p
and
m
.

Before showing the main lemma of the section, we show the following lemma which highlights the use of
\alpha\text{-good}
sets.
It shows that during a feasible recursive call where the set
A
chosen by the algorithm is
\alpha\text{-good}
for a set
Z\subseteq V
, some superset of
Z
is added to the maximal tight set family (prior to function contractions).

Lemma 5.20
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function with
K_{p}>0
,
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function, and
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
and
Q(p,m)
is an integral polyhedron. Let
A,\alpha,p^{\prime},m^{\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
. Suppose that the set
A
is
\alpha\text{-good}
w.r.t. the functions
p
and
m
. Then, we have that

{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}-{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}\not=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let
Z\subseteq V
be such that the set
A
is
\alpha\text{-good}
for the set
Z
w.r.t. the functions
p
and
m
.
We first show that
|A\cap Z|\geq 2
.
We note that by (

α

-Good-Set
) property (3), the set
Z
is not a tight set for the functions
p
and
m
, i.e.
m(Z)>p(Z)
. Thus, we have that

|A\cap Z|=\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(m(Z)-p(Z)+\alpha\right)>1,

where the equality is by (

α

-Good-Set
) property (2) and
Lemma

5.2
(1) – we note that
\chi_{A}\in Q_{p,m}\not=\emptyset
by (

α

-Good-Set
) property (1).

Then, the following sequence of equations shows that the set
Z
is a tight set for the functions
p^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime}
. This implies the existence of a maximal tight set
Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
such that
Z\subseteq Y

and the claim follows as a consequence of (

α

-Good-Set
) property (3).

p^{\prime}(Z)=p(Z)-\alpha b_{(V,\{A\})}(Z)=p(Z)-\alpha=m(Z)-\alpha|A\cap Z|=m^{\prime}(Z).

Here, the second equality is because
|A\cap Z|\geq 1
. The third equality is due to (

α

-Good-Set
) property (2).
∎

The following is the main lemma of this section. It shows that if a recursive call of
Algorithm

2
witnesses
\alpha=\alpha^{(4)}<\min\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(5)}\}
, then at least one of the following happens: (1)
{\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}-{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\neq\emptyset
, (2)
{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}-{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}\neq\emptyset
, and (3) the hyperedge selected by the algorithm in the subsequent recursive call is
1
-good w.r.t. the functions
p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
.

Lemma 5.21
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function with
K_{p}>0
,
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function, and
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
. Let
A,\alpha,\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(4)},\alpha^{(5)},p^{\prime},m^{\prime}
,
p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
.
Suppose that

\alpha=\alpha^{(4)}<\min\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(5)}\}
and
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}>0
. Then, at least one of the following properties hold:

(1)

{\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}-{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\not=\emptyset
,

(2)

{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}-{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}\not=\emptyset
, and

(3)

let
y
be an extreme point optimum solution to
\max\left\{\sum_{u\in A}y_{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})\right\}
; then the set
T\coloneqq\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y_{u}>0\}
is
1
-good w.r.t. functions
p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

Suppose by way of contradiction that the claimed properties (1)-(3) of the claim do not hold. Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the set defined by
Algorithm

2
.
Since
\alpha<\alpha^{(1)}
, we have that
\mathcal{Z}=\emptyset
. Consequently,
V^{\prime\prime}=V
,
p^{\prime}=p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime\prime}
.
By
Corollary

5.3
, we have that the execution of
Algorithm

2
is a feasible execution.
Then, by
Lemma

5.9
(a) and (b), our contradiction assumption, and minimality of sets in the families
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
, we have that
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}={\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
.
Similarly, by
Lemma

5.19
(a) and (b), our contradiction assumption, and maximality of sets in the families
{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
, we have that
{\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}={\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
. We note that by
Corollary

5.3
, we also have the following:
p^{\prime\prime}
is a skew-supermodular function,
m^{\prime\prime}
is a positive function,
m^{\prime\prime}(X)\geq p^{\prime\prime}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,
m^{\prime\prime}(u)\leq K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
for every
u\in V
and
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}),Q(p,m)
are non-empty integral polyhedra.

Let
y
be an extreme point optimum solution to the LP
\max\{\sum_{u\in A}y_{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
and let
T\coloneqq\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}y_{u}>0\}
. Since the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
is integral, we have that
y=\chi_{T}
, where
\chi_{T}\in\{0,1\}^{V}
is the indicator vector of the set
T
.
Let
\chi_{A}\in\{0,1\}^{V}
be the indicator vector of the set
A
.
We recall that
\chi_{A}\in Q(p,m)
by definition of the set
A
and since the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
is integral. However,
\chi_{A}\not\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
by
Lemma

5.7
. Thus, we have that
A\neq T
. Since
\chi_{A}\in Q(p,m)
, it follows by
Lemma

5.1
(1) that the set
A
contains a transversal for the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}={\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
. Similarly, since
\chi_{T}\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
, it follows by
Lemma

5.1
(1) that the set
T
contains a transversal for the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}={\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
. Let
T_{0}\subseteq T
and
A_{0}\subseteq A
be minimal transversals for the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}={\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
such that:

(1)

|T_{0}\cap A_{0}|
is maximum, and

(2)

the set
\left\{\{u,v\}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in T_{0}\cap X,v\in A_{0}\cap X\text{ for some }X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}={\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}\right\}
is inclusion-wise maximal subject to condition (1).

Let
T_{1}\coloneqq T-T_{0}
and
A_{1}\coloneqq A-A_{0}
.

Claim 5.3
.

We have that
|A_{0}|=|T_{0}|
and
|A_{1}|-|T_{1}|\in\{0,1,-1\}
.

Proof.

The sets
A_{0}
and
T_{0}
are minimal transversals for the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}={\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
which is a disjoint family by
Lemma

3.1
. This implies that
|{\mathcal{F}}_{p}|=|A_{0}|=|T_{0}|
.

We note that
|A|\in[\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor,\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil]
. Moreover, we have that

|T|\in\left[\left\lfloor m^{\prime\prime}(V)/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\right\rfloor,\left\lceil m^{\prime\prime}(V)/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\right\rceil\right]=\left[\left\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\right\rfloor,\left\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\right\rceil\right],

where the equality is by
Lemma

5.4
(e). Hence,
|A|-|T|\in\{0,1,-1\}
. Since
A=A_{0}\uplus A_{1}
,
T=T_{0}\uplus T_{1}
, and
|A_{0}|=|T_{0}|
, it follows
|A_{1}|-|T_{1}|\in\{0,1,-1\}
.
∎

Based on the sets
T_{0},A_{0},T_{1}
, and
A_{1}
, we consider different cases to pick vertices
t\in T
and
a\in A
and define a set
B
as follows:

(i)

If
T_{0}\not=A_{0}
, then we pick
a\in A_{0}-T_{0}
and
t\in T_{0}-A_{0}
such that both vertices
a
and
t
are contained in the same minimal
p
-maximizer. Such a pair exists due to the following: the sets
A_{0}
and
T_{0}
are minimal transversals for the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}={\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
which is a disjoint family by
Lemma

3.1
. This implies that
|{\mathcal{F}}_{p}|=|A_{0}|=|T_{0}|
and
|A_{0}\cap X|=|T_{0}\cap X|=1
for every minimal
p
-maximizer
X\in{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
. Since
A_{0}\not=T_{0}
, there exists a minimal
p
-maximizer
X\in{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
such that
A_{0}\cap X\not=T_{0}\cap X
. Then, we may choose the pair
a,t
as
\{a\}\coloneqq A_{0}\cap X
and
\{t\}\coloneqq T_{0}\cap X
.

Here, we note that
a\not\in T_{1}
and
t\not\in A_{1}
by condition
(1)
of our choice of sets
T_{0}
and
A_{0}
which maximize
|T_{0}\cap A_{0}|
. We define
B\coloneqq T-t+a
.

(ii)

Suppose
T_{0}=A_{0}
. Since
A\neq T
but
T_{0}=A_{0}
, it follows that either
A_{1}-T_{1}\neq\emptyset
or
T_{1}-A_{1}\neq\emptyset
. We consider three cases:

(a)

If
A_{1}-T_{1}
and
T_{1}-A_{1}
are non-empty, then we pick
a\in A_{1}-T_{1}
and
t\in T_{1}-A_{1}
such that both vertices
a
and
t
are contained in the same maximal tight set if such a pair of vertices exists. Otherwise, we choose a pair of vertices
a\in A_{1}-T_{1}
and
t\in T_{1}-A_{1}
arbitrarily. We define
B\coloneqq T-t+a
.

(b)

If
A_{1}-T_{1}=\emptyset
, then

A_{1}\subseteq T_{1}
. Since
A\neq T
, it follows that
A_{1}\neq T_{1}
.
5.3
implies that
T_{1}
contains exactly one element apart from the elements of
A_{1}
. Let
t
be the unique element of
T_{1}-A_{1}
. We define
B\coloneqq T-t
. We note that
B=A
.

(c)

If
T_{1}-A_{1}=\emptyset
, then

T_{1}\subseteq A_{1}
. Since
A\neq T
, it follows that
A_{1}\neq T_{1}
.
5.3
implies that
A_{1}
contains exactly one element apart from the elements of
T_{1}
. Let
a
be the unique element of
A_{1}-T_{1}
. We define
B\coloneqq T+a
. We note that
B=A
.

In all cases above, we will show in
5.4
below that the indicator vector
\chi_{B}\in\{0,1\}^{V}
of the set
B
is a feasible point in the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. In cases (i) and (ii)(a), this leads to a contradiction as follows:

|T\cap A|<|B\cap A|\leq\max\left\{\sum_{u\in A}x_{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})\right\}=|T\cap A|.

In cases (ii)(b) and (ii)(c), the fact that
\chi_{B}=\chi_{A}\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
contradicts
Lemma

5.7
.

∎

Claim 5.4
.

\chi_{B}\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
.

Proof.

We recall the definition of
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
:

Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})\coloneqq\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{|V|}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{l}{(\text{i})}{\ \ \ 0\leq x_{u}\leq\min\{1,m^{\prime\prime}(u)\}}\hfill{\qquad\forall\ u\in V}\\
{(\text{ii})}{\ \ x(Z)\geq 1}\hfill{\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall\ Z\subseteq V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\ p^{\prime\prime}(Z)=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\\
{(\text{iii})}{\ x(u)=1}\hfill{\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ \forall\ u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\ m^{\prime\prime}(u)=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\\
{(\text{iv})}{\ x(Z)\leq m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)+1}\hfill{\ \ \ \forall\ Z\subseteq V}\\
{(\text{v})}{\ \ \left\lfloor\frac{m^{\prime\prime}(V)}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rfloor\leq x(V)\leq\left\lceil\frac{m^{\prime\prime}(V)}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rceil}\hfill\\
\end{array}\right\}

We will show that the vector
\chi_{B}
satisfies constraint (iv) in
5.5
. Thus, it suffices to show that constraints (i), (ii), (iii) and (v) are satisfied.
The vector
\chi_{B}
satisfies constraint (i) since
m^{\prime\prime}(a)=m(a)-\alpha>m(a)-\alpha^{(1)}\geq 0
, where the first equality is because
a\in A
, and the first inequality is because
\alpha<\alpha^{(1)}
.

Next, we show that
\chi_{B}
satisfies constraint (ii), i.e. the set
B
is a transversal for the set
{\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
. If
A_{0}\not=T_{0}
, then constraint (ii) holds by choice of
a,t
being contained in the same minimal
p
-maximizer. Otherwise,
A_{0}=T_{0}
and so we have that
A_{0}=T_{0}\subseteq B
by choice of
a\in A_{1}
and
t\in T_{1}
. Thus the set
B
is a transversal for
{\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
and constraint (ii) holds.

Next we show that constraint (iii) holds. Since
T-t\subseteq B
, it suffices to show that
m^{\prime\prime}(t)\not=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
. By way of contradiction, suppose
m^{\prime\prime}(t)=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}
. Thus, we have that

K_{p}-\alpha^{(4)}>K_{p}-\alpha^{(3)}\geq m(t)=m^{\prime\prime}(t)=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}=K_{p}-\alpha=K_{p}-\alpha^{(4)},

which is a contradiction. Here, the first inequality is due to
\alpha^{(4)}<\alpha^{(3)}
. The second inequality is by the definition of
\alpha^{(3)}
. The first equality is because
t\not\in A
by our choice of
t\in T-A
. The third equality is by
Lemma

5.4
(b)
and the final equality is because
\alpha=\alpha^{(4)}
.

Finally, we show that constraint (v) holds. We consider the different cases that define our set
B
and show that the vector
\chi_{B}
satisfies constraint (v) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
in each case. First, suppose that either (i)
A_{0}\neq T_{0}
or (ii)(a)
A_{0}=T_{0}
,
A_{1}-T_{1}\neq\emptyset
, and
T_{1}-A_{1}\neq\emptyset
holds. Then,
|B|=|T|
, and since
\chi_{T}
satisfies constraint (v) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
, it follows that
\chi_{B}
also satisfies constraint (v) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. Next, suppose that (ii)(b)
A_{0}=T_{0}
and
A_{1}-T_{1}=\emptyset
holds. Then,
|B|=|A|
and
|T|=|A|+1
. Since
|A|\in\{\left\lfloor{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rfloor,\left\lceil{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rceil\}
and
|T|\in\{\left\lfloor{m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rfloor,\left\lceil{m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rceil\}=\{\left\lfloor{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rfloor,\left\lceil{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rceil\}
, where the final equality is by
Lemma

5.4
(e) and
\alpha<\alpha^{(5)}
, it follows that
|A|=\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor
and
|T|=|A|+1=\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil
. Consequently,
|B|=|A|=\left\lfloor{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor{m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rfloor
and so constraint (v) holds. Next, suppose that (ii)(c)
A_{0}=T_{0}
and
T_{1}-A_{1}=\emptyset
holds. Then,
|B|=|A|
and
|T|=|A|-1
. By the same argument as in the previous case, we have that
|A|=\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil
and
|T|=|A|-1=\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor
. Consequently,
|B|=|A|=\left\lceil{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rceil=\left\lceil{m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rceil
and so constraint (v) holds.
∎

Claim 5.5
.

|B\cap Z|\leq m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)+1
for every
Z\subseteq V
.

Proof.

First, suppose that (ii)(b)
A_{0}=T_{0}
and
A_{1}-T_{1}=\emptyset
holds. For an arbitrary set
Z\subseteq V
, we have that

|B\cap Z|=\left|\left(T-t\right)\cap Z\right|=|T\cap Z|-\mathbbm{1}_{t\in Z}\leq m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)+1-\mathbbm{1}_{t\in Z}\leq m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)+1.

where the first inequality is because
\chi_{T}\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
satisfies constraint (iv) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. For the rest of the proof, we will assume that either (i)
A_{0}\neq T_{0}
or (ii)(a)
A_{0}=T_{0}
,
A_{1}-T_{1}\neq\emptyset
, and
T_{1}-A_{1}\neq\emptyset
or (ii)(c)
A_{0}=T_{0}
and
T_{1}-A_{1}=\emptyset
holds. If (ii)(c) holds, then we define the element
t
to be a dummy element that is not in
V
.

Let
Z\subseteq V
be a counter-example to the claim such that
|Z|
is maximum. We will need the following observation.

Observation 5.1
.

(1)
|T\cap Z|=m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)+1
, (2)
a\in Z
, and (3)
t\not\in Z
.

Proof.

We have the following:

|B\cap Z|=\left|\left(T-t+a\right)\cap Z\right|=|T\cap Z|-\mathbbm{1}_{t\in Z}+\mathbbm{1}_{a\in Z}\leq m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)+1-\mathbbm{1}_{t\in Z}+\mathbbm{1}_{a\in Z},

where the final inequality is because
\chi_{T}\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
satisfies constraint (iv) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. We note that if any of (1)-(3) are false, then the set
Z
is not a counter-example to the claim, contradicting the choice of the set
Z
.
∎

We first show that there exists a set
Y
in
{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
such that
Y
contains
Z
.
By way of contradiction, suppose that there is no set in
{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
containing the set
Z
. We recall that
\chi_{T}\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. By
5.1
, we have
|T\cap Z|=m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)+1
. Thus, the set
T
satisfies all three (

α

-Good-Set
) properties for
\alpha=1
and so we have that the set
T
is
1
-good for the set
Z
w.r.t. to the functions
p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
, a contradiction.

Next, we consider two cases based on whether the set
Z
is a set in
{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
, and arrive at a contradiction in both cases. First, suppose that there exists a set
Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
such that
Z\subsetneq Y
. Then, we have that

|B\cap Z|\leq|B\cap Y|\leq m^{\prime\prime}(Y)-p^{\prime\prime}(Y)+1=1\leq m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)+1,

a contradiction to the set
Z
being a counterexample to the claim.
Here, the first inequality is because
Z\subsetneq Y
. The second inequality is by our choice of the set
Z
being a counter-example of maximum cardinality. The equality is because the set
Y\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
and consequently
p^{\prime\prime}(Y)=m^{\prime\prime}(Y)
. The final inequality is because the functions
m^{\prime\prime}
and
p^{\prime\prime}
satisfy
m^{\prime\prime}(X)\geq p^{\prime\prime}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V^{\prime\prime}
by
Corollary

5.3
.

Next, suppose that
Z\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
. Here, we have the following two crucial observations.

Observation 5.2
.

A\cap Z=\{a\}
.

Proof.

We recall that
a\in A
by choice of the vertex
a
and
a\in Z
by
5.1
. Thus, we have that

a\in A\cap Z
. Furthermore, we have that
\chi_{A}\in Q(p,m)
by definition of
A
and the fact that
Q(p,m)
is an integral polyhedron. Thus, the vector
\chi_{A}
satisfies constraint (iv) of the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
. Then, we have the following:

1\leq|A\cap Z|\leq m(Z)-p(Z)+1=1.

Here, the first inequality is because
a\in A\cap Z
. The second inequality is because
\chi_{A}
satisfies constraint (iv) of the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
. The final equality is because
Z\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}={\mathcal{T}}_{p,m}
and hence
p(Z)=m(Z)
. Thus, all inequalities are equations and the claim follows.
∎

Observation 5.3
.

There exists an element
y\in T-(A\cap Z)
such that
T\cap Z=\{y\}
and
y\not\in\{a,t\}
.

Proof.

We have that
|T\cap Z|=m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)+1=1
, where the first equality is by
5.1
and the second equality is because
p^{\prime\prime}(Z)=m^{\prime\prime}(Z)
since
Z\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
. Let
y
be the unique element in
T\cap Z
. We note that the vertices
t
and
y
are distinct since
y\in Z
but
t\not\in Z
by
5.1
. Furthermore, the vertices
y
and
a
are distinct since
y\in T
but
a\not\in T
by our choice of the vertex
a\in A-T
. Thus,
y\not\in\{a,t\}
. Furthermore, by
5.2
we have that
y\not\in A\cap Z
and so
y\in T-(A\cap Z)
as claimed.
∎

With the previous observations established, we now consider the three subcases (i), (ii)(a) and (ii)(c) and arrive at a contradiction in all three subcases.
Firstly, we consider subcase (ii)(c): suppose that
A_{0}=T_{0}
and
T_{1}-A_{1}=\emptyset
. Then, it follows that
T\subseteq A
. Thus, there cannot be an element
y\in T-(A\cap Z)
such that
y\in T\cap Z
. This contradicts
5.3
.

We refer to
Figure

1(a)
and
Figure

1(b)
for a visualization of the interaction of the specified sets and elements under subcases (i) and (ii)(a).

(a)

Subcase (ii)(a).

(b)

Subcase (i).

Figure 1
:
Subcases in the proof of
5.5
for
Z\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
.

Secondly, we consider subcase (ii)(a): suppose that
T_{0}=A_{0}
,
A_{1}-T_{1}\neq\emptyset
, and
T_{1}-A_{1}\neq\emptyset
(see
Figure

1(a)
). By
5.2
and
5.3
,
we have the following three properties:

(i)

y\not\in A
but
y\in T
and so
y\in T_{1}-A_{1}
,

(ii)

a,y\in Z\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
, and

(iii)

t\not\in Z
.

Furthermore, there does not exist a set
Z^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
distinct from
Z
that contains the vertices
a
and
t
as otherwise
a\in Z^{\prime}\cap Z
and hence
Z^{\prime}\cap Z\not=\emptyset
, contradicting the disjointness of the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
by
Lemma

5.18
(b). Then, by property (iii), the pair
a,t
are not contained in any set of
{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
. Thus, by properties (i) and (ii), the pair of vertices
a,y
contradicts the choice of the pair of vertices
a,t
.

Thirdly, we consider subcase (i): suppose that
T_{0}\not=A_{0}
(see
Figure

1(b)
). Here, we first
recall that the vertices
a
and
t
belong to the same minimal
p^{\prime\prime}
-maximizer by choice of the pair
a,t
. Let
Y\in{\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
be the (unique) minimal
p^{\prime\prime}
-maximizer such that
a,t\in Y
. Next, we recall that
y,a\in Z
by
5.2
and
5.3
. Furthermore,
Z\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
by our case assumption. Thus, we have that
a\in Y\cap Z
and hence,
Y\cap Z\not=\emptyset
. In particular, this implies that
Z\subseteq Y
by
Lemma

5.18
(c) and so
y\in Y
. Thus, the set
T_{0}^{\prime}\coloneqq T_{0}-t+y
is also a minimal transversal for
{\mathcal{F}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
contained in the set
T
. We note that
\left|T^{\prime}_{0}\cap A_{0}\right|=\left|T_{0}\cap A_{0}\right|
(i.e., the sets
T_{0},A_{0}
do not contradict condition (1) of our choice of the sets
T_{0},A_{0}
) since
y\not\in A
by
5.3
. However, we have that

\displaystyle\left\{\{u,v\}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in T_{0},v\in A_{0},u\in X,v\in X\text{ for some }X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\subsetneq\left\{\{u,v\}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in T_{0}^{\prime},v\in A_{0},u\in X,v\in X\text{ for some }X\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}\right\},

contradicting the maximality condition
(2)
in the choice of the sets
A_{0}
and
T_{0}
. Here, we note that the LHS set is contained in the RHS set because
A_{0}\cap Y=\{a\}
,
T_{0}\cap Y=\{t\}
, and the unique set
Z\in{\mathcal{T}}_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}
containing the vertex
a
does not contain the vertex
t
. Furthermore, the containment is strict
because the RHS set contains the pair
\{y,a\}
, but the LHS set does not.
∎

5.6.3
Number of Recursive Calls and Hypergraph Support Size

In this section, we show that the number of recursive calls of
Algorithm

2
is linear in the size of the ground set
V
. Since
Algorithm

2
adds at most one new hyperedge in each recursive call, we obtain that the number of hyperedges in the hypergraph returned by the algorithm is linear in the size of the ground set. Consequently, this shows that the hypergraph returned by
Algorithm

2
satisfies property (5) of
Theorem

1.8
.

Lemma 5.22
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a skew-supermodular function with
K_{p}>0
,
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function, and
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
.
Then, we have that:

(1)

the recursion depth
\ell
of
Algorithm

2
is at most
11|V|+1
, and

(2)

the number of hyperedges in the hypergraph returned by
Algorithm

2
is at most
11|V|
.

Proof.

We note that part (2) of the lemma follows from part (1) of the lemma since at most one new hyperedge is added during each recursive call and the base case adds no new hyperedge. We now show part (1) of the lemma. Let
i\in[\ell]
be a recursive call of the algorithm’s execution.
We recall that
p_{j}
and
m_{j}
are the input functions to the
j^{th}
recursive call for
j\geq 2
. Since
p_{1}
is skew-supermodular, it follows that the function
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is skew-supermodular,
m_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function,
m_{i}(X)\geq p_{i}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V_{i}
,
m_{i}(u)\leq K_{p_{i}}
for every
u\in V_{i}
, and the execution of
Algorithm

2
on the instance
(p,m,J)
is a feasible execution.
We recall that for the sequence of functions
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{\ell}
, the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
denotes the family of minimal
p_{i}
-maximizers, and
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}=\cup_{j\in[i]}{\mathcal{F}}_{j}
. Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{i\in[\ell-1]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\alpha_{i}\in\left\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\alpha_{i}^{(5)}\right\}\right\}\right|+\left|\left\{i\in[\ell-1]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(4)}<\min\left\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\alpha_{i}^{(5)}\right\}\right\}\right|+1

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq\ell}\right|+2\left|V\right|+1\right)+\left(7\left|V\right|\right)+1

\displaystyle\leq 11|V|+1.

Here, the first inequality is
by
Corollary

5.1
and

5.6
below. The final inequality is because the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq\ell}
is laminar over the ground set
V
by
Lemma

3.2
and hence, has size at most
2|V|-1
.
∎

Claim 5.6
.

\left|\left\{i\in[\ell-1]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(4)}<\min\left\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\alpha_{i}^{(5)}\right\}\right\}\right|\leq 7|V|.

Proof.

We define the potential function
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[\ell]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
as follows: for every
i\in[\ell-1]

\phi(i)\coloneqq\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\right|+\left|{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}\right|+3\left|\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i-1}\right|.

First, we show that the potential function
\phi
is non-decreasing over
[\ell-1]
. We have the following:

\displaystyle\phi(i)

\displaystyle=\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\right|+\left|{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}\right|+3\left|\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i-1}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\right|+\left|{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}|_{V_{i+1}}\right|+3\left|\mathcal{Z}_{i}\right|+3\left|\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i-1}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\cup{\mathcal{F}}_{i+1}\right|+\left|{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}|_{V_{i+1}}\cup{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}\right|+3\left|\mathcal{Z}_{i}\right|+3\left|\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i-1}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}\right|+\left|{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i+1}\right|+3\left|\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i}\right|

\displaystyle=\phi(i+1).

Here, the first inequality is because of the following. By
Lemma

5.19
(d), the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}
is laminar. Furthermore,
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}|_{V_{i+1}}
is the projection of the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}
onto the ground set
V_{i+1}=V_{i}-\mathcal{Z}_{i}
. The inequality then follows by
Lemma

2.1
– we note that if
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\not=\emptyset
, then
Lemma

2.1
says that the inequality is strict. We will use this observation in the next part of the proof.

Let
i\in[\ell-3]
be a recursive call such that
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(4)}<\min\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\alpha_{i}^{(5)}\}
. We now show that
\phi(i)<\phi(i+2)
.
For the sake of contradiction, suppose that
\phi(i)\geq\phi(i+2)
. Since the function
\phi
is non-decreasing, we have that
\phi(i)=\phi(i+1)=\phi(i+2)
. By the observation in the last sentence of the previous paragraph, we have that
\mathcal{Z}_{i},\mathcal{Z}_{i+1}=\emptyset
, i.e.
V_{i}=V_{i+1}=V_{i+2}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i-1}=\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i}=\mathcal{Z}_{\leq i+1}
.
By
Lemma

5.10
(b), we have that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+2}
. Furthermore, by the definition of the families
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i+1}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i+2}
, we have that
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i+1}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i+2}
. Thus,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+1}={\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i+2}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i}={\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i+1}={\mathcal{T}}_{\leq i+2}
since if any of the inclusions are strict, then
\phi(i)<\phi(i+2)
by definition of the function
\phi
, a contradiction. Moreover, by
Lemma

5.10
(d) we have that
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}={\mathcal{F}}_{i+1}={\mathcal{F}}_{i+2}
, and by
Lemma

5.19
(e) we have that
{\mathcal{T}}_{i}={\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}={\mathcal{T}}_{i+2}
. Since
i<\ell-2
, we also observe that
K_{p_{i+1}},K_{p_{i+2}}>0
.
Then, by
Lemma

5.21
, the set
A_{i+1}
added by the
Algorithm

2
is a
1
-good set w.r.t. the functions
p_{i+1}
and
m_{i+1}
. Furthermore, by
Lemma

5.20
, we have that
{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}^{\prime}-{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}\not=\emptyset
. However, we observe that the functions
p_{i+2}=p_{i+1}^{\prime}
and
m_{i+2}=m_{i+1}^{\prime}
since
\mathcal{Z}_{i+1}=\emptyset
. Consequently,
{\mathcal{T}}_{i+2}={\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}^{\prime}
and so
{\mathcal{T}}_{i+2}-{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}\not=\emptyset
, contradicting
{\mathcal{T}}_{i+1}={\mathcal{T}}_{i+2}
.

Then, as a consequence of the previous two properties of our potential function, we obtain that:

\left|\left\{i\in[\ell-3]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(4)}<\min\left\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\alpha_{i}^{(5)}\right\}\right\}\right|\leq\phi(\ell-3)-\phi(0)\leq 7|V|-2.

Here, the final inequality is because
|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq\ell-3}|\leq 2|V|-1
since the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq\ell-3}
is laminar by
Lemma

3.2
,
\left|{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq\ell-3}\right|\leq 2|V|-1
since the family
{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq\ell-3}
is laminar by
Lemma

5.19
(d), and
|\mathcal{Z}_{\leq\ell-3}|\leq|V|
.
∎

5.7
Weak Cover of Two Functions with Quadratic Number of Near-Uniform Hyperedges

In this section we show the following main result.

See
1.9

We first describe how to prove
Theorem

1.9
under the assumption that the input function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a
positive
function. Under this extra assumption, we prove
Theorem

1.8
by running
Algorithm

2
on the input instance
(p,m,\emptyset)
. By
Corollary

5.2
, the execution of
Algorithm

2
is a feasible execution with finite recursion depth. Furthermore, by
Lemma

5.5
, the hypergraph returned by
Algorithm

2
satisfies properties (1), (2), (3) and (4) of
Theorem

1.9
. In this section, we show that the hypergraph returned by
Algorithm

2
also satisfies property (5) of
Theorem

1.9
and that the recursion depth of the algorithm is polynomial. By
Lemma

5.16
and the observation that each recursive call of the algorithm adds at most one hyperedge, it suffices to bound the recursion depth of the
Algorithm

2
by
O(|V|^{2})
.

The rest of this section is devoted to bounding the recursion depth.
In
Section

5.7.1
, we introduce the notion of
pairwise-slack functions
and prove certain properties about them which we subsequently use in

Section

5.7.2
to show that the recursion depth of the algorithm is at most quadratic in the size of the ground set (see
Lemma

5.25
). Thus, the returned hypergraph also satisfies property (5) of
Theorem

1.9
.

Lemma

5.22
and
Lemma

5.16
together show that
Algorithm

2
can be implemented to run in
strongly polynomial
time
given the appropriate function evaluation oracle (see
Lemma

5.16
).

Corollary

5.2
,

Lemma

5.5
,

Lemma

5.25
, and
Lemma

5.17
complete the proof of
Theorem

1.8
under the assumption that the input function
m
is positive.
The positivity assumption on the function
m
can be circumvented in the same manner as in the proof of
Theorem

1.8
(see beginning of
Section

5.6
).

5.7.1
Slack and Pairwise Slack Functions

In this section, we define
slack
and
pairwise-slack
functions. We then show a few key properties about how these functions behave throughout the execution of the algorithm. In subsequent sections, we will use these properties to define a notion of
progress
of the algorithm in order to bound the number of recursive calls (see
Section

5.7.2
). We remark that the slack function is an intermediate function only defined for convenience and that the proof of
Theorem

1.9
will only require the properties of the pairwise-slack function.

Remark 5.1
.

We emphasize that the properties to be shown in this section were not needed in the proof of
Theorem

1.8
where the input function
p
was skew-supermodular. Unlike the proof of
Theorem

1.8
, it is unclear whether
good hyperedges
(see
Section

5.6.2
) even exist under the hypothesis of
Theorem

1.9
– we recall that the existence of good hyperedges was crucial in showing that the recursion depth of
Algorithm

2
is linear when the input function
p
is skew-supermodular (see
Section

5.6.3
). We will subsequently use properties of the pairwise-slack function to show a quadratic bound on the recursion depth of
Algorithm

2
when the input function
p
is the maximum of two skew-supermodular functions.

We now define slack and pairwise-slack functions.
For functions
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, the
(p,m)
-slack
function
\Gamma_{p,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is defined as
\Gamma_{p,m}(X)=m(X)-p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,
and the
pairwise
(p,m)
-slack function

\gamma_{p,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{V\choose 2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is defined as

\gamma_{p,m}(\{u,v\})\coloneqq\min\left\{\Gamma_{p,m}(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u,v\in Z\right\}

for every pair
\{u,v\}\in{V\choose 2}
.
The following two lemmas summarize useful properties of the pairwise slack function during the execution of

Algorithm

2
.

Lemma 5.23
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a function and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function, and
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
, and
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty polyhedron.
Let
A,\alpha,p^{\prime},m^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
.
Then, we have the following:

(1)

\Gamma_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}(Z)\leq\Gamma_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(Z)\leq\Gamma_{p,m}(Z)
for every
Z\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
; furthermore, for a set
Z\subseteq V
, we have that
\Gamma_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(Z)<\Gamma_{p,m}(Z)
if and only if
|A\cap Z|\geq 2
, and

(2)

\gamma_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}(\{u,v\})\leq\gamma_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(\{u,v\})\leq\gamma_{p,m}(\{u,v\})
for every
\{u,v\}\in{V-\mathcal{Z}\choose 2}
;
furthermore,
\gamma_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(\{u,v\})<\gamma_{p,m}(\{u,v\})
for each pair
\{u,v\}\in{A\choose 2}
.

Proof.

We prove both properties separately below.

(1)

Let
Z\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
. Then, we have the following:

\Gamma_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}(Z)=m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)={m^{\prime}}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}(Z)-{p^{\prime}}/_{\mathcal{Z}}(Z)\leq m^{\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime}(Z)=\Gamma_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(Z),

where the inequality is because
{m^{\prime}}\backslash_{\mathcal{Z}}(Z)=m(Z)
by definition of
\mathcal{Z}=\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m^{\prime}(u)=0\}
and
Z\subseteq V-\mathcal{Z}
.
Let
(H_{0},w_{0})
be the hypergraph computed by
Algorithm

2
. Then, we have that

\Gamma_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(Z)=(m-\alpha\chi_{A})(Z)-(p-b_{(H_{0},w_{0})})(Z)=m(Z)-p(Z)-\alpha|A\cap Z|+b_{(H_{0},w_{0})}(Z)\leq m(Z)-p(Z)=\Gamma_{p,m}(Z).

The claim then follows by observing that the inequality in the previous sequence is strict if and only if
|A\cap Z|\geq 2
.

(2)

We note that the first part of the statement, i.e.
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(\{u,v\})\leq\gamma^{\prime}(\{u,v\})\leq\gamma(\{u,v\})
for every
\{u,v\}\in{V-\mathcal{Z}\choose 2}
, follows from part (1) of the current lemma. We now show the second part of the statement. Let
Z
be a set witnessing
\gamma_{p,m}(\{u,v\})
, i.e.
Z\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\arg\min\{m(Z)-p(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u,v\in Z\}
. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\gamma_{p,m}({u,v})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{p,m}(Z)

\displaystyle=m(Z)-p(Z)

\displaystyle=(m^{\prime}(Z)+\alpha|A\cap Z|)-(p^{\prime}(Z)+\alpha)

\displaystyle>m^{\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime}(Z)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(Z)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(\{u,v\}).

Here, the strict inequality in the previous sequence is because
|A\cap Z|\geq 2
since
u,v\in A\cap Z
.

∎

Lemma 5.24
.

Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a function,
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive function, and
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
, and
Q(p,m)
is a non-empty integral polyhedron. Let
A,\alpha,\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(4)},\alpha^{(5)},p^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
.
Suppose that

\alpha=\alpha^{(4)}<\min\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(5)}\}

and
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}>0
. Then, there exists
an optimum solution
Z
to the problem
\min\left\{\left\lfloor{\frac{m(Z)-p(Z)}{|A\cap Z|-1}}\right\rfloor\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|A\cap Z|\geq 2,Z\subseteq V\right\}
such that

(1)

\Gamma_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}(Z)<|A\cap Z|-1
, and

(2)

\gamma_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}(\{u,v\})<\min\left\{|A\cap Z|-1,\gamma_{p,m}(\{u,v\})\right\}
for every pair
\{u,v\}\in{A\cap Z\choose 2}
.

Proof.

We note that part (2) of the lemma is a consequence of part (1) of the lemma and
Lemma

5.23
(2). Hence, we focus on showing part (1) of the lemma.
We say that a set
Z\subseteq V
is a
witness set
if it is an optimum solution to the problem

\min\left\{\left\lfloor{\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}}\right\rfloor\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|A\cap X|\geq 2,X\subseteq V\right\}.

By way of contradiction, suppose that
\Gamma_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}(Z)\geq|A\cap Z|-1
for every witness set
Z\subseteq V
.
Let
(H_{0},w_{0})
and
\mathcal{Z}
be as defined by
Algorithm

2
. Since
\alpha<\alpha^{(1)}
, we have that
\mathcal{Z}=\emptyset
. In particular, we have that
A\cap\mathcal{Z}=\emptyset
,
p^{\prime\prime}=p^{\prime}
,
m^{\prime\prime}=m^{\prime}
, and
\Gamma_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}=\Gamma_{p^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
. Our strategy will be to show that the indicator vector
\chi_{A}\in\{0,1\}^{V}
of the set
A
is in the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
, thus contradicting
Lemma

5.7
.
We recall the definition of
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
– we note that constraint (v) is well-defined since
K_{p^{\prime\prime}}>0
:

Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})\coloneqq\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{|V|}\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{l}{(\text{i})}{\ \ \ 0\leq x_{u}\leq\min\{1,m^{\prime\prime}(u)\}}\hfill{\qquad\forall\ u\in V}\\
{(\text{ii})}{\ \ x(Z)\geq 1}\hfill{\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall\ Z\subseteq V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\ p^{\prime\prime}(Z)=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\\
{(\text{iii})}{\ x(u)=1}\hfill{\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ \forall\ u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\ m^{\prime\prime}(u)=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\\
{(\text{iv})}{\ x(Z)\leq m^{\prime\prime}(Z)-p^{\prime\prime}(Z)+1}\hfill{\ \ \ \forall\ Z\subseteq V}\\
{(\text{v})}{\ \ \left\lfloor\frac{m^{\prime\prime}(V)}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rfloor\leq x(V)\leq\left\lceil\frac{m^{\prime\prime}(V)}{K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rceil}\hfill\\
\end{array}\right\}

Since
\alpha<\alpha^{(1)}
, it follows that
\chi_{A}
satisfies constraint (i) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
.
Since
\alpha<\alpha^{(2)}
, the set
A
is a transversal for the family of minimal
p^{\prime\prime}
-maximizers. Hence,
\chi_{A}
satisfies constraint (ii) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
.
Since
\alpha<\alpha^{(3)}
, we have that
\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m(u)=K_{p}\}=\{u\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}m^{\prime\prime}(u)=K_{p^{\prime\prime}}\}
. Hence,
\chi_{A}
satisfies constraint (iii) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
.

Next, we show that
\chi_{A}
satisfies constraint (iv) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. Consider a set
X\subseteq V
. Suppose that the set
X
is a witness set. Then, we have that
m^{\prime\prime}(X)-p^{\prime\prime}(X)=\Gamma_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}(X)\geq|A\cap X|-1
, where the final inequality is by our contradiction assumption. Thus, the vector
\chi_{A}
satisfies constraint (iv) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
for subsets
X
that are witness sets. Next, suppose that the set
X
is not a witness set.
Here, we consider two cases. First, suppose that
|X\cap A|\leq 1
. Then, we have the following:

m^{\prime\prime}(X)-p^{\prime\prime}(X)=\Gamma_{p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}}(X)=\Gamma_{p,m}(X)=m(X)-p(X)\geq|A\cap X|-1,

where the second equality is by
Lemma

5.23
(1) and the final inequality is because
\chi_{A}\in Q(p,m)
. Next, suppose that
|X\cap A|\geq 2
.
By way of contradiction, suppose that constraint (iv) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
does not hold for the set
X
, i.e.
m^{\prime\prime}(X)-p^{\prime\prime}(X)<|A\cap X|-1
. Then, we have that
m(X)-\alpha|A\cap X|-p(X)+\alpha<|A\cap X|-1
and hence,
m(X)-p(X)<(\alpha+1)(|A\cap X|-1)
. Thus,

\alpha<\left\lfloor{\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}}\right\rfloor<\alpha+1,

where the first inequality is because the set
X
is not a witness set. The above is a contradiction since all three quantities
\alpha
,
\left\lfloor{\frac{m(X)-p(X)}{|A\cap X|-1}}\right\rfloor
, and
\alpha+1
are integers, but we cannot have an integer that is strictly larger than the integer
\alpha
and strictly smaller than the integer
\alpha+1
.

Finally, we show that the vector
\chi_{A}
satisfies constraint (v) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. Since
\alpha<\alpha^{(5)}
, we have that
\left\lceil{m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rceil=\left\lceil{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rceil
and
\left\lfloor{m^{\prime\prime}(V^{\prime\prime})/K_{p^{\prime\prime}}}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor{m(V)/K_{p}}\right\rfloor
by
Lemma

5.4
(e), i.e. constraint (v) of
Q(p,m)
is equivalent to constraint (v) of
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. Since
\chi_{A}\in Q(p,m)
, it follows that
\chi_{A}
satisfies constraint (v) of the polyhedron
Q(p,m)
, and hence,
\chi_{A}
also satisfies constraint (v) of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
.
∎

5.7.2
Number of Recursive Calls and Hypergraph Support Size

In this section, we show that
Algorithm

2
witnesses
O(|V|^{2})
recursive calls. Since the algorithm adds at most one new hyperedge during every recursive call, we will obtain that the hypergraph returned by the algorithm has
O(|V|^{2})
distinct hyperedges.

Remark 5.2
.

Consider a feasible execution of
Algorithm

2
with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
recursive calls. Let
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
denote the input function to the
i^{th}
recursive call.
We recall that
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{i}}
denotes the family of minimal
p_{i}
-maximizers, and
{\mathcal{F}}_{{p}_{\leq\ell}}\coloneqq\cup_{j\in[i]}{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{j}}
denotes the family of cumulative minimal maximizers w.r.t. the sequence of functions
p_{1},\ldots p_{\ell}
.
In the previous section, a necessary step towards bounding the number of recursive calls and hypergraph support size was to show that the size of the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}
is linear (see
Lemma

5.22
in
Section

5.6
).
In particular, we had that the function
p_{1}
, and consequently the function
p_{i}
for every
i\in[\ell]
, was skew-supermodular. However, here we have that the function
p_{i}
is the maximum of two individually skew-supermodular functions, and so it is not necessarily skew-supermodular. Nevertheless, we will see (in the proof of the next lemma) that the size of the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{{p}_{\leq\ell}}
is still linear in the size of the ground set. Consequently, the bottleneck in bounding the recursion depth of the algorithm is analyzing the number of recursive calls for which
\alpha=\alpha^{(4)}<\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(5)}\}
.

Lemma 5.25
.

Let
q,r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be skew-supermodular functions.
Suppose that the input to
Algorithm

2
is a tuple
(p,m,J)
, where
J\subseteq V
is an arbitrary set,
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a function defined by
p(X)\coloneqq\max\{q(X),r(X)\}
for every
X\subseteq V
with
K_{p}>0
and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is a positive integer-valued function such that
m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
and
m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
.
Then, we have that:

(1)

the recursion depth
\ell
of
Algorithm

2
is at most
14|V|^{2}
, and

(2)

the number of hyperedges in the hypergraph returned by
Algorithm

2
is at most
14|V|^{2}-1
.

Proof.

We note that property (2) of the claim follows from property (1) of the claim since at most one new hyperedge is added during each recursive call of the algorithm and no hyperedges are added during the base case. We now show property (1) of the claim. By
Corollary

5.2
, the execution of
Algorithm

2
on the instance
(p,m,J)
is a feasible execution with finite recursion depth
\ell
. Let
i\in[\ell-1]
be a recursive call of the algorithm’s execution.
We recall that
p_{i}
and
m_{i}
denote the input functions to the recursive call for
i\geq 2
.
We recall that for the sequence of functions
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{\ell}
, the family
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
denotes the family of minimal
p_{i}
-maximizers, and
{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq i}=\cup_{j\in[i]}{\mathcal{F}}_{j}
. Then, we have the following.

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{i\in[\ell-1]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\alpha_{i}\in\left\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\alpha_{i}^{(5)}\right\}\right\}\right|+\left|\left\{i\in[\ell-1]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(4)}<\min\left\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\alpha_{i}^{(5)}\right\}\right\}\right|+1

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{\leq\ell}\right|+2\left|V\right|+1\right)+(8|V|^{2})+1

\displaystyle\leq 14|V|^{2}.

Here, the first inequality is
by
Corollary

5.1
and
5.7

below. The final inequality is by
5.8
below.
∎

Claim 5.7
.

\left|\left\{i\in[\ell-1]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(4)}<\min\left\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\alpha_{i}^{(5)}\right\}\right\}\right|\leq 8|V|^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
i\in[\ell-2]
be a recursive call of the algorithm. By
Corollary

5.2
and induction on
i
, we have that
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
with and
m_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
are functions such that
m_{i}(X)\geq p_{i}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V_{i}
,
m_{i}(u)\leq K_{p_{i}}
for every
u\in V_{i}
and
Q(p_{i},m_{i})
is a non-empty integral polyhedron. We note that since
i<\ell-1
, we have that
K_{p_{i}},K_{p_{i+1}}>0
. For notational convenience, let
\gamma_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\gamma_{p_{i},m_{i}}
denote the pairwise
(p_{i},m_{i})
-slack function.

We define the potential function
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[\ell-2]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
as

\phi(i)=\sum_{\{u,v\}\in{V_{i}\choose 2}}\sum_{j\in[\gamma_{i}(\{u,v\})]}\frac{1}{j^{2}}.

We note that the function
\phi
is non-increasing over the domain
[\ell-2]
because
V_{i+1}\subseteq V_{i}
and
\gamma_{i+1}(\{u,v\})\leq\gamma_{i}(\{u,v\})
for every
\{u,v\}\in{V_{i+1}\choose 2}
by
Lemma

5.23
(2). We now show that the potential function strictly decreases in value after a recursive call witnessing
\alpha=\alpha^{(4)}<\min\{\alpha^{(1)},\alpha^{(2)},\alpha^{(3)},\alpha^{(5)}\}
. Let
i\in[\ell-2]
be such a recursive call.
We recall that a set
Z\subseteq V_{i}
is a
witness set
if
Z
is an optimum solution to the problem
\min\left\{\left\lfloor{\frac{m_{i}(X)-p_{i}(X)}{|A_{i}\cap X|-1}}\right\rfloor\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}|A_{i}\cap X|\geq 2,X\subseteq V_{i}\right\}
. By
Lemma

5.24
(2), there exists a witness set
Z\subseteq V_{i}
such that
\gamma_{i+1}(\{u,v\})<\min\left\{|A\cap Z|-1,\gamma_{i}(\{u,v\})\right\}
for every pair
\{u,v\}\in{A_{i}\cap Z\choose 2}
. Then, we have the following:

\displaystyle\phi(i)-\phi(i+1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{u,v\}\in{V_{i}\choose 2}}\sum_{j\in[\gamma_{i}(\{u,v\})]}\frac{1}{j^{2}}-\sum_{\{u,v\}\in{V_{i+1}\choose 2}}\sum_{j\in[\gamma_{i+1}(\{u,v\})]}\frac{1}{j^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{u,v\}\in{V_{i}\choose 2}}\sum_{j\in\left[\gamma_{i+1}(\{u,v\})+1,\gamma_{i}(\{u,v\})\right]}\frac{1}{j^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\{u,v\}\in{A_{i}\cap Z\choose 2}}\frac{1}{\left(1+\gamma_{i+1}(\{u,v\})\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq{A_{i}\cap Z\choose 2}\frac{1}{|A_{i}\cap Z|^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq 1/4.

Here, the second equality is because
V_{i+1}=V_{i}
and
\gamma_{i+1}(\{u,v\})\leq\gamma_{i}(\{u,v\})
for every
\{u,v\}\in{V_{i}\choose 2}
by
Lemma

5.23
(2). The first inequality is because
A_{i},Z\subseteq V_{i}
, the pairwise-slack function is non-negative, and
\gamma_{i+1}(\{u,v\})<\gamma_{i}(\{u,v\})
for every pair
\{u,v\}\in\binom{A_{i}\cap Z}{2}
.
The second inequality is because
\gamma_{i+1}(\{u,v\})\leq|A_{i}\cap Z|-1
by
Lemma

5.24
(2). The final inequality is because
|A_{i}\cap Z|\geq 2
.

By the previous two properties, we obtain the claimed bound as follows:

\displaystyle\left|\left\{i\in[\ell-2]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{(4)}<\min\left\{\alpha_{i}^{(1)},\alpha_{i}^{(2)},\alpha_{i}^{(3)},\alpha_{i}^{(5)}\right\}\right\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\phi(1)-\phi(\ell-2)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\sum_{\{u,v\}\in{V\choose 2}}\sum_{j\in[\gamma_{1}(\{u,v\})]}\frac{1}{j^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 4\sum_{\{u,v\}\in{V\choose 2}}\sum_{j\geq 1}\frac{1}{j^{2}}

\displaystyle<8|V|^{2},

where the final inequality holds because
\sum_{j\geq 1}1/j^{2}=\pi^{2}/6<2
.
∎

Claim 5.8
.

|{\mathcal{F}}_{{p}_{\leq\ell}}|\leq 4|V|-2

Proof.

We recall that the functions
q_{1},r_{1}
are skew-supermodular, and

q_{i}\coloneqq{(q_{i-1}-b_{(H_{0}^{i-1},w_{0}^{i-1})})}/_{\mathcal{Z}_{i-1}}
and
r_{i}\coloneqq{(r_{i-1}-b_{(H_{0}^{i-1},w_{0}^{i-1})})}/_{\mathcal{Z}_{i-1}}
for
\ell\geq i\geq 2
, where
(H_{0}^{i},w_{0}^{i})
is the hypergraph
(H_{0},w_{0})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
is the set
\mathcal{Z}
defined by the algorithm during the
i^{th}
recursive call (we define
(H_{0}^{\ell},w_{0}^{\ell})
as the empty hypergraph and
\mathcal{Z}_{\ell}\coloneqq\emptyset
). Let
i\in[\ell]
be a recursive call.
By
Corollary

5.2
and induction on
i
,
we have that
p_{i}(X)=\max\{q_{i}(X),r_{i}(X)\}
for every
X\subseteq V_{i}
. Consequently, every
p_{i}
-maximizer is either a
q_{i}
-maximizer or a
r_{i}
-maximizer.
We consider the two sequences of functions
q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{\ell}
and
r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{\ell}
, and the corresponding cumulative minimal maximizer families
{\mathcal{F}}_{{q}_{\leq\ell}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{{r}_{\leq\ell}}
respectively.
By the previous observation and the definition of the families, we have that
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{q_{\leq\ell}}\cup{\mathcal{F}}_{r_{\leq\ell}}
. In particular, we have the following:

\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{\leq\ell}}\right|\leq\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{q_{\leq\ell}}\right|+\left|{\mathcal{F}}_{r_{\leq\ell}}\right|\leq(2|V|-1)+(2|V|-1),

where the final inequality is because the families
{\mathcal{F}}_{{q}_{\leq\ell}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{{r}_{\leq\ell}}
are laminar over the ground set
V
by
Lemma

3.2
, and hence, both of them have size at most
2|V|-1
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

We recall that the proof of
Theorem

1.8
(in particular,
Lemma

5.22
) crucially leveraged the following property of
Algorithm

2
: let
p,m
and
p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime}
be input functions during two consecutive recursive calls of
Algorithm

2
and let
A
and
A^{\prime\prime}
be the hyperedges chosen during these recursive calls respectively; then,
A^{\prime\prime}
is a set that has maximum intersection with the set
A
subject to the indicator vector
\chi_{A^{\prime\prime}}
of the set
A^{\prime\prime}
satisfying
\chi_{A^{\prime\prime}}\in Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
. We note that this property is ensured by Steps
3
and
4
of
Algorithm

2
, where the vector
y
is chosen to be an optimum (integral) extreme point solution of the polyhedron
Q(p^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})
that maximizes along the objective direction
\chi_{A}
, the indicator vector of the set
A
. Furthermore, the set
A^{\prime\prime}
is defined as the support of this (integral) vector
y
.
We do not know how to take advantage of this maximum intersection property in the analysis for two functions (i.e., under the hypothesis of
Theorem

1.9
). We conjecture that
Algorithm

2
can be used to prove a stronger version of
Theorem

1.9
– namely that the number of hyperedges in the support is
O(|V|)
as opposed to
O(|V|^{2})
and leave this as an open question.

6
Applications

In this section, we discuss applications of Theorems

1.7
,
1.8
, and
1.9
.
In
Section

6.1
, we discuss a reduction from symmetric skew-supermodular strong cover to symmetric skew-supermodular weak cover problems. This reduction will be useful in the three connectivity augmentation applications that we discuss subsequently:
We consider
DS-Hypergraph-CA-using-H
and
DS-Simul-Hypergraph-CA-using-H
in
Section

6.2
, hypergraph node-to-area connectivity augmentation using hyperedges in
Section

6.3
, and degree-constrained mixed-hypergraph global connectivity augmentation using hyperedges in
Section

6.4
.

6.1
Strong cover to weak cover reduction for symmetric skew-supermodular functions

In this section, we recall a reduction from symmetric skew-supermodular
strong
cover using hyperedges problem to symmetric skew-supermodular
weak
cover using hyperedges problem due to Bernáth and Király
[
8
]
and state implications of their reduction in conjunction with our main results. These implications will be useful in subsequent applications.
Bernáth and Király showed
Lemma

6.1
below.

Lemma 6.1

(
[
8
]
)
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a symmetric skew-supermodular function and
(H=(V,E),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
be a hypergraph with
\sum_{e\in E}w(e)=K_{p}
. If

(H,w)
weakly covers the function
p
, then
(H,w)
strongly covers the function
p
.

Our results for
DS-Skew-SupMod-WeakCover-using-H
and
DS-Simul-Skew-SupMod-WeakCover-using-H
(i.e., Theorems

1.7
,

1.8
, and
1.9
) showed the existence of a hypergraph
(H=(V,E),w)
that weakly covers the function
p
such that
\sum_{e\in E}w(e)=K_{p}
. Hence, using
Lemma

6.1
in conjunction with Theorems
1.7
,

1.8
, and
1.9
lead to the corollaries below regarding
strong
cover for
symmetric
skew-supermodular functions.

Corollary 6.1
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a symmetric skew-supermodular function and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a non-negative function satisfying the following two conditions:

(a)

m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,

(b)

m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
.

Then, there exists a hypergraph
\left(H=\left(V,E\right),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)
satisfying the following four properties:

(1)

d_{(H,w)}(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,

(2)

b_{(H,w)}(u)=m(u)
for every
u\in V
,

(3)

\sum_{e\in E}w(e)=K_{p}
, and

(4)

|E|=O(|V|)
.

Furthermore, given a function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and access to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
of a symmetric skew-supermodular function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow Z
where
m
and
p
satisfy conditions (a) and (b), there exists an algorithm that runs in time
O(|V|^{5})
using
O(|V|^{4})
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
and returns a hypergraph satisfying the above four properties. The run-time includes the time to construct the hypergraphs that are used as inputs to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a symmetric skew-supermodular function and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a non-negative function satisfying the following three conditions:

(a)

m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,

(b)

m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
,

Then, there exists a hypergraph
\left(H=\left(V,E\right),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)
satisfying the following five properties:

(1)

d_{(H,w)}(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,

(2)

b_{(H,w)}(u)=m(u)
for every
u\in V
,

(3)

\sum_{e\in E}w(e)=K_{p}
,

(4)

|e|\in\{\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor,\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil\}
for every
e\in E
, and

(5)

|E|=O(|V|)
.

Furthermore, given a function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and access to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
of a symmetric skew-supermodular function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow Z
where
m
and
p
satisfy conditions (a) and (b), there exists an algorithm that runs in time
\poly(|V|)
using
\poly(|V|)
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
to return a hypergraph satisfying the above five properties. The run-time includes the time to construct the hypergraphs that are used as inputs to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.
Moreover, for each query to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
O(|V|)
vertices and
O(|V|)
hyperedges.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
q,r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be two symmetric skew-supermodular functions
with
K_{q}=K_{r}
,
and
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the function defined as
p(X)\coloneqq\max\left\{q(X),r(X)\right\}
for every
X\subseteq V
.
Furthermore, let
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a non-negative function satisfying the following three conditions:

(a)

m(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,

(b)

m(u)\leq K_{p}
for every
u\in V
, and

Then, there exists a hypergraph
\left(H=\left(V,E\right),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}\right)
satisfying the following five properties:

(1)

d_{(H,w)}(X)\geq p(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
,

(2)

b_{(H,w)}(u)=m(u)
for every
u\in V
,

(3)

\sum_{e\in E}w(e)=K_{p}
,

(4)

|e|\in\{\lfloor m(V)/K_{p}\rfloor,\lceil m(V)/K_{p}\rceil\}
for every
e\in E
, and

(5)

|E|=O(|V|^{2})
.

Furthermore, given a function
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and access to
{q}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
and
{r}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
of symmetric skew-supermodular function
q,r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow Z
where
m,q,
and
r
satisfy conditions (a) and (b), there exists an algorithm that runs in time
\poly(|V|)
using
\poly(|V|)
queries to
{q}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
and
{r}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
to return a hypergraph satisfying the above five properties. The run-time includes the time to construct the hypergraphs that are used as inputs to
{q}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
and
{r}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.
Moreover, for each query to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
O(|V|)
vertices and
O(|V|^{2})
hyperedges.

For the setting of Corollary
6.1
, Szigeti
[
58
]
showed that conditions (a) and (b) are necessary to achieve properties (1) and (2), and the same conditions are sufficient to achieve properties (1)-(3). Thus, using Lemma
6.1
and Szigeti’s results, we have a reduction from
DS-Sym-Skew-SupMod-StrongCover-using-H
to
DS-Skew-SupMod-WeakCover-using-H
. Consequently, Corollary
6.1
implies the following result.

Corollary 6.4
.

There exists an algorithm to solve
DS-Sym-Skew-SupMod-StrongCover-using-H
that runs in time
O(|V|^{5})
using
O(|V|^{4})
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where
V
is the ground set of the input instance. Moreover, if the instance is feasible, then the algorithm returns a solution hypergraph that contains
O(|V|)
hyperedges.
For each query to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
O(|V|)
vertices and
O(|V|)
hyperedges.

For the setting of Corollary
6.2
, Bernáth and Király
[
8
]
showed that conditions (a) and (b) are necessary to achieve properties (1) and (2), and the same conditions are sufficient to achieve properties (1)-(4). Thus, using Lemma
6.1
and their results, we have a reduction from
DS-Sym-Skew-SupMod-StrongCover-using-near-uniform-H
to
DS-Skew-SupMod-WeakCover-using-near-uniform-H
. Consequently, Corollary
6.5
implies the following result.

Corollary 6.5
.

There exists an algorithm to solve
DS-Sym-Skew-SupMod-StrongCover-using-H
that runs in
\poly(|V|)
time using
\poly(|V|)
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where
V
is the ground set of the input instance. Moreover, if the instance is feasible, then the algorithm returns a solution hypergraph that is near-uniform and contains
O(|V|)
hyperedges.
For each query to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
O(|V|)
vertices and
O(|V|)
hyperedges.

For the setting of Corollary
6.3
, Bernáth and Király
[
8
]
showed that conditions (a) and (b) are necessary to achieve properties (1) and (2), and the same conditions are sufficient to achieve properties (1)-(4). Thus, using Lemma
6.1
and their results, we have a reduction
from
DS-Simul-Sym-Skew-Skew-SupMod-StrongCover-using-H
to
DS-Simul-Skew-SupMod-WeakCover-using-H
(and also
from
DS-Simul-Sym-Skew-SupMod-StrongCover-using-near-uniform-H
to
DS-Simul-Skew-SupMod-WeakCover-using-near-uniform-H
). Consequently, Corollary
6.6
implies the following result.

Corollary 6.6
.

There exists an algorithm to solve
DS-Sym-Skew-SupMod-StrongCover-using-H
that runs in
\poly(|V|)
time using
\poly(|V|)
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where
V
is the ground set of the input instance. Moreover, if the instance is feasible, then the algorithm returns a solution hypergraph that is near-uniform and contains
O(|V|^{2})
hyperedges, where
V
is the ground set of the input instance.
For each query to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
O(|V|)
vertices and
O(|V|^{2})
hyperedges.

The run-times in Corollaries
6.1
,
6.2
, and
6.3
include the time to construct the hypergraphs that are used as inputs to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
,
{q}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, and
{r}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
.

6.2
Hypergraph local connectivity augmentation using hyperedges

In this section, we prove Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
, and
1.3
. We will need the following lemma from
[
7
]
showing that a certain set function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is symmetric skew-supermodular and admits efficient algorithms to implement
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
. We restate and prove
Theorem

1.1
below the lemma, and remark how to modify the proof to obtain the proofs of Theorems
1.2
, and
1.3
.

Lemma 6.2

(Lemma 3.1 of
[
7
]
)
.

Let
(G=(V,E),c\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
be a hypergraph. Let
r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\binom{V}{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a function on pairs of elements of
V
and
R,p_{(G,c,r)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be functions defined by
R(X)\coloneqq\max\{r(\{u,v\})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in X,v\in V\setminus X\}
for every
X\subseteq V
,
R(\emptyset)\coloneqq 0
,
R(V)\coloneqq 0
, and
p_{(G,c,r)}(X)\coloneqq R(X)-d_{(G,c)}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
.

1.

The function
p_{(G,c,r)}
is symmetric skew-supermodular.

2.

Given hypergraph
(G,c)
, function
r
, hypergraph
(G_{0}=(V,E_{0}),c_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
, disjoint sets
S_{0},T_{0}\subseteq V
, and
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
, the oracle
{p_{(G,c,r)}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}

((G_{0},c_{0}),S_{0},T_{0},y_{0})
can be computed in
O(|V|^{3}(|V|+|E_{0}|+|E|)(|E_{0}|+|E|))
time.

See
1.1

Proof.

Let the input instance be specified by
((G,c),r,m)
, where
(G=(V,E),c\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
is the input hypergraph,
r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\binom{V}{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is the input target connectivity function, and
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is the input degree-specification.
We consider the set function
R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
defined by
R(X)\coloneqq\max\{r(\{u,v\})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in X,v\in V^{\prime}\setminus X\}
for every non-empty proper subset
X\subseteq V
,
R(\emptyset)\coloneqq 0
, and
R(V)\coloneqq 0
.
Next, we consider the function
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
defined by
p(X)\coloneqq R(X)-d_{(G,c)}(X)
for every
X\subseteq V
. By
Lemma

6.2
, the function
p
is symmetric skew-supermodular. Moreover, for a given hypergraph
(G_{0}=(V,E_{0}),c_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
, and disjoint sets
S_{0},T_{0}\subseteq V
, the oracle
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}

((G_{0},c_{0}),S_{0},T_{0})
can be implemented to run in
O(n^{3}(n+m+|E_{0}|)(m+|E_{0}|))
time.

We observe that a hypergraph
(H=(V,E_{H}),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
is a feasible solution for the input instance
((G,c),r,m)
of
DS-Hypergraph-CA-using-H
if and only if the hypergraph
(H,w)
is a feasible solution for the instance
(p,m)
of
DS-Sym-Skew-SupMod-StrongCover-using-H
. By
Corollary

6.4
there exists an algorithm to solve
DS-Sym-Skew-SupMod-StrongCover-using-H
that runs in time
O(n^{5})
using
O(n^{4})
queries to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, where for each query to
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
, the hypergraph
(G_{0},c_{0})
used as input to the query has
O(n)
vertices and
O(n)
hyperedges. Thus, using our implementation of
{p}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}
from the first paragraph, this algorithm can be implemented to run in
O(n^{7}(n+m)^{2})
time.
∎

Theorem
1.2
follows using the same same reduction as in the proof of Theorem
1.1
and using Corollary
6.5
. Similarly, Theorem
1.3
follows using the same reduction as in the proof of Theorem
1.1
and using Corollary
6.6
: construct a pair of symmetric skew-supermodular functions
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
using functions
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
respectively similar to the proof of
Theorem

1.1
and apply
Corollary

6.6
.

We note that an optimization variant of hypergraph connectivity augmentation problem is closely related: Szigeti
[
58
]
proposed and studied the following optimization variant of
DS-Hypergraph-CA-using-H
:
the input is a hypergraph
(G=(V,E),c\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
and a target connectivity function
r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\binom{V}{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
The goal is to find a hypergraph
(H=(V,E_{H}),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})

with minimum
\sum_{e\in E_{H}}|e|w(e)
(i.e., minimum total weighted size),
such that
\lambda_{(G+H,c+w)}(u,v)\geq r(\{u,v\})
for every
u,v\in V
. Szigeti gave a pseudo-polynomial time algorithm for this optimization problem. Our results imply a strongly polynomial time algorithm for this optimization problem via a standard reduction (which is well-known in the graph connectivity augmentation literature); moreover our algorithm also returns a near-uniform hypergraph as an optimum solution. We refrain from stating the results for the optimization variant in the interests of brevity.

6.3
Hypergraph node-to-area connectivity augmentation using hyperedges

The graph node-to-area connectivity augmentation using edges problem was solved by Ishii and Hagiwara
[
38
]
. Bernáth and Király
[
8
]
proposed a hypergraph variant of this problem that we describe now.
We recall that for a hypergraph
(H=(V,E),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
, a subset
W\subseteq V
and a vertex
u\in V-W
, the min
(u,W)
-cut value is denoted by
\lambda_{(H,w)}(u,W)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\min\{d_{(H,w)}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}u\in X\subseteq V-W\}
.
In the degree-specified hypergraph node-to-area connectivity augmentation using hyperedges problem (denoted
DS-HyperGraph-Node-to-Area-CA-using-H
), the input is a hypergraph
(G=(V,E),c\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
, a collection
\mathcal{W}\subseteq 2^{V}
of subsets of
V
, a target connectivity function
r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and a degree-specification
m\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
The goal is to verify if there exists a hypergraph
(H=(V,E_{H}),w\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
such that

\lambda_{(G+H,c+w)}(u,W)\geq r(W)
for every
W\in\mathcal{W}
and
u\in V-W
and
b_{(H,w)}(u)=m(u)
for every
u\in V
, and if so, then find such a hypergraph.

Bernáth and Király
[
8
]
showed that feasible instances of
DS-HyperGraph-Node-to-Area-CA-using-H
admit a near-uniform hypergraph as a feasible solution and such a solution can be found in pseudo-polynomial time. Our results imply a strongly polynomial-time algorithm for this problem. The next lemma shows that the appropriate function maximization oracle can be implemented in strongly polynomial time. We defer the proof of the lemma to
Appendix

A
. We state and prove the main result of the section below the lemma.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
(G=(V,E),c\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
be a hypergraph and
r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be a function defined over a family
\mathcal{W}\subseteq 2^{V}
.
Let
p_{(G,c,r)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the function defined as follows: for every
X\subseteq V
,

p_{(G,c,r)}(X)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\begin{cases}\max\left\{r(W)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W\in\mathcal{W},W\cap X=\emptyset\text{ or }W\subseteq X\right\}-d_{(G,c)}(X)&\text{if }\emptyset\subsetneq X\subsetneq V,\\
0&\text{otherwise, i.e. }X\in\{\emptyset,V\}.\end{cases}

Then, we have that

1.

the function
p_{(G,c,r)}
is symmetric skew-supermodular, and

2.

given hypergraph
(G,c)
, the family
\mathcal{W}
, the function
r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, hypergraph
(G_{0}=(V,E_{0}),c_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+})
, disjoint sets
S_{0},T_{0}\subseteq V
, and vector
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
, the oracle
{p_{(G,c,r)}}\text{-}\mathtt{max}\text{-}\mathtt{sc}\text{-}\mathtt{Oracle}

((G_{0},c_{0}),S_{0},T_{0},y_{0})
can be computed in
\poly(|V|,|E|,|E_{0}|)
time.

Theorem 6.1
.

There exists a strongly polynomial time algorithm to solve
DS-HyperGraph-Node-to-Area-CA-using-H
. Moreover, if the instance is feasible, then the algorithm returns a solution hypergraph that is near-uniform and contains
O(n)
hyperedges, where
n
is the number of vertices in the input hypergraph.

Proof.
